# A2. Executive Summary (approximately 14 pages)

## Background

Between the years 2010 and 2015 Nicaragua lost approximately 150,000 ha of tropical forests every year equivalent to 14.45 M CO2eq at a rate of 3.2%. Deforestation occurs mainly in the Caribbean Region (CR) where it is estimated that 87% of deforestation occurred on private lands and 13% within forestland and the majority of the nation's indigenous populations. The CR contains the BOSAWAS National Park, which is one of the largest protected areas in Central America. These forests are important areas for the conservation of biodiversity and the livelihoods and cultures of indigenous and Afro-descendant peoples. Within the core zones of these protected areas the deforestation rate between 2010 and 2015 was estimated at 34% per year, which is more than double the national average.

## Drivers of Deforestation

The main drivers for deforestation and forest degradation in the CR are: The following demographic pressures caused by drought and food insecurity have led to an increase in migration from rural areas to urban centers. Combined with low land prices, undervaluation of standing forests, lack of access to technical and financial support for sustainable land use practices, and weak law enforcement, these factors have contributed to the aim as much as land as possible, often encroaching into indigenous territories. Between the years 1980 and 2015, 2.2 million ha of forests were lost and 14 million ha of new forest was established.

## The National REDD+ Strategy

To revert to a sustainable development path for Nicaragua, and to meet its share of the entire country's emissions reduction target, it is estimated that approximately US$305.7 million will be required to mobilize resources in order to address the task at hand of approximately US$115.7 million will have to be mobilized.

Nicaragua has decided to implement its National REDD+ Strategy through a programmatic approach (GCF-REDD+). The GCF-REDD+ strategy is based on the Nicaragua's National Forest Sector Plan and the GCF (32.81%) shall be invested through Bio-CIMA in 7 years to unlock Emission Reduction Results-Based Instruments (ERBI) under the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) of the Kyoto Protocol that has been approved by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF). RBIPs at US$ 24.3 million will be implemented within the CR and US$105.7 million will be invested in the rest of Nicaragua.

## Component 1: Conserving and producing for life

61.84% of Project Budget ($US49,486,228) will be invested to create an enabling environment that offers clear and simple norms, efficient local institutions and technical assistance needed to improve livelihoods through sustainable land use practices, including conservation zoning, business planning and market access for the individual family, producer group and indigenous communities. This will include the creation of a network of community-based forest and silvo-pasture productive landscape restoration and forest conservation models. These shall be implemented in 10 priority areas where deforestation pressures are high (Maina I - III). Ecosystem services to be protected or restored include: agroforestry, silvopastoral systems, small-scale agroforestry and silvo-pastoral systems; 40,215 ha of micro-irrigated catchments; and 541,826 ha of natural forests. These areas will be implemented in collaboration with indigenous communities, women and young people directly.

## Component 2: Investing in the enabling environment

10.29% of Bio-CIMA budget ($US119,905,361) will be invested to create an enabling environment that offers clear and simple norms, efficient local institutions and technical assistance needed to improve livelihoods through sustainable land use practices including equipment and capacities to support sustainable land use planning and environmental/forest law enforcement. This shall include the creation of a network of community-based forest and silvo-pastoral systems. These will be implemented in 10 priority areas where deforestation pressures are high (Maina I - III). Ecosystem services to be protected or restored include: agroforestry and silvo-pastoral systems; 40,215 ha of micro-irrigated catchments; and 541,826 ha of natural forests. These areas will be implemented in collaboration with indigenous communities, women and young people directly.

## Component 3: Investing to create an enabling environment

10.29% of Bio-CIMA budget ($US119,905,361) will be invested to create an enabling environment that offers clear and simple norms, efficient local institutions and technical assistance needed to improve livelihoods through sustainable land use practices including equipment and capacities to support sustainable land use planning and environmental/forest law enforcement. This shall include the creation of a network of community-based forest and silvo-pastoral systems. These will be implemented in 10 priority areas where deforestation pressures are high (Maina I - III). Ecosystem services to be protected or restored include: agroforestry and silvo-pastoral systems; 40,215 ha of micro-irrigated catchments; and 541,826 ha of natural forests. These areas will be implemented in collaboration with indigenous communities, women and young people directly.

## Conclusion

The GCF-REDD+ strategy is based on the Nicaragua's National Forest Sector Plan and the GCF (32.81%) shall be invested through Bio-CIMA in 7 years to unlock Emission Reduction Results-Based Instruments (ERBI) under the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) of the Kyoto Protocol that has been approved by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF). RBIPs at US$ 24.3 million will be implemented within the CR and US$105.7 million will be invested in the rest of Nicaragua.

Complementary, Bio-CIMA will facilitate the public-private dialogue processes involving all relevant actors to create the investment facilities (Trust Funds) to promote and bring to scale the sustainable

landscape restoration and forest conservation models, and to simplify norms and procedures to reduce transaction costs for natural resources sustainable management, use and market access. All these interventions will contribute to the creation of a regulatory and environment, paving the way to transform extensive and destructive land uses into sustainable land uses, including by supporting especially small-scale vulnerable farmers and households on deforestation fronts.

* **A Centralized Land Use Information System (LUS) that integrates land use information into an integrated and sustainable farm, landscape and ecosystem restoration approach.** 72.5% of the Project budget will be allocated to this component, which includes the development of a LUS.

Technical personnel from public institutions and extension services, farmers and beneficiaries will be trained in the use of the LUS, including through the implementation of training on participatory landscape planning (TLP), and the implementation and maintenance of productive landscape restoration and forest conservation projects. The LUS will also include tools for land use information management, market access, environmental legislation and norms, among others Environmental Information Systems (EIS) will be developed to support the LUS. These systems will be put in place which include a Deforestation and Forest Fire Early Warning System (SAT) and a Timber Tracking System (TTS). The land use changes monitoring system operated by MARINA within the REDD+ National Strategy and Action Plan (NSAP) will also be integrated into the LUS.

This is very ambitious plan to shift the prevailing development paradigm, which is based on extensive natural resources and landscape exploitation, towards climate smart, sustainable development can only be reached through a comprehensive approach that involves all stakeholders. It requires strong political decision makers at regional and local level and create environmental awareness through an intensive public education campaign.

Existing National Funds will be capitalized and strengthened, and new investments managed as Trust Funds by private banks with the objective of participating countries to generate additional income from their natural resources at scale.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Project Title (max. 150 words, approximately 2 pages)

The Caribbean Region (CR) constitutes more than half of the national territory and contains 73% of the country’s population. The CR is characterized by a high degree of urbanization (68%) and 45% of national population (12.7 inhabitants/km²) most (67%) in rural areas. The main economic activities in the CR are subsistence agriculture, livestock, coffee, cocoa, palm oil, bamboo, fishing (including shrimp and lobster), logging, tourism, and mining. The region is characterized by a high degree of deforestation, with 50% of the forested area being broadleaf forests closed. However, the deforestation rate in the CR has been very high: Between the years 1983 to 2015, the total forest cover decreased from 47.6% to 35%, equivalent to losses of 11.7 M ha CO₂/year – and additional anthropogenic forest degradation of 16%, equivalent to an additional loss of 1.9 M ha CO₂/year. The CR is characterized by a high degree of pasturism, crops or to secondary vegetation “tacolotes”, which increased in area 30% and 53% respectively.

The main underlying causes for deforestation and forest degradation in the CR have been thoroughly assessed through the Environmental Deforestation Risk Program (EDR) and the Environmentally Responsible Program (ERP) for the Caribbean Coast of Nicaragua. Demographic pressures caused by drought in the Pacific and Central Regions of Nicaragua and the expanding road system have generated strong migration pressure from these regions to the CR, where there is a high demand for land. The institutional capacities to enforce environmental, land planning and forestry laws are still weak. These factors combined with the lack of alternative livelihoods and the presence of large cattle ranches and other responsible markets have driven settlers to convert forests into extensive pastures with the aim to initially take as much land as possible, often encroaching into indigenous territories. Between the years 1983 and 2015, 24% of the total forest cover was converted to pastureland (see Table 1).

The area converted to permanent crops multiplied by ten in that period[1].

**References:**

[1] Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MAGA), Matriz, Nueva Segovia) and central (Matagalpa, Chontales) parts of Nicaragua. low land prices and a more constant (and relatively high) rainfall pattern are the main factors that have contributed to this expansion.

The predominant livestock production system is extensive, where animals are grazing freely on mostly traditional pastures. The lack of feed security in the dry season (due to the limited availability of nutrient available) lead quickly to pasture degradation, deforestation and loss of biodiversity and low reproductive rates. In addition, most cattle farmers do not have access to veterinary services; thus they mainly depend on pastures without much supplementary feeding during times of challenging weather conditions (e.g. drought or flooding).

The predominant livestock system is dual-purpose (milk and beef), characterized by low stocking rates (less than one animal per ha), poor productivity and reproduction parameters, also when compared with other countries in the region. This situation is mainly determined negatively by the lack of availability of good quality feed. Milk production ranges from 3 to 7 kg per animal per day (on average 4.5 kg). Most of the milk produced goes into cheese for the domestic market and export markets, where the price paid to farmers can be as high as $280/kg (for butter), but only very few farmers sell their animals at a younger age (14 months, 150 kg), to intermediaries or farmers.

Agriculture contributes with 17% in Nicaragua & GDP and represents more than 70% of the country’s total exports. The main agricultural products are coffee, cocoa, palm oil, bananas, shrimp, lobster, timber, livestock, and tobacco.

**12. Livestock production is heavily influenced by free trade agreements with Central American countries, Venezuela, and the US have stimulated livestock expansion. Between the years 2000 - 2009, the national livestock herd increased from 35 million head to over 48 million head. The value of livestock products increased by 175%. Presently, beef and dairy products are among the top four food exports in terms of value. In 2015, Nicaragua exported over 222,000 metric tons of livestock products, representing a 39% increase from 2014. Livestock production contributes more than 25% of the total value of exports. The indicators of the Business as Usual (BAU) scenarios presented in Section III suggest that livestock production will continue to be an important sector for Nicaragua even if without a further increase in livestock numbers (which is against the current trend) the expansion of pastures and deforestation will continue, with negative impacts on biodiversity, water resources, the expansion of pasturales into protected areas and indigenous territories, and a further deterioration of already degraded pastures causing further soil erosion, deterioration of ecosystems and ecosystem services.

13. While in year 2000 the Agriculture, Forestry and Land Use (AFOLU) sector accounted to nearly 50% of GHG emissions, it has been reduced to less than 40% by 2015. The largest share is still in 2015 most if it still being generated through loss of forest. Both CH₄ and N₂O emissions increased by 36% between years 2000 to 2010 to 6,492 and 2,252 GgCO₂eq., respectively, mainly from the enteric fermentation of ruminants and manure management. The largest share is still in 2015 most if it still being generated through loss of forest.

The CORA contains the BOSAWAS Biosphere Reserve™ in its northwest, while the Río San Juan Biosphere Reserve™ lies to the east. Both reserves are located within the Corridor de la Vida and contain the Indo Maiz Biological Reserve. These areas mostly covered with tropical rain forest are home to some of the most biodiverse ecosystems on Earth, including species that are found at higher numbers of tree, bird, and insect species than all of Europe. Within these protected areas forest cover diminished by 27.2% between years 2010 and 2015*, demanding urgent action and substantial investment to reverse this trend. The CORA is also home to the largest contiguous area of BOSAWAS Biosphere Reserves continues to be alarming, as shown by the forest cover change assessment for the period 2014-2016. This loss in forest cover not only decreases the regular supply of water for rural livelihoods and the permanence and biological connectivity of very important natural forest lands in indigenous territories in the Wasgam and Pinzópolke forest areas.

### CLIMATE RATIONALE

Due to Nicaragua's geographic position, the country is highly exposed to frequent climatic shocks produced by excessive precipitation (hurricanes and tropical depressions) and droughts of varying intensities. In 1980, Hurricane David caused US$25 million in damages (equivalent to 3% GDP). Since then, natural disasters occurred in 1982, 1986, 1998, 1999, 2001, and 2014. Primary hurricanes and tropical storms that impact mostly the OR of Nicaragua caused damage to infrastructure, displaced people from their homes, and destroyed crops. In 2010, Hurricane Earl caused US$3 billion in damages (equivalent to 5% GDP) of Nicaragua causing losses of US$1.3 billion, of which US$246 million was in the agriculture sector. Many of the affected areas were not able to recover from these disasters and are still recovering or have yet recovered¹. Most recently, in November 2016 Hurricane Otto hit the Rio San Juan Biosphere damaging and defoliating seventy-two percent of the area.

Nicaragua ranks as one of the most vulnerable countries and ranks sixth in the Climate Risk Index rank from 182 countries³⁴. Family farming is particularly vulnerable to climate risk: it encompasses most producers, especially smallholders who are more exposed than large-scale agriculture. It represents eighty percent of all producers. In land area, family farming comprises ninety percent of agricultural land. In

## agricultural production, their output value comprises 89 percent of the total. Their relevance for food security is unquestionable. Family farms contribute an estimated 60 percent of agricultural GDP from production of basic grains (maize, rice, beans, and sorghum) and livestock[1]. In 2001, in one of the most severe droughts on record, maize yields fell by more than 50% across Central America. The projected increase in Aridity is projected to rise between 10 and 25% in Central Nicaragua in the coming 20 years[2]. These climate change impacts will have a significant impact on food security and livelihood resilience, adding migration pressures to the CR impacting on indigenous territories, natural forests and ecosystems.

Although the CR has a tropical forest climate with average rainfall above 2000mm/year, climate anomalies are projected to increase in frequency and intensity. The number of days when maximum temperature exceeds 35°C is expected to increase by more than 10% for the period 2010-2039, and increase in number of days where maximum temperature will surpass 35 °C.

The rate of change in mean annual temperature projected for Central America (1.8°C) is greater than average temperatures changes in tropical areas[3]. These changes will affect the suitability of the main crops that support rural livelihoods in the CR, especially livestock and coffee, increasing especially the vulnerability of family farmers and smallholders.

The increase in mean annual temperature will increase the current suitable range (18–28 °C) for coffee production Bio-CLIMA will promote the introduction of alternative crops such as cocoa and bananas. The expansion of cocoa from coffee towards cocoa cultivation, as a promising alternative crop with higher heat tolerance within agroforestry systems[4].

### 25% increase in temperatures will impact on the wellbeing and productivity of livestock, negatively impacting family farmers that depend on them to sustain their livelihoods. Bio-CLIMA shall promote the introduction of drought tolerant varieties of forage crops such as Napier grass (Pennisetum purpureum) and water flows, improving the provision of ecosystem services to increase livelihood resilience of vulnerable family farmers and smallholders.

### Finally, an increase of 10% in dry days in the Caribbean region is predicted for the period 2010-2039, which will affect seasonal water availability for human populations and animals at micro-catchment scale. The restoration and conservation efforts at the landscape level will increase the resilience of ecosystem services to secure the wellbeing and the resilience of livelihood of the local population.

B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

### B.2.1. Bio-CLIMA: a programmatically embedded within the Theory of Change of the National REDD+ Strategy "ENDE REDD+", for which the causes of deforestation and forest degradation, and the major barriers for REDD+ were thoroughly assessed during the Readiness Phase[4]

It was determined that the structural underlying causes for deforestation and forest degradation in CR are the demographic pressures caused by droughts in the Pacific and Central regions, combined with a lack of access to markets. The main drivers leading to deforestation and forest degradation in CR where "ide" forest-covered land has been abundant, poverty, insufficient and weak institutional capacity, and limited technical and financial support for sustainable land use practices[5]. These demographic pressures, combined with low land prices, undervaluation of standing forests, lack of access to technical and financial support and lack of access to responsible markets, have driven settlers to convert forests into agricultural lands. The main structural drivers and underlying causes that act as vectors for the deforestation identified in the project area are extensive cattle farming and agriculture.

### B.2.3. The Theory of Change of the ENDE REDD+ includes 37 action lines (AL) for REDD+, which are grouped under the following six pillars:

- Strengthening awareness-raising, education, communication and the fostering of values and information sharing on forests and climate change in Europe, while promoting the sustainable use and co-vision of indigenous and Afro-descendant peoples (7 AL)
- Strengthening the national, regional and local coordination and building capacities of governmental institutions and civil society organizations to effectively implement REDD+ with the laws and policies on forests, environment, agriculture and energy (5 AL)
- Providing technical assistance for the development and implementation of landscapes and biological corridors through afforestation, reforestation and natural regeneration in the Caribbean Coast region as well as the Pacific, Central and Northern regions (8 AL)
- Increasing the capacity of producers and indigenous communities to increase their incomes of producers and number of jobs (7 AL)
- Encouraging the development of sustainable forestry and agricultural value chains with a focus on sustainable and low-emission markets, which value sustainability and reduced deforestation (6 AL)
- Strengthening its change adaptation initiatives in territories of indigenous and afro-descendant peoples of the Caribbean Coast, the Pacific, Central and Northern regions (4 AL)

### B.2.7. Bio-CLIMA's project activities are fully aligned and match the action lines that have been selected by the ENDE REDD+ strategy which are relevant for the Project Region. Bio-CLIMA in 42 action lines of the ENDE REDD+ Strategy:

- Assist producers and indigenous communities to formulate LUMPs, TDPs with business plans: Activities 1.1.1 – 1.1.3
- Facilitate forest / forest conservation agreements between settlers and Indigenous Communities: Activity 1.1.4

## Implementation

- **1. Infrastructure, T&D, Investment and Market Access: Renewable Energy and Interconnections**
  - **Objectives:** Coordinate with governments to enhance renewable energy and interconnection projects.
  - **Actions:** Implement policies, provide technical assistance, and facilitate market access.

- **2. Sustainable Land Management (SLM)**
  - **Objectives:** Promote SLM practices that improve soil health, increase agricultural productivity, and reduce greenhouse gas emissions.
  - **Actions:** Provide training, support research, and develop guidelines for sustainable land management.

- **3. Forests**
  - **Objectives:** Support forest conservation projects to enhance carbon sequestration and biodiversity protection.
  - **Actions:** Implement reforestation programs, protect existing forests, and promote sustainable forestry practices.

- **4. Water Resources Management (WRM)**
  - **Objectives:** Implement WRM strategies to ensure sustainable water use for agriculture, industry, and human consumption.
  - **Actions:** Develop water management plans, improve irrigation systems, and enhance water conservation technologies.

- **5. Urban Development and Climate Resilience**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance urban resilience against climate change impacts through improved infrastructure and planning.
  - **Actions:** Implement green building standards, promote public transportation, and develop disaster risk reduction strategies.

- **6. Adaptation and Mitigation**
  - **Objectives:** Strengthen adaptation measures to reduce vulnerability to climate change while promoting mitigation actions that align with national development plans.
  - **Actions:** Develop early warning systems, improve disaster preparedness, and implement carbon capture technologies.

B.3. Project description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

30. In the framework of National Program of Human Development, the National Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation Policy, and the National REDD+ Strategy*, Bio-CLIMA will contribute to the global objective of reducing emissions through avoided deforestation and forest degradation caused by livestock, forest degradation and livestock, as well as the enhancement of carbon stocks. Project actions will directly benefit 100,000 people in Nicaragua, who are vulnerable to climate change impacts, and whose livelihoods have been strongly affected by climate change.

31. Bio-CLIMA is embedded within the programmatic approach Nicaragua has chosen to implement the National REDD+ Strategy* “ENDRE-REDDE”**, which was formulated, broadly consulted and agreed upon in 2014 with the participation of government institutions, civil society organizations and relevant social actors***. The preparation of the ENDRE-REDDE Strategy and its key elements, systems and policies were supported by the Global Environment Facility (GEF) through a project implemented since 2011. This support which ends in June 2020, involved readiness organization and consultation, various assessments including drivers of deforestation and alternatives for REDD+, economic feasibility of strategies, institutional strengthening, development of the ENDRE-REDDE Strategy’s framework and monitoring, FERs, MRV capacity building, institution strengthening, land tenure and other services. The implementation of Bio-CLIMA builds on this important project and on all the relevant outputs of this ENDRE+ Readiness Phase.

32. To be able to achieve REDD+ Readiness to Strategy Implementation, Nicaragua has decided to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (EDF) in the period 2015–2020 in order to unlock Result Based Payments that have been agreed upon within the Nicaragua’s Emission Reductions Program (ERP).

33. Bio-CLIMA’s transformative vision to produce paradigmatic change shall be achieved through a three-pronged strategy: I) Strengthening institutional coordination and natural resource management by providing communities and farmers with capacities, technical assistance, incentives and market opportunities for sustainable land use, forest conservation and the preservation of biodiversity while conserving the natural resources on their farms and territories. II) Supporting the development of public policies and private investment through strong and efficient institutions, tailored financial instruments and investment facilities, improved and transparent territorial governance with clear norms simple procedures, and enhanced land-use and environmental education. III) Strengthening the capacities of technical personnel, farmers and community members in low-carbon practices and technologies to promote sustainable land use, forest conservation and landscape change with supporting tools and instruments for monitoring and control, public awareness and environmental education.

34. Component 1: Preserving and producing for life: Bio-CLIMA’s strongest investment will be put into the following trends where the risks of forest carbon and biodiversity loss are the highest. These are located on two core intervention areas: I) Indigenous territories within the Bosawás Reserve, II) The area between the municipalities of Matagalpa and Jinotega, which has the highest risk of deforestation in the areas of Wasopán and Piripocapa (see Maps 1–3).

Output 1.1 Land use management: Strengthening forest governance and land-use planning for landscape restoration, forest conservation and climate-resilient production.

Output 1.2 Land use management: Strengthening forest governance and land-use planning at individual farm and community level shall be supported through the provision of technical support as also facilitation and


---
**Page 20**
---

**Legal support to reach peaceful cohabitation and forest conservation agreements between indigenous communities and non-indigenous settlers**

*Activity 1.1.1.2 Assist small producers to formulate Land Use Management Plans (LUMP) with business plans (BPs)*

*Activity 1.1.1.2 Assist indigenous communities to formulate Territorial Development Plans (TDPs) including land use and management plans*

*Activity 1.1.1.3 Assist middle-sized producers to formulate Land Use Management Plans (LUMP) with business plans (BP)*

During the first two years, indigenous and non-indigenous family farmers, and indigenous communities will be supported by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) through technical assistance (TA) to undertake the land use and management planning of their farms, productive units and territories. The planning instruments that will be developed are Land Use Management Plans for individual families (LUMP), and Territorial Development Plans (TDP) for indigenous communities. These plans will be formulated in collaboration with the farmers and communities concerned, and will be accompanied by sustainable investment and business plans (+sb) with the objective that productive landscape restoration and forest conservation is financially viable and sustainable. Agreements will ensure equitable access to land and resources, including water, and will address gender issues, particularly access by women-headed households.

36. These two planning instruments will need to fulfill the following minimum requirements:

* Be based on a participatory process that ensures the inclusion of the knowledge, needs and perspectives of all, especially indigenous and non-indigenous male and female youth and adult women;
* Identify, map and demarcate the production zones (agroforestry, silvo-pastoral, crops, etc.) and establish rules for forest and watershed conservation, degraded landscape restoration and biological connectivity designations.
* Degraded land on soils with a slope steeper than 50% (26 degrees), which are prone to further degradation, will be excluded from agricultural use. These lands will be restored through the creation of the CHFFs (and remnants of natural forest) on the farm to be conserved.

37 Activity 1.1.1.4 Facilitate celebration and formalization of landscape restoration and forest conservation agreements. Non-indigenous families (so called "tercenas") that have settled within indigenous territories will sign a land use agreement with the indigenous community, which is known as a "Peaceful Co-Habitation Regime Agreement" with the GTI only if following conditions are met, which are in alignment with the Benefit Sharing Plan of the ERFO:

* The GTI and the non-indigenous family farmers must reach an agreement
* The family or community of settlers are small and medium size farmers that have used the land subject to the agreement.
* The family or community of settlers formally commits to comply with the land use plan and zoning of the territory, as well as to respect the conservation activities and landscape restoration activities.

The family or community of settlers will have to renounce any ownership claim to the land and explicitly recognize land ownership of the respective GTI. This clause will involve that any...

*Land tenure within the region project and the legal status of different actors within indigenous territories has been thoroughly assessed with the aim of ensuring that all parties involved in the process are aware of their rights and obligations. The assessment was carried out as part of the Proyecto de Redacción de Enmiendas a la Costa Caribe, Reservas Bosqueñas y otras Masas (MIRENA 2017). The actual situation of land tenure is complex and varies from one indigenous territory to another.*

*Please see Benefit Sharing Plan in Annex 24, Section 2.2.1 (page 33), page 5 of the ERFO Benefit Sharing Plan of the ERFO (version July 2017). The benefit sharing plan will be subject to consultation processes at regional and local level. Selection criteria will be further elaborated and refined through the Operational Manual.*

## Sub-component: 2 Investments in landscape restoration, forest conservation and climate-resilient production.

Only after the land use and management planning has been successfully concluded (LUUP), investments will be made to support sustainable land management and production. Productive investments in landscape restoration and forest conservation will be supported through three productive land restoration sub-projects: Sustainable Silvo-agriculture and Cocoa Agroforestry; Sustainable Forests; and sub-project for three degraded landscapes restoration models (Sustainable Community Enterprises, Community Forest Management and Community Forestry). The financial and environmental returns from these investments are to be adjusted to the beneficiary and community typology; and to the financial, social and environmental return of each project. These projects will be included in the Operational Manual and will be further refined in the Operational Manual.

As beneficiaries are mainly family farmers and indigenous communities living in poverty, support shall be given mainly through grants, and eventually also through loans with a very high repayment period (up to 30 years).

### Output 1.2.1 Degraded, pasture-and rangeland-nested. Landscape restoration through cocoa agroforestry and silvo-agriculture. The landscape restoration model will be financed by two specific Trust Funds:47. In the southern-western part of BOASWAS, in the buffer and connectivity zone between BOASWAS and the protected areas Cero Sasiayo, Cerro Coa Blanca and Cerro Bajo de la Sierra, cocoa agroforestry will be implemented as a landscape restoration model within the category as the zone of Inicio Maza as a Biological Reserve does not permit agricultural uses as agroforestry. The project is aligned with the National Cocoa Agroforestry Strategy and complementsarily with the Benefit Sharing Plan for RBPPs (Annex 24, Section 2.2), eligibility criteria for beneficiaries are:

- Sustainable land management and business plan (LUMP+sb or TDP+sb) in place
- Commitment to forest land restoration and no to further deforestation
- Legal title of the property with a clear tenure agreement with the GTI
- Small / medium landowner with less than 50 ha of agricultural land (not including forest land)
- Location within the buffer zone of BOASWAS
- High potential for productive landscape restoration and biological connectivity
- Others to be further determined and / or refined within the Operational Manual

### Activities: 1.2.1.1, 1.2.1.2 Small and medium producers restore degraded pastures into sustainable landscapes with positive systems (p.3). It includes rotational grazing with electric fences.

b) Introduction of improved deep-rooting forages for pastures and cut-and-carry grasses with live fences consisting of protein producing shrubs. These practices, together with improved livestock management, improve productivity and reduce greenhouse emissions (11 MtCO2e/R 20 years*), methane from enteric fermentation being reduced by 35% and nitrous oxide emissions by 40%. Soil structure will be improved through live tree fences and valuable tree species in pastures that shall provide shade and increase biodiversity. The introduction of deep-rooting forages, cut-and-carry grasses with live fences can increase feed productivity, biodiversity and add economic value. Soil structure will be improved and stabilized, and the use of herbicide residues improves from 2.1 to BAI) to 6.1 CO2e/ha/year, while livestock emissions can be tipped from 1.2 to 3.2 LTUna, while Bio-ClimA will support indigenous and non-indigenous small farmers and medium sized farmers (5-50 ha) in the introduction of sustainable pasture systems with improved forages and rotational pastoral systems. Smaller farms (<5 ha) will be supported to improve pastures up to 10 ha / farm, while medium-sized farms (5-50 ha) will be supported to increase their livestock numbers by 20%. In both cases the support will be aimed at improving stocking rates and productivity while restoring degraded land and expanding/conerving forest cover. The introduction of SSRS involve the combination of native trees and shrubs with improved forages, cut-and-carry grasses with live fences, soil and land restoration, increase resilience to extreme weather events (drought, excess rainfall), provide firewood and contribute to carbon sequestration. This will also allow farmers to diversify their income sources through the additional income (which can be substantial) through the sale of timber and fruits. Because of the importance of livestock production, massive adoption can have a significant impact. The introduction of SSRS implies that 50% of the emission reduction target is achieved by smallholder farmers (small farms (<5 ha) will be supported to improve pastures up to 10 ha / farm), which consists of: I.) Introduction of improved grasses that have higher nutritional value and are better adapted to drought and waterlogging, in combination with live tree fences and shrubs. II.) Introduction of leguminous trees and shrubs for recovery of degraded soils, reduced soil erosion, water and biodiversity conservation. III.) The introduction (small farms) of deep-rooting forages, cut-and-carry grasses with live fences to improve nutrient availability, especially during the drier months. IV.) Protein banks, to increase nutritional quality of the ration. Shrub legumes' deep roots reduce erosion and optimize recycling of nutrients. V.) Electric fences to facilitate pasture management and control livestock movement. VI.) The use of the biomass. V.) Live fences, to be planted around pastures, which also serve as protein banks.

Apart from improving livestock productivity, stabilizing the agricultural border and improving the resilience of rural livelihoods, these measures will also have a positive impact on greenhouse gas emissions. Although the figures in table 1 below show that GHG emissions of full SSPS can be reduced significantly if the SSPS is fully adopted at farm level, GHG calculations for the Bio-CLIMA project take a more conservative approach and assume that only 50% of farmers will adopt the technology. This would result in an emission reduction target of approximately 1 t CO₂eq/ha/y. The adoption of these models on 12,144 ha in the Project region shall therefore reduce GHG of at least 1.1 MCO₂eq in 20 years*. As requested by the GCF no GCF finance will be invested in livestock activities.

increases the resilience of ecosystem services, but also an important intergenerational asset of high value timber for the family that can be sustainably managed and selectively harvested in the future. Carbon sequestration from biomass (BAU) is estimated to increase by 25 to 35 GtCO₂ per year in CFI countries.

While the overall objective of the implementation of a CRF project at farm and community level, is to stabilize the agricultural frontier to reduce deforestation and improve local livelihoods, the specific objectives for the Forest Management component are:

1. To establish sustainable forest management systems that will be based on the principles of indigenous peoples' rights and customary land tenure.
2. To ensure that the benefits from the use of natural forests are shared equitably among all stakeholders.

### 47. Outline 1.2.2 Natural forest ecosystems and forest land areas restored and sustainably used

Indigenous and/or descendant communities located within the Indigenous Territories of the CRF of Rincón de la Vieja (RTV) will be supported to establish sustainable management systems for natural forests, including natural forest ecosystems and natural forest lands on an area that covers nearly a million hectares (Table 2 b). To this end, the project will support the communities in developing their own proposals (sub-projects) which will then undergo a competitive selection process. The communities will prepare funding proposals (sub-projects) to undergo a competitive selection processes where they will be evaluated against pre-defined criteria and indicators. The communities and their governing bodies (inter-communities committees). As the Project will support the communities to prepare these sub-project together with their own community members, it is expected that the level of ownership and counterpart requirement will have to be decided individually by sub-project in accordance with selection criteria and indicators.

Depending on their location respective to protected areas, the legal status and their protection category, these sub-projects can be categorized as Community Forest Management (CFM) or Community Forest Resource Management, and Community Forest Restoration sub-projects.

### 48. Support for Community Supported Enterprises (SCEs) in indigenous territories

This activity will be located within the core and buffer zones of BOSWAS and Indio Maíz protected areas where the communities have been identified as having a high potential to establish SCEs. According to the TPD indigenous communities will be assisted to prepare and submit sub-projects called "Sustainable Community Enterprises (SCEs)" to be co-financed by the project through grants or concessional loans, depending on their location within the core or buffer zones of protected areas and/or on investment. To be considered for support SCEs will have to include a business and investment plan ("One Plan"). The TPD indigenous communities will be supported in preparing this plan which is expected to promote the wellbeing and livelihood resilience of the communities through forest and biodiversity conservation activities, including ecotourism, handicrafts, production of non-timber forest products, resins and medicinal substances and/or other productive community enterprises. These sub-projects can be categorized as Community Forest Management (CFM) or Community Forest Resource Management, and Community Forest Restoration sub-projects.

On the basis of these assumptions, it is estimated that 960 hectares will be restored and managed under sustainable forest management systems in the RTV Indigenous Territories. The total cost for this activity is US$ 54,000 per sub-project, but the level of financial support shall be tailored to each project depending on its location within the core or buffer zones of protected areas.

On these bases and the assumption that each SCE supports the protection of at least 3,000 ha of natural forests, it is estimated that Bio-Clim will be able to achieve 95% SEES benefiting 9,487 people of the indigenous communities in the RTV Indigenous Territories (see Table 2 c).

The activities will be implemented in the CRF project area of Rincón de la Vieja National Park where it will be located in natural forest areas in high risk of deforestation located within 12 indigenous territories in the RTV Indigenous Territories. The communities that will participate in these activities are those that have been sustainably managed for commercial timber production and restoration/reforestation. Forest management to be started by the communities in these territories will also be supported to achieve group certification (see Activity 1.2.3) in order to comply with high environmental and social standards for sustainable management of natural forests.

### 40.
Similar to the SCEs, the Community Forest Management (CFM) and the Community Forest Restoration (CFR) sub-projects will have to be prepared and presented by the communities to the FONAFIFO for approval. The Fund will provide technical assistance in preparing these sub-projects. CFM sub-project may finance the design, approval and implementation of forest management plans, low-input agroforestry systems, reforestation and afforestation activities, as well as other related activities. CFR sub-project may include activities needed for the productive restoration of forest individual trees or small groups of trees, including planting, thinning, pruning, fertilization, and regeneration of regrowth of successful forested land and natural regeneration, enrichment plantings and other related activities. The Fund will provide technical assistance in preparing these sub-projects within the overall GAP. It is estimated that 282 sub-projects will be co-financed by Bio-Clima, and that these sub-projects will result in the restoration of approximately 105 ha of degraded forest land and nearly 800 ha/parcel, resulting in 78,185 ha of sustainably managed natural forest and 131,608 ha of reforested/open space. These activities will benefit 1311 vulnerable people and have a potential of offsetting 5.9 M CO2-eq. in 20 years.

### 41.
In alignment and complementarity with the Benefit Sharing Plan (see Annex 24) the Funds will publish guidelines for the preparation of sub-projects, which will be subject to competitive funding on a competitive basis. Selection criteria for these three modalities of sub-project proposals that will be considered by the Fund are as follows:

* Formulated by an indigenous or afro-descendant community located within the implementation region (as to maps 2 and 3);
* Provide opportunities for communities to sustainable livelihoods within the community;
* Include participatory mechanisms to ensure the knowledge, needs and individual and collective rights of women and men in the planning, design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation are taken into account;
* Contribute towards improving the quality and women's economic empowerment through both access to financing and other resources and generation of tangible and measurable benefits.

## Output 1.2.3
**Farmer cooperatives**, producer organizations and community enterprises (SCERs and CRMs) will be supported to improve their entrepreneurial capacities, facilitate business contacts, the participation in fairs and commercial exchanges. Bio-Clima will also support producers in obtaining organic and/or fair-trade certification labels to increase the value of their products and services and to enhance the marketing of their products. The latter will be reinforced by investment and capacity development to enforce forest law and forest management and similar international certification will be provided during the first years of the project.

### Activity 1.2.3.1
Farmer cooperatives, producer organizations and indigenous community enterprises (SCERs and CRMs) will be supported to reach high standards in their production processes and services. Producer organizations and indigenous communities will improve the quality, add value to and enhance the merchandising of their products and services in order to reach and sell to higher-value markets.

### Activity 1.2.3.2
Targeted business contacts between producer organizations and indigenous community enterprises (SCERs and CRMs) and private sector companies will be facilitated through participation of producer and indigenous community organizations in national and international fairs, business events and through the facilitation of commercial exchanges, in collaboration with the corporate private sector. The objective is to increase the value of their products and services and ensure that youth and adult women will equitably participate in and benefit from these opportunities, including by receiving fair remuneration.

### Activity 1.2.3.3
Producer organizations and community enterprises will be supported to obtain international certification labels for their products and services (e.g., organic, fair-trade). Forest management and similar international certification will be provided during the first years of the project. This will require equitable participation and decision-making processes; resources and benefits will be distributed equitably among men and women.

## Component 2: Good Governance
### Subcomponent 3.1 Regional natural resources governance strengthened.
More than 10.29% of Bio-Clima’s budget will be invested to create an enabling environment for forest management and conservation in the CRH that would offer clear and simple norms, harmonized with national legislation. The project will also support the strengthening of public environmental institutions in the CRH with new and additional technical personnel, equipment, vehicles, office space, and other resources necessary to improve their capacity for forest management and similar international certification will be provided during the first years of the project.

### Subcomponent 3.2 Local natural resources governance strengthened.
More public budget will be assigned to the local institutions in the CRH to support them in their role as territorial authorities responsible for improving the oversight and control of their territories, which covers an area of 17 million hectares. These state and institutional building activities will be financed mainly by the public debt from CAEBI, and not by the project.

### Subcomponent 3.3 National natural resources governance strengthened.
Support will be provided to analyze and update forestry, environmental and land-use normative framework at national level, regional and local level, as also to update the management plans

Through the credit component of Bio-CLIMA, the Government of Nicaragua will invest substantial financial resources to strengthen regional public institutions and the 23 Indigenous Territory Governments (ITGs) in the Río San Juan and the Alto Wangle y Boquerón regions. In addition to forest management, plans for climate change adaptation governance as well as environmental and forest law enforcement urgently need additional capacities and resources, which currently are scarcity present at these levels of government. To address this gap, a number of activities will support conservation and sustainable, climate adapted agricultural production will be provided with technical assistance and capacity building in the areas of land use planning, landscape restoration, and the improvement of forest governance on the total forest area impacted by Bio-CLIMA in the 23 Indigenous Territories. The project is expected to reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation in Component I is expected to reduce GHG emissions of 47.3 MCO2e in 20 years in a conservative scenario of reaching full scale implementation.

The landscape restoration component of the project, which is part of the climate intervention strategy is designed to go beyond a one-off project; its climate resilient production models will have been scaled up and integrated into the broader agricultural development strategies for the Region. The trust funds will be set up with Project Support through activity 2.1.3.2 provides sustainability scale, and an important investment in additional technical capacities and strengthened institutions will serve to ensure that these landscape restoration activities are sustainable over time.

The project interventions aim to contribute to the reduction of deforestation and forest degradation by converting degraded and destructive land-use forms into sustainable, climate resilient practices, targeting especially small vulnerable farmers and households on the deforestation frontier.

# B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagrams)

## OVERALL COORDINATION SCHEME OF THE NATIONAL REDD+ STRATEGY

**B6 As Bio-CLIMA and Nicaragua's Emissions Reduction Program (ERP) are fully complementary in their approach to the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy, both will operate under the same institutional coordination architecture, which is structured at three levels: national, regional, and territorial. The ERP will be implemented through a technical implementation level locally. The National Level provides for strategic guidance and inter-institutional coordination. The Regional Level coordinates with the National Level and supports the relevant sectoral institutions in charge of sectorial policy setting and programming, including INAFOR, MARENA, and MINAE.**

**To: The coordination with the Regional Governments (RG) and entities, and with the Indigenous Territorial Governments within the Steering Committee is facilitated by the Secretariat for the Development of the Caribbean Coast (SDEC). The National REDD+ Strategy ensures that national interests are duly balanced. Regional Governments support MARENA with coordination, monitoring and evaluation activities in their territories. The Indigenous Territorial Governments (GTI) support Strategy implementation within their territories at territory and community level.**

### TRUST FUNDS AND NATIONAL FUNDS
It is envisaged that three specific trust funds, the Silvopastoral Restoration Trust Fund (SPR-TF), the Community-based Rehabilitation Trust Fund (CAR-TF) and the REDD+ Result-Based Payments Trust (RBP-TF) will be set up with Program facilitation and technical support. These Trust Funds will be commercialized to procure goods and services and to make the payments needed to support the activities for landscape restoration, forest conservation and climate-related production included in Project Outputs

1\.2 and 1\.3.2, complementarily with the National Forest Fund (FONADEFO) and the National Fund of Environment (FAN).

76\. Each Trust Fund will be governed by an Inter-Institutional Committee (IIC) were relevant sectorial institutions and actors participate. In the case of the RBP-IF it has already been agreed upon that the IIC will consist of the following members: one representative from the Ministry of Environment, Natural Resources and Energy (MARN), the Secretary for the Development of the Caribbean Region (SDC), one representative from each of the three private sector entities involved in the project, one representative from the National Council of Private Sector Entities (CNPE), a representative of the Coordination of the GTZ of the Alto Mayo y Bocay (AVB), one representative of the National Association of Coffee Producers (ANAPROCAFE), one representative of the National Association of Private Producers, and one representative from the academia. Other relevant entities like the Protected Areas System (SPPA) and the National Commission for the Protection of the Environment (CNPE) may be included as relevant actors may be included, depending of the specific issues to be discussed and decided. The duration of this IIC will be 5 years, starting from the date of signature of the UNF Agreement with the World Bank/FCPF, which is 7 years, and the settler will be the MCHP for the period of time that relevant actors and entities are included in Annex IV.

77\. The Silvo-pastoral Restoration Trust (SPT-IF) and the Cocoa-Agriculture Restoration Trust Fund (CAR-TF) will have a duration of 10 years of project implementation with intensive technical, legal and facilitation (see output 2.1.3 Public-private dialogue and cooperation strengthened). The Project will enter into a private-public partnership with the objective of promoting sustainable development in livestock and cocoa sectors with the objective to create long term investment facilities as investment vehicles for the private sector. These Trust Funds will be governed by an Inter-Institutional Committee (IIC) that will make sure that legitimate and independent participation of relevant stakeholders is granted, and that internal cross-checking mechanisms guarantee transparency and accountability in the use of these resources. As also a General Management Manual shall be included in each of the Trust Fund contract and operationalized through its Operational Manuals as result of the dialogue, negotiation and consensus reached with the private sector.

78\. The financial, operational and administrative management of these three Trust Funds will be commissioned to one or various public financial institutions active in the Nicaraguan financial sector that will act as a trustee. These institutions shall have the capacity to manage the Trust Fund and shall complete a capacity assessment of the financial institutions taking into consideration a risk assessment based on their organizational structure, policies, procedures, and controls, including their ability to implement and comply with CABEI's policies.

79\. As to Nicaraguan Law No. 741 on Trust Fund Contracts) and as in most Latin American countries, Trust Funds (Fideicomisos) constitute an autonomous worth that can be used by the Trustee exclusively for the purposes of the Trust Fund. The Fideicomiso is a juridical entity with its own assets under the Settlor. A fideicomiso is not a just personal but a wealth or group of assets which is transferred to the trustee. The trustor's interest in the trust property is extinguished and the trustor's rights over his autonomous patrimony is separated from the Trustee's patrimony, and as such cannot be affected by claims against the Trustee. The Fideicomiso will be governed by an Inter-Institutional Committee (IIC), who will also oversee the activities carried out by the Trustee and approve annual reports on the use of funds and budgets. Given these attributes, fideicomisos are used in Latin American countries to effectuate and transparent use of resources and shield them off from undue political interferences and corruption.

Section 3\. Institutional Roles and Arrangements, Budget Sharing Plan (of page 24).

80. The specific trust fund contracts shall be signed between the selected trustee(s) and the MCHG. These trust fund contracts will specify the responsibilities that the trustee will have regarding the management of the funds and the reporting requirements for the delivery of project outputs.

The trust fund contracts will contain exact instructions for the trustee to carry out the specific trusts, procure goods and services needed in order to produce Project outputs in an efficient and transparent manner. The contract will also include provisions on how to ensure that the projects are georeferenced monitoring and reporting system to make sure any double-dipping is avoided. Initial capitalization of the Trust Funds shall be made by the MCHG, using funds from the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF) and CABI loan resources. As all these three Trust Funds will not be investment, but resources administration if such surplus occurs for any reason, these resources shall be spent only for the purposes defined in the trust fund contracts.

81. Preliminary efforts to identify a financial institution that will act as trustee for these Trust Funds shall be in accordance with the Benefit Sharing Plan of the EBF (attached as Annex 24) and include:

- Financial Institution duly established under Nicaraguan Law.
- Experience in the establishment and management of Trust Funds as Trustee
- No conflicts of interest with any of the project beneficiaries
- Willingness of build-up presence in the Project area to guarantee disbursements to local beneficiaries
- Offer of a variety of different instruments to make monetary disbursements
- Technical capacity to prepare and execute competitive bidding processes and sub-projects
- Ability to provide technical assistance for the implementation of projects
- Diversification of financial instruments for sustainable livestock and agroforestry sub-projects
- Access to credit lines from international financial institutions (IFIs) such as the Inter-American Development Bank (IADB), the World Bank, and the European Investment Bank (EIB)
- Capacity at AE will complete a capacity assessment of the financial institution chosen, taking into consideration the above criteria. The assessment shall include an evaluation of policies, procedures and controls, including their ability to implement and comply with CABI’s policies.

62. The financial resources to co-finance the sub-projects for Sustainable Community Enterprises, Community Forest Management and Restoration included in “Output 1.2.2 Natural forests ecosystem services” are provided by three funds: the National Fund for the Environment (FNE), which is the National Funds, which are the Forest Development Fund FONADEFO since 2003, and the National Fund of the Environment (FAN). These Funds will provide non-reimbursable incentives to beneficiaries in order to promote sustainable forest management. The FNE and FAN are managed by the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MARN) and the National Forest Institute (INFONAT), respectively. The governance mechanism of both funds allow for the participation of sectoral governmental institutions and non-governmental entities and actors, setting the basis for participation, transparency and accountability.

63. The FONADEFO is managed by the co-Directorate at the National Forestry Institute INFONAT and is governed by an inter-institutional Regulating Committee composed of MARN, MARENA and the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (INFONAT). B. Justification for Nicaragua GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

91. The International Monetary Fund has classified Nicaragua as a moderately risky country. In this very complex and challenging economic context, the Government of Nicaragua will be making an enormous effort to achieve its ambitious transformation agenda. This agenda can only be achieved with an equivalent big grant portion from the GCF and the GEF needed to create the social basis for the implementation of the climate change strategy. The objective is to enable deforestation reductions in the CR. GCF and GEF grant funding is needed, in addition to CAF financing, to support the creation of the enabling institutional environment, improved business climate to risk private finance, and the provision of technical assistance to the Government of Nicaragua for the implementation of agricultural and climatic technical advice, and suffer from poor basic infrastructure. Bio-CLIMA will support the creation of the enabling institutional environment, improved business climate to risk private finance, and the provision of technical assistance to the Government of Nicaragua for the implementation of agricultural and climatic technical advice, and suffer from poor basic infrastructure. Bio-CLIMA RBP continue supporting forest conservation and rural livelihoods in the medium and longer term after project.

92. Therefore, it cannot hire high indebtedness and must therefore prioritize concessional debt. Due to the very complex economic context, the following instruments have been designed - General Public Debt Act (Law 477), Debt Strategy and Annual Public Indebtedness Policy - to establish a framework for the management of public debt and to ensure that it is sustainable.

93. In addition to the already stringent financial panorama Nicaragua is facing, the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic are also being felt in the country. The government has taken measures to protect the health sector and to the reactivation of the economy. The CR of Nicaragua, especially the vast and remote areas that make up the border zone with Costa Rica, have been severely affected by the pandemic. Many of the institutions are only public regulators, technical assistance is provided in a very limited nature, but also financial service providers and financial markets are completely absent. Private financial institutions are not present either. The lack of private sector participation in the financial system, TA and market access, like the Trust Funds will be to create. Vulnerable groups such as women, youth, indigenous people, and rural communities have been severely affected by the pandemic for the transformation of farming techniques, restoring degraded landscape through climate smart and resilient agriculture, and improving the quality of life of vulnerable populations in the context of public services. Without the grant that is being requested by Nicaragua for this Project, the country could no invest in the creation of the enabling institutional environment, improved business climate to risk private finance, and the provision of technical assistance to the Government of Nicaragua for the implementation and revert the vicious cycle of poverty, natural resources depletion and deforestation in the vast territory of the Caribbean Region, which has and extension of the national territories of the Netherlands and Belgium.

94. Within such a challenging environment public investment (national and international) need first priority to be directed towards the creation of the enabling institutional environment, improved business climate to risk private finance, and the provision of technical assistance to the Government of Nicaragua regarding public infrastructure, accessibility and services that are completely absent, and that must also be taken into consideration.

### B.6 Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 94 The productive landscape restoration and forest conservation modules that will be introduced by the Project are expected to generate financial benefits that are much higher than the investment initially mobilized and have a financial IRR between 11.2 and 16.4%, while SCE, CFSR and CFM sub-projects are estimated to generate financial returns of 8% or more. The project will also provide significant co-benefits for the beneficiaries in the middle and long term. The linked to high value markets with project support, and additional income from other sources such as carbon credits, will be a key driver for scaling up the Project quickly so scaled up to cover the entire CR and produce forest conservation and livelihood improvement benefits.

#### 95 The Project will initially impact on 8,417 productive units and 603,035 ha of forests within the project implementation areas to be scaled-up to additional beneficiaries and communities in the 23 indigenous territories. The Project will also: i) Develop a landscape restoration model and a market model through which farmers share knowledge using the farmer field school methodologies; ii.) A REDD+ pilot initiative that is being developed by the Nicaraguan government, with support from the initial project put forward and executed by indigenous and Afro-descendant communities; and iii.) Incentives to sustainable land use practices in the CR. The Project will be implemented under the auspices of REEDF and other the Environmental Fund. While Nicaragua is expected to sign an Emission Reduction Agreement (ERA) with a major international buyer, it is also expected that the project will generate 1 Mt of CO2 equivalent until 2025; a follow-up agreement for emission reductions payments will follow, be this will be subject to the results of the first agreement. The Project will be implemented in such a way as to potentially market value reach far beyond the 7-year duration of the Bio-CLIMA project and its future RFPs will secure a forest conservation and sustainable development pathway in the medium and long term.

#### 96 Bio-CLIMA and the ERP are complementarily for the implementation the national REDD+ Strategy END. The Project will provide the financial resources, institutional capacity, technical assistance, capacities and provide the enabling environment for REDD+. the ERP will allow for REDD+ Result Based Payments (RBP) to be generated from the Bio-CLIMA project. This will result in a total RBP potential of more than US$ 473 million, which will be additional to those generated by the financial returns on the Project. The Project will also provide an enabling environment for REDD+ in Nicaragua in the most pessimistic scenario of achieving only 50% of the emission reduction targets set in the ERP; the financial returns from the Project will be sufficient to cover the costs of implementing farmers and producers that restore landscapes and/or conserve forests will reach a monetary value for more than US$7 million at year 8 when Bio-CLIMA is expected to end.

#### 97 Since the Silvopastoral and Agroforestry Trust Funds have been set up by Bio- CLIMA, a strong institutional framework has already been facilitated by the Project in order to induce them into the scheme to partner with the objective to promote sustainable, carbon neutral and fair-trade production of cocoa. The Cocoa Producers Association (CPA) is the umbrella organization for Cacao Oro, Altamigro and Atlanticgen (cocoa); as the Association of Catfe Farmers and Meat Exporters (FEDECANAFE), CATFE and MEATEX are the main actors in the coffee and beef sectors and larger production companies through financial support, technical assistance and market support.

#### 98 The substantive investment government of Nicaragua is doing in order to strengthen local institutions and capacities will be the basis for replication and scaling-up of best practices introduced by Bio-CLIMA. This will allow the CR to build together with its citizens logical and innovative framework for climate-smart sustainable land and forest use, governance conditions and a general enabling environment which will permit scaling-up to the whole CR.

# C2. Financing by component

Nicaragua has decided to focalize its Emission Reduction Program in the Caribbean Sea region, which has an area of more than 7 million hectares, a territory of the use of the Netherlands and Brazil together. This area is facing many challenges related to deforestation and improve ecosystem and livelihood resilience such as a vast, remote and poorly developed territory, a programmatic approach was chosen for this reason. The project will be implemented in three components that are interrelated and synergistic. Therefore, the financial structure by component and by activity shown in Tables 9 and 10 below show how different funding sources and instruments complement each other.

| Components | GCF Financing (USD) | Co-Financing by Funded Parties (USD) | CARBON CREDIT | CAF REDD | GEF | (%) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Component 1 | $94,352,086 | 20,198,029 | 77,086,920 | 8,385,596 | 23,453,905 | 6,047,644 |
| Component 2 | $11,905,361 | 113,379 | 69,603,447 | 10,198,962 | - | 371,027 |
| Component 3 | $3,836,330 | 547,855 | 1,000,960 | 1,000,960 | 337,202 | 194,400 |
| Project Management | - | $738,821 | - | 145,795 | - | 350,221 |
| Independent Evaluation | - | $210,000 | - | - | 105,000 | - |

**Total:** $116,982,245 | $26,139,067 | $77,054,362 | $18,099,530 | $24,316,473 | $3,826,213 | 100.00% |

**Total (%)**: 22.59% | 52.81% | 18.42% | 21.02% | 7.16%

The mutual complementarity of funding sources results in a blended grant and loan finance for most activities. This has been combined with assigning a bigger weight to a given funding source when it is more appropriate (e.g., the GCF funding for the design phase, planning and management planning for landscape restoration, forest conservation and climate resilient production (Subcomponent 2.1.3) and the CAF funding for the design of the project and the identification of preparatory activities for any investment). On the other hand, restoration of degraded pastures into silvo-pastoral systems (Activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2) has a much stronger financial component originated from credit as it is more appropriate to use this funding source in order to comply with the requirements of the CTNFP (Act. 1.2.1.4) and the conservation and restoration of natural forest ecosystems, and forest lands mainly for biodiversity conservation purposes (Activities 3.1.1.1 and 3.1.1.2) will be funded by GCF. It should also be noted that all investments and costs to strengthen the environmental authorities and the regional coordination mechanisms in the Caribbean Sea region (Subcomponent 4.1.1, 4.1.2 and related costs) will be covered by credit; while capacity development activities included in Output 3.1.1.1 and 3.1.1.2 shall be exclusively financed through grants (GCF and GEF).

103. While **Bio-CILIMA** impact is mainly on climate change mitigation, it will have significant adaptation co-benefits: Mitigation (impact Result 8.M.4) The Project will contribute to a shift towards more efficient use of land resources in the CR through better land management practices (investment, knowledge transfer, markets and governance) for climate smart production, landscape restoration and community-based sustainable forest management. It is estimated that **Bio-CILIMA** will have an impact on adaptation in the CR. It has been estimated that **Bio-CILIMA** will contribute to reduce 12 BI CO2 eq/ha in 7 years of project implementation, through better land use and sequestration in productive landscape restoration systems and through CTFNP, sustainable community enterprises and community based sustainable forest management.

management and forest restoration, including GHG emissions avoided through reduction of methane emission from improved livestock management. The total mitigation impact is estimated to be 47.3M tCO₂e in 20 years, assuming a conservative scenario of only 50% performance of the ERF.

104. Bio-CLIMA will contribute to increased climate resilient development through following results: Resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services in CR; improved land management practices for sustainable use of forests and ecosystems in the CR with an emphasis on the Bosawás and San Juan Biodiversity Reserves as also on endangered forests of indigenous communities, improved forest restoration and reforestation activities of community forestry systems and silvo-pastures, and improved land management tools and instruments will strengthen the adaptive capacity and reduce the exposure to climate change impacts for 104.235 direct beneficiaries (98.6% are indigenous and/or Afro-descendant living in marginalization and poverty), and 614,721 indirect beneficiaries (9.4% are indigenous and/or Afro-descendant living in marginalization and poverty).

105. Through Bio-CLIMA relevant institutional systems within MARENA, MEFFCA, INAFOR and the Regional Governments of the CR for land use and planning, environmental protection and forestry development will be strengthened. This will also contribute to the implementation of the National Climate Change Strategy by providing information in decision making through support to INETER (climate risk and weather alerts); INFONA (Deforestation Early Warning and Fire Detection System) and MINAFOR (SUCC-MRV). This information will feed into the LUMPs, TDPS and other climate change related plans.

106. Bio-CLIMA's innovative approach of integrating GEF-7 funds will also lead to impact on biodiversity conservation in CR. ## NDC and SDG Targets are Achieved and Sustainable

### BAU Scenario
- **Forest Ecosystems**: Forests continue to degrade, leading to reduced resilience from forest fragmentation and ecosystem services degradation.
- **Land Use Change**: Land use change is not present. The knowledge and capacity levels of the government remain unchanged in 2030, which promises sustainable land management (SLM) and land use plans but no environmental performance improvement or monitoring.

### Paradigm Shift
- **Forest Ecosystems**: Forests are restored and protected, leading to increased resilience from forest fragmentation and ecosystem services degradation.
- **Land Use Change**: Land use change is not present. The knowledge and capacity levels of the government improve in 2030, which promises sustainable land management (SLM) and land use plans with environmental performance improvement or monitoring.

### D.2. Paraginum potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 107. Scaling-up and replication: Once the productive landscape restoration models have been implemented successfully by farmers and communities and show positive financial returns and livelihood improvements, they will be replicated in other areas of Nicaragua, including those outside the LUMP and the TDPs. This effect should spill-over to neighbors, the community and the whole indigenous population who aim to change the development paradigm in the WHR. The innovative approach applied by the Program is that it will not only restore degraded lands but also create new territories in the Central American Region where deforestation in some protected areas is advancing.

#### 108. In addition, the project will be scaled up and replicated in other countries as far as they are financially viable and socially sound will trigger community driven development initiatives to further scale-up the model. The project will also contribute to the creation of a new paradigm where indigenous and auto-decedent peoples are owners and custodians to benefit their livelihoods and culture: the notion that forests and biodiversity are an enormous asset still needs to be put into concrete practice in many countries.

#### 109. Bio-CLIMA will be mobilizing important investment to create capacities and the enabling environment for landscape restoration, including the creation of a REDD+ (Reduced Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation) project under the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM). Receiving a payment for forest and eco-system conservation will produce a change in the perception of the value of standing conserved forests and ecosystems for their owners and triggers the engagement of communities, indigenous peoples and other stakeholders to participate in long-term virtuous transformation processes.

#### 110. Capacity building: A significant amount has been done in training and capacity building to strengthen the knowledge base at all the level; strengthening institutions and human capital basis.

#### 111. The project will also contribute to reducing the vulnerability of communities, households and small farmers to risks in input climate smart agriculture and sustainable cattle ranching and forest conservation will be reduced, and market access and development improved. The Trust and REDD+ RBPs provide for long-term financial sustainability and a mechanism for the continuous improvement of landscape restoration and the scope to the project.

#### 112. Bio-CLIMA is committed to an shift to the prevailing development paradigm, which is based on extensive natural resources and landscape exploitation, towards climate smart, sustainable development can only be achieved if very deep cultural and behavioral transformation of attitudes and values within the society at large. This will require a long-term process that involves raising awareness at national level, to private operators, farmers and communities and to the general public through communication campaigns, workshops, seminars, conferences, exhibitions and fairs and a specifically designed and targeted education program for schools and universities.

### D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 114. Environmental co-benefit: Bio-CLIMA will contribute to the protection of the most threatened and biodiversity rich areas in Nicaragua. The San Juan Bosque Biosphere Reserve has an area of approximately 2 million ha., 15% of the national total land area and as such the second largest rainforest in the Americas after the Amazon. The Sierra de la Muerte Biosphere Reserve, which is part of the San Juan Bosque is the second largest area of toward-rain forest reserve in Nicaragua and contains a higher number of tree, bird, and insect species than all of Europe. Taken together, these areas are home to some 35 different ecosystems, thirteen of the nation's 21 most important watersheds, and close to one
