# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 63 OF 93

**D**

million people[20]. The livelihoods of these residents, mostly indigenous and afro descendant dependent upon these forest and ecosystems, and they are highly vulnerable to impacts driven by climate change[115].

The international literature has widely documented the multiple ecosystem services provided by agroforestry systems (AFS)[23], including their ability to improve soil quality in degraded soils[68]. Besides increasing resilience to climate change[90], AFS can buffer the effects of extreme climate events such as droughts and floods, and provide a range of other benefits for livelihoods[145].

Moreover, AFS are known to improve the microclimate[74]. They are also effective at controlling erosion and land degradation by increasing organic matter and cycling nutrients. Agroforesters have also been shown to regulate the quantity and quality of water runoff and provide riparian buffers.

In addition, natural forest plantations that are exposed to rising risks associated with climate change (including drought, forest fires, pests), Close to Nature Forest Plantations (CNFPs) that are diverse in species and structure, can be established on degraded lands under poor environmental conditions[14] and are particularly suitable for the productive rehabilitation and restoration of degraded landscapes[15]. If managed properly, mixed planted forests from guided and enriched natural regeneration can offer a range of ecosystem services including timber production, non-timber forest products, and other ecosystem services such as biomass production and the delivery of ecosystem services and biodiversity improves[7].

The agroforestry systems that are being promoted by the CGIAR Research Program on Forests, Trees & Agroforestry (FTA) provide promising options for poverty reduction and for sustaining livelihoods[16]. These enable diversified production systems because of various intercrops, and reduce risks associated with pests and diseases, while also providing a range of ecosystem services.

Bio-Clima will specifically target families that have been pushed to migrate to the most remote and marginal areas in search of food security. The project will provide these vulnerable households with tools to stabilize their livelihoods at the agricultural frontier procuring higher income from deforestation free, sustainable agroforestry systems[17]. By providing technical assistance through the CGIAR Trust Fund mechanisms, organizational and market access support Bio-Clima will support these vulnerable households to have more equitable and fair benefits from their produce. The Project will also assist in promoting climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies that are tailored for each household resulting from increases in crop diversity aimed at reducing exposure to the risks of CC-related crop failures, disease outbreaks, and pest infestations[18]. This will be achieved through a participatory approach.

Improved livelihood resilience and sustainable agricultural family farming will be vital for the population of the CC to recuperate from the effects of the COVID-19 crisis.

The CGIAR Research Program on Forests, Trees & Agroforestry (FTA) promotes that biomass production and delivery of ecosystem services improves with tree diversity and that forest plantations that are diverse in species, structure and function are more able to adapt to changing environmental conditions than monocultures.

## Projected climate scenarios predict a much higher frequency of forest fires and a growing incidence of pests to which monocultures are much more vulnerable compared to mixed, sustainable, multi-functional "Close-to-Nature Planted Forests" (CTNPF) that will be introduced through this project. Through Bio-CILMA, the project aims to increase the resilience of forests by increasing their capacity for carbon sequestration and organic matter content, as a result of climate-smart agriculture and landscape restoration, including increased soil fertility and water retention.

The project will strive to stabilize migration flows, not only at the agricultural frontier, but also migration from the countryside to the cities and beyond.

### 119. Forest-based development: All activities within Bio-CILMA, like for example the LUMPs at farm level or TDP at community level, as well as the formulation of the SCES or the CFFM plans will ensure that women are included in all aspects of forest-based development and that their role in women's land management and agriculture will be promoted and women's participation in agricultural decision-making processes will be increased. The promotion of sustainable use of non-timber forest products and conservation of traditional knowledge in the use of non-timber forest products will be especially fostered and taken into consideration when financing the competitive forest conservation initiatives by communities.

### 120. Women are the main transmitters of the culture they play a significant role in the dynamics related to land tenure, access and management. The project recognizes that women's participation in agriculture, conservation, restoration and climate adaptation and mitigation is essential for the sustainability of families and communities. They understand the reforestation and preservation of land have important cultural values and are key to maintaining their traditional way of life and ensuring a healthy environment to future generations. The traditional knowledge of the Caribbean region is guarded by the women who pass it on from generation to generation, through stories, songs, dances and connection to mother earth. Indigenous and Afro-descendant peoples' identity, spiritual, health practices have been passed down for centuries.

### 121. The project's approach to land in the Caribbean region will be based on intercultural gender analysis, so that it reflects individual and collective land rights and indigenous understandings of territory which encompass both natural resources and cultural heritage. These are considered by many as spiritual, health and other practices, in addition to sustainable use for subsistence and income-generation activities. The project will support the strengthening of women's organizations and their activism to defend their land rights.

The Caribbean coast of Nicaragua is a racially and ethnically diverse region that has faced some of the highest rate of violence against women and children in the country.

### D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 122. Vulnerability of the country: According to the 2016 Standard of Living Survey by the National Development Institute, Nicaragua's general poverty in Nicaragua dropped from 37.8 to 29.3 percent between 2014 and 2016; while its extreme poverty rate fell from 8.3 to 6.9 percent. Despite this progress, poverty remains high. Nicaragua is still one of Latin America's least developed countries facing ongoing challenges in terms of economic growth, social inclusion, and environmental sustainability.

The project will support the strengthening of women's organizations and their activism to defend their land rights.


---
**Page 69**
---

123. Vulnerable groups and gender aspects: The Caribbean Region, which has been singled out within Nicaragua’s National Human Development Plan as a high priority, since it contains 54% of the national territory most of Nicaragua’s forestland, the majority of the nation’s indigenous populations, a quarter of its population living in poverty, and is also one of the regions most affected by deforestation. While it is true that both poverty and extreme poverty have been halved since 2005, Nicaragua still has the highest levels of poverty in Central America. The municipalities of San Juan department have the lowest human development indices (0.50-0.55) of Nicaragua. Bio-CIMA will focus on these areas and target vulnerable groups such as indigenous people and those who are families that first plan to farm development programs through a LUMP and a business plan; none of these instruments will be done without full involvement of all family members, securing their consent for the project and ensuring that women and young people are fully considered. Women-headed households will be prioritized as beneficiaries.

124. Nicaragua is aware of its need to strengthen institutional presence in the CR, the Alto Wangi y Riobic to reduce deforestation and therefore plans to invest significant budgetary resources in government institutions such as the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MARN). Project are a priority to creating an enabling environment for sustainable development and investment.

D.S. Country ownership (max 360 words, approximately 1 page)

125. Nicaragua is committed to implementing the country’s international commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in areas of climate change mitigation and adaptation, but also seeks to accomplish the three main goals of the Convention: the protection and sustainable use of the environment; the conservation and sustainable development of biodiversity; and the fair and equitable sharing of the benefits arising from the use of genetic resources. Nicaragua is a signatory to the Kyoto Protocol and has ratified the Paris Agreement. Nicaragua’s National REDD+ Strategy and will directly implement following four strategic lines of action of the National Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation Policy No.: 1) Development of agriculture that is resilient to climate change, through the promotion of agroforestry systems; 2) The use of bio-digesters; 3) Use and conservation of ecosystem services to achieve low-carbon sustainable development that is adapted to climate change; 4) Strengthening the capacity for the management and monitoring of planted forests in forest land-use zones; and Nt.7) Promote knowledge, research, finance and information on climate change mitigation and adaptation, as the modernization and strengthening of alert and early warning systems.

127. In its NDCs3 Nicaragua proposes to implement following concrete measures in the AFOLU sector: i) Encourage the use of agroforestry systems; ii) Promote the adoption of practices that reduce the impacts of climate change; iii.) Reduce extensive cattle grazing and introduce silvo-pastoral systems, iv.) Establish protected areas for the conservation of native trees and shrubs, v.) Establishment of native tree nurseries, vi.) Extend the use of bio-digesters., v.) Restore and conserve ecosystems and ecosystem services, taking advantage of adaptation and mitigation synergies, with special emphasis on watershed management and reforestation in degraded lands; vii) Promote the conservation of planted forests. Through these measures, which will be implemented also by Bio-CIMA, Nicaragua will raise its carbon absorption capacity to 10% above baseline levels.

Likewise, Bio-CIMA will support following priority measures to improve the countries capacity to adapt to CC as listed in the NDCs: i.) Modernization of hydro-meteorological services to provide relevant climatic information and early warning systems; ii) Raise the efficiency for the protection

125 Within Nicaragua's REDD+ Strategy, Bio-CLIMA has been designed to be complementary and not competitive with other initiatives such as the National Forest Fund (FONAFIFO), the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility, Bio-CLIMA is being developed since April 2018 by the "Grupo Técnico Interinstitucional para el Desarrollo de la Economía Verde" (GITEV) of MINAE, INETER, IFAD, NIFOR and the technical team of the FAD. All the institutions involved in the project are supported by the fact that the Government of Nicaragua will co-finance Bio-CLIMA through loans with the CAFEI and the National Development Bank (BID), which will allocate the entire the GET-F 71 STARS allocation to co-finance Bio-CLIMA.

**126. The project is expected to result in entity to entity**

CAFEI is a multilateral bank for the development of Central America. CAFEI's mission is to promote economic integration and the balanced economic and social development of its founding member countries, which are Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, Panama, and the Dominican Republic. CAFEI supports public and private development projects that create jobs and contribute to improving the productive capacity of the region in order to reduce poverty and inequality and improve environmental indicators in the Region. During the last 60 years, CAFEI's support to the Region has resulted in credit approvals of more than US$38.56 billion and disbursements of more than US$39.32 billion. Derived from CAFEI's experience in financing projects that have a positive impact on poverty reduction along the Central American Integration Axis, through regional initiatives in specific sectors, financing and promoting the region as an investment destination, and by supporting the development of human resources and social capacities that lead to achieving the objective of improving the well-being and quality of life of the people of the Region. CAFEI's support is provided through three main modalities: (i) approval of projects and programs that favor social appropriation and that address the need to preserve the environment, (iv) Transversal Gender Equity Axis, through programs and projects that promote equal opportunities for women and men.

131. MARENA will be responsible for overall coordination and the together with MHCP, responsible for the execution of the Project, with implementation support of MECCA, INIA, INETER, IFAD, and the Regional and Territorial Governments of the Caribbean Coast, the Alto Wangki y Bocay. Lessons learned from previous projects in which MARENA has been involved include: (i) The institutions are experienced in executing rural development projects with financial support of different cooperation partners such as the World Bank, IDB, CAF, IFAD, NIFOR, and the FAD; (ii) The institutions have experience in implementing the sustainable livestock program BOVINOS (1.7 M € European Union) which is being implemented also in the Municipality of Nuevo Guera (RAACES); as also the program to support climate change adaptation and mitigation in the municipalities of the RAAC and the RAA, which are relevant to build upon. Both projects have been executed with a strong focus on indigenous communities and their specific implementation structures. In difference with both these projects that benefit members of agricultural cooperatives and indigenous communities, Bio-CLIMA will target both indigenous and non-indigenous vulnerable populations.

132. The MHCP has solid capacities and long experience in the execution of multi-sectoral projects and has a strong presence at the national level. The MHCP is responsible for the project implementation and mandate the MHCP negotiates and contracts loans with financial institutions, represents Nicaragua in the governing bodies of international cooperation funds such as the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and others and in the NDA and for the GCF. The bi- and multi-lateral project portfolio managed and executed directly by the MHCP ranges between US$10.0 million and 186.0 million and include the following examples among

other provisions: a Contingency loan for Natural Disasters (US$186 million), b) Budget support for the year 2019 (US$100 million), and c) Integration Program for the Border Zone, (US$7.6 million).

### 133. CABEI has performed a assessment for MARENA and MARINA financial management systems. The results of this assessment are included in the annexes. It has determined that both institutions have an organizational structure, specialized staff and the tools and mechanisms to carry out the functions assigned by law. Both institutions also have experience with projects similar to those proposed under the Bio-CIMA project.

### 135. MARENA on the other hand, according to its functions assigned by law, executes the climate change adaptation and mitigation projects in the country. The General Planning Directorate has a high quality staff in adaptation, mitigation, social and environmental safeguards and carbon accounting. Likewise, the General Planning Directorate has an external cooperation unit that, in coordination with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, is responsible for the management of the political and operational focal point to the GEF. Euroclima currently holds the pro tempore presidency of the Country’s National Committee for Climate Change (CNC), which meets every six months.

values are on the average ranges for investment projects in the region considering that the beneficiaries will be groups of small, medium and large producers. The project efficiency will be significantly improved by the social, environmental, institutional and economic externalities generated by the implementation of the emission reduction measures. These externalities will also contribute to the sustainability of the national actors involved in the project.

### 138.
The total cost of the project is estimated at US$4,094,029 million, equivalent to 55.40% of the total cost being financed by GCF-CLIMA, will be financed through GCF resources: these would be put together by a US$26,139,067 major grant (22.98%), and US$37,595,462 (32.81%) from the World Bank (IFC) and US$18,999,530 million (16.42%), a grant from the Global Environmental Facility (GEF-7) in the amount of US$8,816,530 million (7.62%). The remaining 12% will be provided by an additional loan from CABEI of US$29,316,473 million (25.01%) and US$236,5 million (see also Table B).

### 139.
The GCF grant, which accounts for only 22.59% of total project cost, would be invested mainly in increasing the productivity of smallholder farmers through the adoption of improved agricultural practices and restoring landscapes; as also in tools, instruments and activities to generate and monitor emission reductions.

### 140.
The incentives for landscape restoration through silvo-pastoral systems can be canalized through the Trust Fund, as also the budgetary support for capacity building, good governance and institutional strengthening. The total cost of these investments is estimated at US$263 million, which accounts for less than 0.825%. The Logical Framework and Financial and Economic Model included in Annex B shows that the project would have a positive return on investment.

### 141.
**Economic and financial viability:** Estimates of the project's economic and financial indicators show that the project has a positive return on investment, with an internal rate of return (IRR) for carbon sequestration and avoided emissions30 for improved forest governance, show that the investment would have positive returns. The incremental financial and economic MVP would be US$412,630,701 and US$589,701 million respectively. The IRR of the project is 19% and 30%. Benefit/Cost ratio of 1.8 and investment payback period of 12 years, respectively. The project would have a positive return on investment with an internal rate of return (IRR) of US$267,87 per hectare/year. ## E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 143. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation will be conducted in accordance with CABEI and GCF procedures by the project team and the CABEI Country Office (CABEI CO). The Logical Framework Matrix provides a clear description of the project’s outputs, indicators, assumptions, risks, and methods of verification. The M&E plan includes: an inception report; project implementation reviews; a mid-term review as part of the project’s annual report; and an end-of-project review.
- The project's M&E plan will be presented and finalized at the project's Inception Meeting following a collective fine-tuning of indicators, means of verification, and the full definition of project staff M&E responsibilities.

### 144. Project start MARENA and MiHCP will call for a Project Inception Workshop to be held within the first 2 months after project approval. The workshop will bring together key stakeholders from all relevant organization structure: the CABEI CO, and, where appropriate, public entities and technical advisors, as well as other interested parties. The workshop will serve as an opportunity to build ownership of the project results and to plan the first-year actual work and procurement plan.

### 145. Annual Project Report This key report will be prepared by the Project Executing Entities MARENA and MiHCP and will be consolidated by the Project Manager and approved by the Project Steering Committee to monitor progress made since project start and for the previous reporting period.
- This report will include a summary of project activities, including indicators; baseline data and end-of-project targets (cumulative); project outputs delivered during the reporting period; project inputs used in achieving these outputs; and other expenditure reports. It will include reporting on co-financing of activities from funding sources different to the GCF which shall be reported by MARENA and MiHCP to the AE CABEI, which will report them to the GCF.

## RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### F.1. Risk Assessment (See Annex 5)

#### 4.1. Project Risks (Annex 6, pages 7-10)

148. Given that the project has an explicit focus on forest conservation through best-practice land-use planning and management, the risk of adverse effects on conservations values is limited. However, because of the work with non-indigenous farmers, communities and indigenous peoples in areas of critical habitats and given the external pressures present in the project region, the overall project risk has been rated as "moderate" and measures have been designed to ensure risks are kept to a minimum.

149. The majority of the project builds on the lessons learned from experiences determined for the National REDD+ Strategy, the PDP and the UNFCCC Warsaw Framework guidance. Focus is given to active and effective participation by local stakeholders and indigenous communities through effective multi-level landscape governance will limit the potential for human rights abuses and negative impacts on marginalized groups. The project has a strong emphasis on building capacity in the areas of collaboration in the areas of conservation and sustainable development, and the project is firmly in line with commitments made under the National REDD+ Strategy. The project also builds on the PDP as it is a strong platform for cooperation and limited risk. Financial and operational risks have also been identified and specific mitigation measures incorporated into the design accordingly.

On the other hand, the Caribbean Region is one of the prioritized zones by the current Nicaraguan government considering its geographic, productive and development potential; as well as its existing environmental degradation and deforestation processes, which have led to a significant process of desertification and recurrent natural phenomena. This is why the need for financial resources in these territories has been one of the main actors called out by the Nicaraguan authorities to preserve conservation and promote cultural and natural heritage and existing livelihoods in these geographical zones.

155. Project works will be carried out with the participation of the indigenous communities, as well as the Afro-descendant communities in the capacity to formulate ambitious sub-projects to conserve, restore and manage forest landscapes in their territories within and outside both protected areas (BOSAS/RAIS and Indio Maíz - see maps 2 and 3), which include most forest covered areas in the Caribbean Region. The participation of these communities will be guaranteed by the fact that it will guarantee an effective and inclusive socialization and promotion process with the intention that the inhabitants of the territories involved in the project are the ones who will benefit from the activities included in it, as well as the productive areas and services that will be provided by it, with the intention of generating in the short and medium term a portfolio of initiatives to be financed by Bio-Circ.

156. These effects include the improvement of the road network, access to energy sources, water and sanitation services, as well as the promotion of agricultural, productive and rural development among others. These synergies will guarantee an economic reactivation sustained on the basis of equitable social, economic and environmental development.

### Description:
Bio-CILMA will provide strong technical, financial and market incentives to promote sustainable land use and improve the livelihood of communities on their land use and to optimize their land use and to improve their livelihood on the land that is already deforested and degraded. On the other hand, Bio-CILMA will also work with indigenous and afro-descendant territories to strengthen their capacity to plan for and control the land-use plans and territorial development plans to be formulated and agreed upon. On the other hand, REDD+ Result Based Payments will accrue and benefit those indigenous and afro-descendant communities that have been able to meet their obligations or whose right holders may not have the capacity to meet their obligations or because right holders might not have the capacity to claim them.

### Selected Risk Factor 2
Category: Governance

### Description:
Bio-CILMA will be implemented in indigenous and afro-descendant territories some of them with the presence of right holders who have been able to meet their obligations or whose right holders may not have the capacity to meet their obligations or because right holders might not have the capacity to claim them. Bio-CILMA will also work with indigenous and afro-descendant communities to strengthen their capacity to plan for a peaceful neighborhood for years and even decades, but there have also been events of violent environmental conflicts in some areas. On the other hand the project could lead to adverse impacts on enjoyment of the rights of the affected populations if right holders are not able to meet their obligations or because right holders do not have the capacity to meet their obligations or because right holders might not have the capacity to claim them.

### Mitigation Measures:
Nicaragua's National Constitution and law provide a legal framework to protect land tenure rights of indigenous and afro-descendant people. Nevertheless, some right holders may not have the full enjoyment of these rights due to lack of awareness about their rights or because they are not members of the Indigenous Territory Governments (GTMs) to exercise and defend their land rights and natural resources are not fully protected by law. The project will work with indigenous and afro-descendant communities "as partners" and to formalize them within a "win-win situation". Bio-CILMA will also invest in strengthening the capacities, technological and logistical means of local public institutions to enforce the law. On the other hand, Bio-CILMA will be in close coordination with the ongoing World Bank financed PNDEZ II project


---
**Page 87**
---

which is increasing important resources on land conflict resolution and titling, especially in the Alto Visiray y Bocay Region.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Other | Low |

The project will develop land-use and management plans (LUMPs) and territorial development plans/plans to optimize land uses, restore degraded pasturesland and reduce the pressure on forest areas and increase protection of forest fragments at the farm and production landscape level. Some of these land-use plans may include provisions for land redistribution, which could lead to social conflict and/or forced displacement; land-use restrictions may increase the possibility of economic displacement, especially for poorer and marginalized individuals, who are more likely to change current production practices.

(Migration Measures)

Bio-CLIMA includes specific actions to address this risk. First, land-use planning will only take place through participatory processes involving all stakeholders in the CR. Second, the productive restoration and forest conservation models offer higher income and market opportunities for small-scale farmers and indigenous communities. Third, Bio-CLIMA provides technical assistance in the short-term and strengthening supply chains of deforestation-free produce to increase incomes and reduce poverty among vulnerable groups. Fourth, Bio-CLIMA will provide financial support to communities and communities. In addition, REDD+ result-based payments will be channelled to communities and indigenous peoples, many of whom are amongst the poorest and most vulnerable.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium |

The project depends on actions of multiple institutions and stakeholders, ranging from different national line ministries, regional and local governments including indigenous territorial governments, agricultural producers, civil society organizations, and communities. The lack of institutional capacity within the CR and within the different levels may undermine implementation and pose an operational implementation.

Project Component 2 has been designed to strengthen the institutional setting in the CR and improve governance at all levels through a multi-stakeholder approach. This will be achieved by strengthening dialogue and decision-making mechanisms to engage key stakeholders at all levels under the auspices of SOCC and the Territorial and Indigenous Governments of the Caribbean Coast, the Alto Wangki and Banglo.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low |

Institutions in the CR of Nicaragua are relatively young and weak. The lack of institutional culture and stability could lead to underperformance in Project implementation and/or inadequate use of funds.

Bio-CLIMA’s Project Components 2 and 3 will invest huge resources and important efforts to strengthen the institutional setting at all levels, including the SOCC, the CR, and the TIGs. These investments are made in order to support the State building and strengthening measures that are being undertaken by the Government of Nicaragua. The management of these resources is a shared responsibility. The allocation of resources that will be invested through Bio-CLIMA shall be channelled through five different financial mechanisms: (i) the National Fund for Climate Change; (ii) the SOCC Trust Fund; (iii) the TIG Trust Fund, other (please refer to Trust Funds 3 and National Funds 2 governance and oversight in Section B.4). All of them are governed by inter-institutional instances with the participation of various sectors and actors to ensure transparency and accountability. The CR will be responsible for the implementation of the project through private-public dialogue to establish the CAR and SPIR Trust Funds through which major Project investments will be channelled to beneficiaries. These Trust Funds will result from a private-public dialogue that will be facilitated

### Uncertainties with regard to future REDD+ related payments are a potential financial risk for the full implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy (ENDE REDD+) while it may not directly affect Bio-CLIMA which will have secured this co-financing until year 2025 through the Emission Reduction Program Agreement (ERP) with the World Bank. The ENDE REDD+ process, however, poses a potential risk factor for the sustainability of Bio-CLIMAs and its impacts and actions.

The GCF, BICE and GEF investments aim to provide the means and the know-how to communities and private landowners to implement the National REDD+ Strategy (ENDE REDD+) in Nicaragua (under TDR and TDP) that will trigger changes in land use injections in order to secure emission reductions and the payment of carbon credits. The ENDE REDD+ process is currently being implemented by CABEI, with the World Bank FCPF failed to materialize for any reason. Nicaragua would offer other financial resources to support multilateral or private entity, including the REDD+ window of the GCF, in order to secure the sustainability of Bio-CLIMAs and its impacts and actions.

### G. GFf POLICIES AND STANDARDS

#### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max 750 words/maximum 3.5 pages)

The environmental and social risk assessment was necessarily assembled by CABEI and due to its complementarity with the ERP approved by the FCPF, also with the World Bank Group safeguards and operational policies. As to CABEI’s policy on environmental and social risks, it is stated that in case of projects implemented on indigenous territories, it must be rated Environmental and Social Risks Category A (high risk). Furthermore, the socio-political context in Nicaragua is sensitive because of the social protests unleashed by the government’s economic reforms in 2018. The implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy has caused a critical political moment in Nicaragua. There is also an accumulation of tensions stemming from conflicts over land and natural resources, which are exacerbated by the lack of access to information with competing interests and demands of different land holders and people without land title, and illegal logging activities. An Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) and related instruments according to CABEI’s policy were developed in order to address these risks. More details can be found on MARENA’s website since February 2020 at: http://www.marena.gob.ni/redderebos/projects-bio-clima.

#### G.2 Environmental

The Project is not likely to generate a wide range of significant adverse risks and impacts, nor are there any known or reasonably foreseeable significant adverse risks that could be either avoidable or reversible. Serious adverse effects are not expected. The effects of the Project on areas of high value or sensitivity are expected to be positive, given the environmental and conservation objectives of the Project, reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, effects on biodiversity and


---
**Page 89**
---

living natural resources. Furthermore, Bio-CLIMA will foster the adoption of sustainable and landscape restoration and forest conservation modules. Some examples of potential adverse risks and impacts on the environment are related to the implementation of infrastructure such as the opening of new paths for access to communities, which can lead to deforestation, degradation of the vegetation, the loss of biodiversity due to the invasion of non-native species and environmental degradation due to canopy openings and the increased risk of fires. Forest management and restoration activities may also have an impact on the environment through soil erosion, sedimentation, and hydrocarbon pollution by the use of machinery. Risks related to the cocoa and livestock production chain can also be identified as a potential adverse risk and impact on the environment.

Most of these risks and impacts are predictable and specific mitigation actions reduce their probability of occurrence or minimize their magnitude. The project will implement several measures in line with developed environmental safeguard instruments, such as the ESMP, the Integrated Pest Management Guidelines (IPMG), the Guidelines for a Biodiversity Action Plan (GBAP) and Guidelines for the Forest Management Plan (FMP). These measures are described in detail in the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA).

Social. The Bio-CLIMA project per se is not likely to cause significant negative impacts on human populations, as it will contribute to improving their livelihoods by providing them with access to land that has been systematically excluded groups through the improvement of sustainable livelihoods, the increase in income opportunities to access high value, fair trade markets. Bio-CLIMA will specifically target the implementation of activities and investments in areas where there is a high risk of social conflict.

Furthermore, Bio-CLIMA is also expected to enhance livelihoods through a more coordinated support from various stakeholders involved in the project area. The project will contribute to improving access to land for agroforestry, small and medium scale sustainable silvo-pastures, sustainable forest use and conservation activities. In addition, the project will contribute to reducing poverty by providing opportunities to produce such impacts will be screened out of the project activities. Furthermore, a strong focus will be put on the capacity building of the social and safeguard teams of the Project Management Unit of MARENA, which will also be responsible for monitoring and evaluating the implementation of the ESMP during the cycle of the Project.

ESIA Results. As for the social impacts associated to the ER Program and the Bio-CLIMA Project, the socio-political context of Nicaragua regarding land tenure is very sensitive, as a result of the accumulation of tensions stemming from conflicts between indigenous communities, non-indigenous farmers and large landowners. These conflicts are mainly related to the following factors: land grabbing, illegal and informal land leases, coupled with competing interests and demands of different land holders. The project area will be affected by these social conflicts, which may lead to restrictions imposed on indigenous territories and forms of forest administration; restriction to indigenous populations of traditional activities such as hunting, fishing and gathering; and the implementation of the Emissions Reductions. All these structural factors can contribute to create potential conflicts in the project area, which shall be addressed by various specific project activities including Activity 1.1.4 and several other activities included in Component 2 of the ER Program and in the ESMP for indigenous and indigenous communities included in Project components 2 and 3.

In order to ensure that these risks are effectively managed and/or mitigated, an Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) will be carried out by MARENA at the beginning of the project when the exact dates of implementation, investments and actions have been defined and agreed upon with the beneficiaries. The ESIA will also include a detailed analysis of the potential adverse risks and impacts and implement site-specific measures, as well as EISA and Indigenous Peoples Plan (IPP) in the GTI. ---
**Page 90**
---

164. MARENA has also developed a Process Framework (PF), which purpose is to enable the process to be followed by which potentially affected communities and affected population will participate in planning, designing and agreeing on the restrictions regarding to protected areas, as well as in proposing alternative activities that are compatible with the conservation of the area. The PF applies to indigenous and/or afro descendant peoples, and it is not expected that activities of the Bio-CLIMA project will have a negative impact on indigenous, afro descendant people or other minorities.

165. MARENA has also developed a Land Management Plan (LMP), which provides guidance for the potential positive and negative impacts on IPs are identified and recommendations on how to screen for them. The LMP is based on the information provided by the PF and the ESMF, as well as other relevant documents.

166. The Project focuses on productive landscape restoration and forest conservation activities, the implementation of which will have a positive impact on IPs. It is expected that most of the natural resources are likely to be found in forest or rural areas, and some of the objects identified may be pre-Hispanic structures, sacred sites, protected land, inter alia. Once finds procedures are considered through the PF, the LMP will provide guidance for how to screen for IPs.

167. The Bio-CLIMA project will be implemented primarily by government staff from the implementing public institutions and other relevant actors. However, it is expected that some of these activities may involve IPs themselves. Project activities may also include community based sub projects (SCES, CMFA, CFRHK), and projects where most activities are carried out by contractors and subcontractors from the Trust Fund in the form of a Public Private Partnership (PPP). The LMP will provide guidance on how to screen for IP and identified those risks involved with standard socials.

168. Consultation Process: From September 19 to 24, 2019, MARENA consulted the following ESMF stakeholders: indigenous peoples (Miskito, Sumo, Rama, Garifuna, Wounaan, Embera, and Bribri); consultation was carried out in the locations of San Andres at Alas Wangi; October, September 19 to 24, 2019. Bilwi: RACCS September 19 to 20 and in Buenavista RACCS September 23 to 24, 2019 with an approximate participation of 50% of the indigenous population (Miskito, Sumo, Rama) and 15% of the indigenous (Miskito, Mayangna, Ulwa, Rama) and Afro descendants (Creoles and Garifuna). The participants were represented by: Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MINAE), National Governments, Regional Autonomous Council, Commonal Governments (Bueledos Creole, Tabasqueno) Territorial governments (Malumbok, Wak, Lilamni, Tuukha, Tawla, Karaita, Wangi Twi Taba, Bribri, Rama, Sumo, Miskito, Mayangna, Ulwa, Garifuna, Creoles and Garifuna), the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MINAF), the National Council for Indigenous Peoples (COPINRA), Don Bosco, Wanapen, Pinzopaka, Blufields, Rams), University BICULI, URCACOGEN; associations: CERCA, CONAGUA, COGUA, COPINRA, CONAIE, MINAE, MINAF, MINEDUC, MINDEF, MININFRA, MINPANAMA, MINPROTECCION, MINTRABAJOS, MINTRANSPORTES, MINURBANA, MINURBANA (Guararicundo), the press and radio, and ministries and public institutions: [MARENA, INFARF, METICOA, MINAF, MINDEF, CEN DEFENSA, PROCONAGUA, etc.]. The legal and environmental instruments used for the consultation are all the above-mentioned and documentation of the consultation has been published by MARENA in the following link: http://www.marenagob.ni/desarrollocomponentes/dialogos-y-consultas-para-la-preparacion/

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 170.
The regional autonomy normative framework and laws, as well as laws, policies and specialized gender institutions are in place to provide a legal basis for the use of instruments to defend women's rights. Nevertheless, women in the CR have suffered various forms of gender discrimination.

#### 171.
In general, the RACCN and RACCS suffer from higher rates of poverty and lower rates of development than the national average. Women in the RACCN are more likely to be poor than those in the RACCS and 0.64% for the RACCS. Women in the RACCN and CR also have a lower life expectancy than the national averages (68.2 in the CR compared to national rate of 72 for women; 63.6 compared to 68.1 for men).

#### 172.
Rates of motherhood are higher for girls and teens in the RACCN and RACCS compared to national rates. The proportion of married women who have had at least one child is also higher than the national average. Women's access to education, health services, and planning rates are lower and women's unsatisfied need for contraception is higher in the RACCN and RACCS than national rates. Various forms of domestic and sexual violence are a tremendous problem for women living in the RACCN and RACCS. The CR has one of the highest rates of gender-based violence[1]. Furthermore, their access to health services and reduction mechanisms are more difficult. Nonetheless, women's participation is increasing through the various committees established in both the RACCS and RACCN involving women's organizations and state agencies.

#### 173.
The CR has a high global and regional gender indices show very different rankings for gender equality in Nicaragua. In 2018, Nicaragua ranked 95th out of 146 countries on the UNDP Global Gender Gap report, which emphasized women’s formal political representation, education and others. The CR ranked 73rd out of 146 countries on the same index. Nicaragua has a gender gap by more than 80%. At the same time, Nicaragua ranked 105 of 159 countries in the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) Gender Inequality Index (GII), which measures inequality across four dimensions: reproductive health and empowerment, economic participation and opportunity, education, and others factors[2]. Nicaragua also held position 15 of 29 Latin American and Caribbean countries for rate of death by current or former intimate partner[3].

#### 174.
The CR has experienced many severe negative effects related to environmental, socio-economic and cultural factors. Increased flooding and soil degradation have affected and continue to affect the livelihoods of women in the RACCN and RACCS. Women have had to go much farther from their homes to practice subsistence agriculture and hunting activities. This has also affected indigenous and Afro-descendant people’s spirituality practices and traditional medicine because they are dependent on natural resources for their survival[4]. The CR is a region where indigenous production practices are effective for conservation and climate change mitigation, and afro-descendants have been the main custodians of these practices.

#### 175.
Women's experiences with conservation, biodiversity and climate change and defending individual and collective rights are complex. On the one hand, they have suffered greatly because of


---
**Page 92**
---

increasing diversity of biodiversity since it has made their responsibilities and cultural practices more difficult. Women and children now must go farther and spend more time to fetch water. Women make a clear connection between their concerns and others' attempts to provide for their family and their broader community. They are also aware that efforts to secure access to land and other natural lands is about more than just making money. Rather, they are also concerned about the maintenance of cultural traditions. Women have also suffered different forms of gender and ethnic inequality, discrimination and violence in their communities. The women's groups have been working together with other community players in defending communal land rights and continuing and adapting ancestral conservation practices. 176. A gender transformative approach is needed to address these issues. This requires a multi-level approach. A gender transformative approach seeks to contribute to increasing women’s and girls’ empowerment, which is defined as the ability of individuals or groups to make choices about their lives and maintain and reproduce inequalities. There are three interwoven dimensions of change that will be applied in the various project components: (i) individual capacities (knowledge, attitudes and skills) that emphasize gender equality; (ii) structural changes at different levels (household, community, enterprise and etc.) focused on norms embedded at those levels; and (iii) social structures (laws, policies, institutions and practices) that are the enabling environment for change. 177. The budget for the GAP is USD$3,550,668 or 11.7% of the entire Bio-Clima budget. Hiring practices is one main line of action. 50% of all those hired as technical and professional staff, as well as contractors will be women. At least half of all consultants will be women. The project will hire descendants from the Caribbean Region (CR) who speak Spanish and at least one local language. Technical and professional staff will be trained in gender equality and gender mainstreaming once implemented; men and women and women will receive equal pay for work of equal value. All staff and consultants will be trained in the GAP and will have responsibilities to implement it corresponding to their roles. 178. Gender equity components and mini gender action plans are required for all plans in Output B. These will include specific actions that will ensure that women and girls, as well as households, territorial governments, enterprises, cooperatives and others will identify structural barriers and other forms of discrimination they face and will be empowered to address them and monitored. Women and youth may use them as instruments to promote greater equality and their own empowerment. 179. Women who head households will be prioritized for project benefits. The project will provide families with childcare and other measures to ensure women’s full participation and engagement in decision-making processes, including at times and locations that are safe and adapted to women and youth's daily activities by community. 180. Gender equity components and mini gender action plans will also be included as part of the project outputs. These involve mixed-gender and gender-separate meetings for reflection. Local Indigenous and Afro-descendant elders will be engaged to train both staff and beneficiaries in traditional conservation practices, including those related to land rights and use. Materials will be written in local languages. Training and other sessions, as well as resulting materials, will document both constraints to intercultural dialogue and the need to address them through specific actions on education and production practices as well as revisions to legal and normative frameworks and other outputs. 181. Violence against women and girls will be addressed in various ways through the project, including training for staff and beneficiaries; awareness raising activities; and a Gender-Based Violence Mechanism for violence against women. 100% of those hired or contracted by executing entities and implementing partners against whom a complaint is lodged and verified will be fired and not rehired.

**Page 93**

182. The public awareness strategy and environmental education sub-component will promote gender equality, women’s empowerment and non-violent masculinities. It will emphasize indigenous knowledge, needs and rights regarding conserving and restoring biodiversity and will promote intercultural dialogue.

183. The monitoring framework will be designed to collect quantitative and qualitative data to measure gender equity results and indicators. The main topics to be measured are: (i) women’s and men’s decision-making power in the household; (ii) women’s and men’s access to resources at municipal and regional governments; (iii) women’s and men’s more equitable participation in the care economy; (iv) women’s and men’s equal access to credit, land tenure, property rights, information and control of resources, including spending and distribution of income; (v) awareness of positive and negative impacts of gender equality on the environment; and (vi) strengthening of gender across technical components – capacity building, legal and normative framework, and mainstreaming. All person-related data will be cross-tabulated by gender, age, ethnicity and livelihood.

### G.3 Financial operations and procurement (max 10 words, except where noted)

184. The CABEI is a multilateral development bank with the mandate of Central America. CABEI’s mission is to promote the economic integration and the balanced economic and social development of its founding member states. It provides financial resources for public and private projects that generate jobs and contribute to improving its member countries’ human capital, infrastructure, competitiveness and environmental sustainability in their human development indicators. During the last 60 years, CABEI’s support to the Region has resulted in credit approvals of more than US$31.05 billion and disbursements of more than US$30.98 billion.

185. The Bank supports its member countries’ economic and social development through projects based on agricultural and rural development, promoting the Central American sector of micro, small and medium enterprises (MSMEs), infrastructure, energy, water supply and sanitation, and environmental sustainability. The Bank’s support to the sector has been provided through its own resources and with the resources of cooperative programs from bilateral and multilateral sources.

186. CABEI has a long-standing relationship with international funds and has a good track record in implementing programs that use international financial management practices. For Bio-CIMA, CABEI will provide technical and financial support to the project implementation activities financed through the GCF (under GEF and the REDD+ RBP from the FCPF). For the reception of GCF resources, CABEI will be responsible for the disbursement of funds. The final disbursement decisions will be made at the request of the MHCP and the Project Management Unit (PMU) attached to MARENA, and to the Silvo-pastoral and Cacao-Agroforestry Restoration Trust Funds, that will be managed by a financial institution in accordance with CABEI’s policies. The disbursement decisions will require technical and financial support from the CABEI Country Office in Nicaragua.

187. CABEI will provide technical and financial and technical and financial support from the CABEI Supervision Unit through its team at headquarters in Tegucigalpa, Honduras, and also from the CABEI Country Office in Nicaragua. Project monitoring and evaluation will be provided during the implementation of the project by the CABEI Supervision Unit.

188. For all disbursements, CABEI must receive from the borrower a disbursement request letter duly signed by the authorized representative of the borrower, accompanied by evidence of the transfer of funds. The request must have the supporting documentation and evidence of the destination of the funds in accordance with the requirements of the executed contract or agreement, as well as any other documentation required by CABEI.

189. All documents are added to CABEI’s systems. Once validated by the CABEI Project Supervision Unit, the disbursement request will be sent to the Legal Department for review and signature by the General Counsel or a duly authorized representative of the General Counsel. The lawyer will validate the legal conditions. Likewise, the Analyst of the Environmental and Social Sustainability Unit will validate that all environmental and social aspects have been considered as reflected in the Loan Contract and in the Environmental and Social Action Plan created for the project. Once all the technical areas of CABEI have been validated, the role goes to the Financial Operations Publico

Control of which is in charge of executing the transfer of resources through the Electronic Payment System. In the event of any contractual breach, the disbursement will not be made until the breach has been remedied.

190. The procurement of goods and services (including consultant services) for GCF-funded activities will be carried out following CABEI standard practices and procedures, which are aligned with GCF procurement guidelines.

191. The financial information will include at least the following documentation that CABEI shall provide to the GCF: i) Financial statements prepared in accordance with the Financial Activity Agreement (FAA); ii) Annual performance reports on the status of project implementation, including disbursements made during the relevant period, progress reports, or more if so requested by the GCF; iii) Any other information that CABEI deems necessary to demonstrate its independent auditing body. CABEI will observe the highest ethical standards during the acquisition and execution of BioCLIMA, in order to guarantee the quality of project implementation and the effective use of assigned international and national services.

Dear Mr. Gómez:

We refer to the project Bio-CLIMA: Integrated climate action to reduce deforestation and strengthen resilience in Bosawás and Rio San Juan Biospheres in Nicaragua as included in the funding proposal submitted by the Central American Regional Environment Program (CAREB) to the Green Climate Fund (GCF).

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Ministry of Finance and Public Credit, the National Designated Authority of Nicaragua.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/03, the contents of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating this no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Nicaragua has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Nicaragua's national priorities, strategies and plans;

(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal complies with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP146

## Proposal name:
Bio-CLIMA: Integrated climate action to reduce deforestation and strengthen resilience in BOSAWAS and Rio San Juan Biospheres

## Accredited entity:
Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

## Country/ies:
Nicaragua

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| The project targets the poorest and most vulnerable region in the country and addresses a critical need to strengthen capacity of indigenous peoples and communities, particularly women, by piloting innovative approaches in sustainable forest management frameworks. | The monitoring and operations mechanism of the trust funds established/supported through this project will need to be strengthened. |
| The proposal seeks to ensure the long-term transformation and sustainability in the entire Corridor of Life, including the Bosawas Biosphere Reserve and the Rio San Juan Biosphere Reserve through the establishment of new funds to sustain and boost investments. |  |

The proposal takes a multi-fund approach to coherence and complementarity with other funds (GEF-7) and the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF), which is new, and creates an opportunity to complement GEF-8 from 2023 onwards.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XXIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 106**
---

1. The BioCLIMA project aims to restore degraded forest landscapes in the most biodiverse-rich region of Nicaragua, the Caribbean region (CR), and to channel investments into sustainable land-use management and forest management. The CR is currently experiencing a loss of biodiversity and ecosystem services at an alarming rate due to unsustainable management of ecosystems, demographic pressure, and a lack of investments. The CR holds 90% of the highest biodiversity in the country, and it is part of the important central American biodiversity corridor.

The project is seeking USD 64 million in GCF financing for a total project cost of USD 127 million. The project will be implemented by the National Forestry Institute (INIFOR), with a grace period of 10 years and tenure of 40 years, as well as USD 26.1 million in grants, through the BioCLIMA Trust Fund, which is supported from the accelerated entity (AEI), CABEI, under the framework for the Reduction of Poverty and Environmental Sustainability (FPRES) and USD 8.29 million from the Global Environment Facility (GEF) through the System for Transboundary Integrated Management of Forests in Central America (SITIFOR) and the Forest Investment Program (FIP). In addition, USD 24.3 million is to be provided as co-financing by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF), including results-based payments (RBP).

The AEI has categorised the BioCLIMA as an environmental and social safeguards (ES&S) category of project noting that the project directly engages with indigenous peoples (IPs) and communities.

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

**Component 1: Conserving and producing for life (final cost: USD 94.4 million; GCF cost: USD 20.2 million in grants and USD 36.6 million in loans – for a total of USD 56.8 million)**

* **(a) Land-use management planning for landscape restoration, forest conservation and climate change adaptation (USD 19.4 million; GCF cost: USD 7.0 million).**
* **(b) Productive investments in productive landscape restoration and forest conservation through three productive landscape restoration models: sustainable silviculture, cocoa-agroforestry, and agroforestry (USD 25.8 million; GCF cost: USD 13.4 million).**

**Component 2: Good governance (final cost: USD 11.9 million; GCF cost: USD 0.1 million in grants and USD 0.7 million in loans – for a total of USD 0.8 million)**

Under this component, the project expects to contribute to creating an enabling environment for investment in natural resource management and conservation in the CR that will offer clear and simple norms, efficient local institutions, and transparent governance schemes. The project will also strengthen the institutional capacity of IPs and communities’ capacities to support sustainable land-use planning and environmental/forest law enforcement. The 21 indigenous territories involved in this component will be supported with USD 0.7 million to improve the oversight and control of their territories, covering an area of 1.7 million hectares (ha). Complementarily, BioCLIMA will facilitate the public-private dialogue processes involving


---
**Page 107**
---

all relevant actors to create the investment facilities (trust funds) to promote and bring to scale the sustainable landscape restoration and forest conservation models, and to simplify norms and procedures to reduce transaction costs for natural resources sustainable management, use and market access, as well as to improve land tenure security, land-use planning and governance environment, paving the way for transforming extensive and destructive land-use forms into more productive and efficient practices, targeting in particular small vulnerable farmers and households on deforestation-prone lands.

Component 3: Capacity development (total cost: USD 8.4 million; GCF cost: USD 5.4 million is granted by the CR)

The third component will help transform the sectoral land-use approach into an integrated and sustainable farm, landscape and ecosystem restoration approach. Investments will be made in capacity building for climate-smart agriculture and landscape restoration personnel from various institutions, extension services, farmer communities and other beneficiaries of the project. This includes training on integrated land-use management and planning, territorial development planning, and the implementation and maintenance of productive landscape restoration and forest conservation modules.

Capacity building activities include:

(a) Provision of training to technical workers, stakeholders and communities;
(b) Development of tools and instruments, including information systems; and
(c) Awareness activities for climate change adaptation, mitigation, landscape restoration and forest conservation.

Environmental information and management systems to generate data and intelligence to support law enforcement will be put in place, including a deforestation and forest-fire early warning system, and a timber tracking system.


### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The project will contribute to a reduction of 12.8 MtCO₂eq over 7 years of project implementation, through better land use and sequestration in productive landscapes. Over the project's lifetime (2015-2022), it is estimated that 349,600 ha of cocoa will be managed using a conservative scenario of only 50 per cent performance of the Emission Reduction Programme (ERP), leading to an avoided deforestation rate of 78.5 per cent. The estimated cost is USD 1.24/CO₂eq removed.

While the project is a mitigation project, Bio-CLIMA will also contribute to resilience of ecosystems and communities in the face of climate change by promoting landscape restoration through the CR. The introduction of climate-smart agriculture and landscape restoration practices for cocoa in agricultural landscapes will increase the adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers. These instruments will strengthen the adaptive capacity, and reduce the exposure to climate risks, of 51,100 people. Most of them are from indigenous and Afro-descendant communities, living in marginalized areas with high vulnerability to climate change impacts. The project will benefit 614,721 people.

The project has a high impact potential both from its mitigation and adaptation focus through the significant reduction of greenhouse gas emissions delivered mainly through restoration and conservation of degraded landscapes.


---
**Page 108**
---

14. The project provides an alternative to the current "business as usual" scenario. It promotes community ownership and involvement in seeking to restore and manage productive landscapes in a sustainable manner, and in securing the long-term prospects of the landscape.
15. The project will enable communities to diversify their livelihood strategies through job creation, further involvement and ownership of the sustainable management of landscapes, and through the innovative support and access to finance from the trust funds.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

16. The project seeks to promote a long-term, sustainable and very innovative approach in order to restore and manage productive landscapes which also function as key supporting landscapes for many poor and vulnerable communities. The establishment of the trust funds and the boost provided by access to finance will provide a unique opportunity to build sustainable enterprises in the CFS.
17. The trust funds will continue to exist after the project ends, and continue to support the flow of resources into the community. This model is very innovative and could easily be replicated in other regions in the country. The intention of the trust funds and their scope and scale are very innovative and will provide Nicaragua with an opportunity to test and adapt the model.
18. The local communities will be empowered and play a key role in the success of the project. Through this approach, they will have a say in decision-making on investments, subprojects and business plans and planning.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

19. The project activities with communities at the centre will have high co-benefits across environmental, social and economic issues. Through the improved management of natural resources, the project will help create an enabling environment at the local rural level, simulating a positive feedback loop that can be scaled up to the national level.
20. The project will enable communities to diversify their livelihood strategies through job creation, further involvement and ownership of the sustainable management of landscapes, and through the innovative support and access to finance from the trust funds.
21. The strong focus on restoring important biodiversity corridors will have significant positive impacts for both people and nature in the region. By empowering the poor, the communities, being the custodians of these important ecosystems, will be empowered to manage these important landscapes in a more diverse and sustainable manner.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipients Scale: High

22. The CFS is the most vulnerable and the poorest region in the country. The region has suffered from decades of deforestation and severe degradation over the years. The project will seek to invert this trend by empowering the communities to diversify livelihoods and boost investments into landscapes, create diversification of livelihoods and promote more sustainable approaches in the region.
23. The project will enable communities to boost investments at the very local level, ensuring a very powerful model to scale up to other parts of the country, with a strong focus on poor communities.


---
**Page 109**
---

3. **Country ownership** Scale: High

The project will contribute to implementation of the measures in the agriculture, forestry and other land use sector in the nationally determined contributions (NDCs), not limited to: (i) modernization of hydrometeorological services to produce relevant climate information and early warning alerts; (ii) raising efficiency for the protection of the biosphere reserves and the conservation of biodiversity in the CR; (iii) increasing resilience capacities in climate finance; (iv) capacity development, access to technologies and finance for the agriculture sector; (v) promoting sustainable land management practices and agroecosystems in a resilient way through a landscape approach. Moreover, the project will seek to accomplish the three main objectives of the CR.

Bio-CLIMA has been designed to seek complementarity with the country’s REDD-plus strategy and initiative, the ERP. The project is also aligned with the National Human Development Plan (NHDP).

The CR is a key biodiversity hotspot in the country and has suffered from high deforestation. The project seeks to boost investments and sustainable management of land-use practices in the CR, which will help the country fully adopt its climate change priorities to increase investment and support to the CR.

The project is fully aligned with national policies, strategies and priorities. It supports a model (landscape approach) that can be replicated elsewhere, and provides a good basis for long-term sustainability and scaling up to other regions in the country.


3.6 **Efficiency and effectiveness** Scale: Medium/High

The project is to be financed through a very sustainable combination of grants and loans with a very innovative blend of co-finance. It offers a unique opportunity to bring in private sector financing for climate change mitigation.

The use of GEF-7 and the FCPF is considered very innovative - providing an innovative use of significant co-finance in the country, and promoting a very sustainable path for the future. The project will also be able to leverage additional resources from other sources and attract a variety of finance from other sources in the future.

The economic and financial analysis for the 20-year period of the project’s lifetime, with a social rate of return of 18% and an internal rate of return on public services of carbon sequestration and avoided emissions for improved forest governance, show positive investment returns. The forecasted net present value (NPV) is USD 375 million at a discount rate of 6%, respectively, with a benefit-cost ratio of 1.8 and investment payment period of 12 years, respectively.

The project’s economic analysis shows that the average cost per hectare unit is expected to have an annual additional net income of USD 270.2 per benefited hectares.


### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Environmental and social (E&S) risk category: Among the various components of the project, CR has a high environmental and social risk category due to its complexity. Through the implementation of territorial development plans (TDPs) of indigenous communities, land-use management and business plans of individual families, and sustainable community projects.

This document discusses a project involving productive landscape restoration, climate resilient and sustainable livelihoods, forest management, reforestation projects, and biodiversity conservation activities within the Green Climate Fund (GCF) framework.

### Risks and Mitigation

The project includes various risks such as:
- Violation or non-respect of rights of IPs
- Lack of capacity and resources for IPs to enforce their territorial defense and law enforcement
- Inadequate management of forest-based resources and biodiversity

To mitigate these risks, the project has developed a risk management plan. The document highlights that the project area is characterized by indigenous peoples (IPs) such as the Betsimisaraka, Antandroy, Bara, Manantsoara, Mahafaly, and others.

### Project Implementation

The project will implement TFPs (Total Forest Protection) in collaboration with IPs. An IPPF (Indigenous Peoples' Participation Framework) has been prepared to guide the implementation of TFPs. The document emphasizes that IPs should be consulted before implementing activities that may adversely affect them or their communities, ensuring their support is obtained.

### References

The document cites several references including:
- GCF/27/05
- UNFCCC/CP.19/INF.83


---
**Page 111**
---

be excluded from the project. Given the high level of engagement with communities, the project may need to be attentive to any potential COVID-19 risk present at the time of implementation.

(b) Risk of aggravating sociopolitical conflict between campesino-colonos and the IP concerned. This risk may be the most significant for this project. As described in the ESFM, the sociopolitical situation in the project area is sensitive as a result of the accumulation of historical conflicts over land and forest resources, including conflicts between indigenous communities and agricultural frontier colonists. Land-grabbing and illegal logging are also major drivers of conflict in the region, with significant impact on the areas. These issues are often complicated by competing interests of different landowners, by people without legal titles, and by illegal logging activities. The project interventions may exacerbate these conflicts if they are not carefully designed to avoid such conflict. This risk is to be managed by (i) the application of screening criteria that exclude certain types of activities from the project; (ii) the inclusion of land titles that are under dispute, and activities that would be carried out in lands under disputed titles will be excluded from the project; (iii) the facilitation of dialogue between indigenous peoples and non-indigenous parties to achieve peaceful cohabitation through “landscape restoration and forest management” activities. The ESFM also includes a construction solution based on the free, prior and informed consent of the affected communities. In addition, the proposed subproject will also include an analysis of any situation of conflict or violence in the areas, with emphasis on the potential conflicts mentioned, and consider more specific mitigation measures.

(c) Risk of loss or restriction of access to land and forest resources in protected areas: The project has released a list of activities that may have an impact on indigenous displacements/dwellings. Any activity that could produce such impacts will be screened out of the project activities. In particular, activities that require the purchase of land and those that involve the construction of infrastructure will be excluded from the project. However, in certain cases, some project interventions, such as the implementation of agroforestry systems or the establishment of sustainable use of natural resources in protected areas, thereby affecting forest-based livelihoods. To address this risk, the ESFM includes a list of activities that may have an impact on indigenous displacements/dwellings. The PF sets out the principles to be applied and actions to be undertaken in the event of restrictions or loss of access to land and forest resources. These include the provision of options or alternative solutions to the people affected.

(d) Inadvertent impacts of the interventions on deforestation and biodiversity: Although the project has taken measures to avoid activities that may have an impact on these areas, some activities that may inadvertently affect the health of forest and biodiversity. For example, certain agricultural practices may lead to soil erosion and extend the deforestation fronts, and cause erosion and the removal of vegetation. Exotic fast-growing species of plants may turn out to be invasive species, resulting in the degradation of native plant communities. The ESFM also includes a list of activities that exclude land use measures in the risk and impacts matrix. Activities that involve conversion of forested areas into agricultural or livestock production systems or natural habitats including, among others, conversion to agriculture or tree plantations, and activities that may lead to deforestation will be excluded from the project unless commercialization will not be funded. The ESFM also includes guidelines for biodiversity management which require the preparation and implementation of a biodiversity management plan.
