### Summary of compliance with GCF ESS standards

The instruments submitted adequately address the key issues and risks relating to GCF ESS standards. Below are summaries of the status of compliance.

#### ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The ESFs have been prepared. The ESFs use the result of two strategic E&S assessment round tables conducted for Nicaragua's national REDD-plus strategy (ENDE-REDD). These ESFs include a description of the project’s potential environmental and social risks and impacts of site-specific activities or subprojects during project implementation.

#### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

The project may directly hire fishers/woodworkers and other workers for the construction work. The project will also use the services of contractors in the small construction works, while rural workers may be hired to carry out some of the site-specific activities. A guide for labour management (Guía para la Gestión de Masa de Obrero) has been prepared, based on the country’s labour laws and the World Bank's standards for labour and working conditions. The project will comply with all relevant national and safety standards; non-discrimination and equal opportunities among workers; worker protection against forced and child labour; and ensuring worker’s rights and access to a GRM.

#### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The project will have potential impacts on soil erosion, sedimentation, and water quality due to the proposed activities and from silv/agroforestry activities. These are expected to be minor, highly localized, and temporary in nature. The ESF provides generic measures that may be applied by the project to mitigate these impacts (e.g., use of mulches). However, the overall impact of the project on soil erosion and sedimentation is expected to be positive due to the proposed afforestation activities and the establishment of silv/ agroforestry plots. Another potential impact could be an increase in the use of pesticides as a result of project support for integrated pest management practices related activities under agroforestry. This potential issue will be addressed through the implementation of appropriate integrated pest management plan.

#### ESS 4: Community health and security

Community health and safety risks and impacts are deemed not significant for this project. There is only a very small possibility of increased exposure to, and hence, increased prevalence of vector-borne (e.g., malaria) diseases in communities living within or near forested areas due to workers’ activities during the implementation of the project’s subprojects.

#### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project will avoid impacts from involuntary and involuntary resettlement. Activities that would involve involuntary resettlement will be screened out from the project through the adoption of the exclusion criteria for access to natural resources in protected areas, a PF for involuntary restriction of access to natural resources in protected areas, and the development of land acquisition plans, through a participatory approach, for activities that would involve involuntary resettlement.

#### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

Inadvertent adverse impacts of project activities on biodiversity and the forest will be minimized by the adoption of the exclusion criteria during screening of


---
**Page 113**
---

individual subprojects. Activities that would clearly involve conversion, deforestation or any other alteration of natural forests or natural habitats, and activities related to illegal harvesting of timber and non-timber products for commercialization will not be considered. The GCF Gender Policy (GCP) will continue to guide the design of project interventions on the ground. Subprojects that would involve negative impacts on women will require a gender sensitivity management plan. There are also guidelines for the formulation of forest management plans.

### ESS 7: Indigenous peoples. The indigenous communities are main target beneficiaries of the project, and they have been involved in the identification of IPs, IPs’ needs, and by also facilitating and supporting peaceful co-habitation agreements among IP communities. In order to ensure that IPs are not adversely affected by project activities, individual subprojects will be screened for any activities deemed to adversely affect IPs and Afro-descendant people, and also activities on sites where communities have not been consulted or where there is no agreement with the IPs. Subprojects that benefit IPs, an IPPF has been prepared in consultation with IPs, and IPs are involved in project design consultations and consent.

### ESS 8: Cultural heritage. The overall potential risks to the impact of the project on cultural heritage are low. Activities that involve the removal or alteration of any physical cultural property (including sites of archaeological, palaeontological, historical, architectural, artistic, ethnographic, religious, or other significance) funded for cultural heritage has also been prepared.

### Stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanism. The preparation of the comprehensive ESF and the GCF-ESMIF implementation involved the holding of round tables. These comprised working sessions and workshops to draft the strategic options for each ESS. The ESF and its specific stand-alone elements underwent a consultation process with representatives from government, civil society sectors. The ESMF provides a description of the GRM for the project. To address stakeholder engagement during project implementation, a stand-alone stakeholder engagement plan has been prepared. A summary of the ESF, the ESF’s specific stand-alone elements and the GRM have also been made available at the AE level, at the national level and at the project level. The GCF Indigenous Peoples’ Policy (IPP) provides for the establishment of a grievance mechanism that is available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made, as required by paragraph 106 of this GCF Environmental Impact Policy.

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan (GAP) and, therefore, has completed the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The gender assessment provides an assessment of the country’s commitment to gender equality and women’s empowerment. It also provides an assessment and analysis of gender gaps, differences in access to resources in Nicaragua on the implementation of the project.

The Government of Nicaragua (GoN) is a signatory to a significant number of international conventions such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women, and the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women. The 1987 Constitution of Nicaragua recognizes women’s equality, and a number of the country’s laws and public policies already include a gender


---
**Page 114**
---

The gender assessment indicates that there are six main ethnicities in the CR, with some common challenges and gaps in decision-making, access to resources, and gender-based gaps. The gaps are evidenced through unequal land ownership, low participation in decision-making at all levels of government, limited access to productive agricultural resources, and limited access to health and social services. In general, men own more and larger sections of land than women. Moreover, women have far less access to credit, inputs, and extension services compared with men. Women are often not recognized as farmers in their communities, and their contributions at the household level vary greatly. Participation in economic activity varies widely according to location, ethnicity, age, and marital status. The share of women's participation in the economy is wider than the national average, especially in rural areas, where women's level of participation can be up to 50% compared with only about 20% for men.

In addition, women produce higher poverty rates, and farms owned by women producers receive less credit from banks. Women also have lower levels of education and literacy than men. The gaps are wider in the Caribbean macro-region by area and ethnicity, ranging from 21.6 per cent and 25.4 per cent for indigenous women compared with non-indigenous women in the Guiana and Caribbean Autonomous Region, respectively, to as high as 50 per cent among the indigenous Rama people of the Caribbean Coast.

At the community level, women's effective participation in forest- and land-use planning is limited by a lack of access to information on forest resources, traditional sociocultural norms and sanctions, pressure by husbands, time poverty, and household responsibilities. Women are also less likely than men to participate in decision-making processes at all levels.

Violence against women is a major problem affecting women in the Caribbean Coast. The Ministry of Justice, courts, and medical-legal institutions all have specific units specialized in family violence cases. However, there are still many obstacles for women, including lack of information, fear of retaliation from their husbands or partners, and adolescents face tremendous obstacles to accessing justice.

The CR has provided a GCF-funded project that includes activities to support gender-intersectoral dialogue and transformative change and the objectives of the Bio-CLIMA. The GAP provides activities to address the challenges faced by women, and includes indicators, targets, timelines, and monitoring and evaluation (M&E) mechanisms for each activity. The M&E will be used to collect baselines for the remaining indicators at the beginning of project implementation. The CR will also include a gender analysis in the baseline report and in the annual progress reports ensuring the inclusion of the gender expert in various decision-making and management structures. The GAP includes activities that address the specific challenges, needs, and needs of women, women-headed households, indigenous women, youth, and older people. It also includes measures to ensure that women can GRMs to address gender-based violence issues.

The GAP identifies project outputs and activities that relate to land use and management planning, including the development of a participatory process for the CR at all levels: the household, cooperative, producers organization and indigenous community level. It includes improved access to high-value markets for women, through women's groups in mixed-gender cooperatives and producer organizations. Moreover, it ensures equitable participation of women of different ethnicities involved in the project will include providing training on gender equality and women's empowerment in the CR, including childcare services where needed. The GAP also targets the legal and normative framework, including the CR Constitution and laws, to ensure that they are gender-sensitive. Activities with activities to update and ensure equitable participatory processes for adult and young women and men of different ethnicities. In addition, investment opportunities, covering the three


---
**Page 115**
---

trust funds to be set up by the project will prioritize proposals that ensure sustainable practices around land forestry and production that empower women economically and include women in decision-making roles. The project will proactively engage men and women from indigenous and non-indigenous groups in most of the proposals funded as well as in the governance and oversight mechanisms.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

* GCF is requested to provide a senior loan of USD 38 million and a grant of USD 26.1 million to transform extensive cattle farming, agriculture and timber exploitation into more intensive, deforestation-free production forms in the CEF in Nicaragua.
* The AE will provide a senior loan of USD 19 million from the GCF and an equity contribution of USD 30 million from the FCPF and USD 8.25 million from the GEF will be used as co-financing to the project.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

* The AE, CABEI, has an extensive track record in the region of over 5 decades and has disbursed USD 26.2 billion through its network of more than 100 intermediate financial institutions (IFIs) in the region.
* MARENA and the Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (MIPC) are the executing entities (EEs) for this project. MARENA will implement all grant-related activities, while MIPC will implement all loan-related activities. Both institutions have a proven track record in financial capacity assessment of the EEs. It has concluded that both institutions have the capacity to manage the funds and to ensure the effective implementation of the projects on rural development and climate change projects financed by different cooperation partners.
* MIPC has experience in the execution of multilateral projects, and it has a reliable financial management system with a proven track record for project implementation.

#### 4.3.3 Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

* Management of trust funds: The project will set up three specific trust funds under Outputs 1.2 and 2.1.1 managed by MIPC. A total of USD 95.2 million, 82 per cent of total financing, is to be disbursed through these funds.
* The role of trustee of these three funds is critical for the success of the project's implementation. The EE will select a financial institution that has experience in managing trust funds and can provide guarantees. Lessons can be taken from the fact that CABEI as the AE will complete a capacity assessment of the financial institutions, taking into account financial management and procurement policies.

controls, and ability to implement the project activities. In addition, to ensure the transparent and effective use of financial resources, each fund will be managed by its own governance and oversight structures, which will be independent of each other.

### Co-financing resources risks:
At the time of drafting this report, co-financing for the project was not included in the agreement with the GCF for grant co-financing to be made by the end of 2020. The letters from the Government indicate that the GCF will provide co-financing for the project. In the event that the full amount of co-financing is not provided, the terms and conditions of the co-financing, including the monitoring and reporting arrangements of the co-financing need to be further clarified and included in the short term, at two out of three co-financing resources will not be channelled via the GCF.

### Credit risk:
GCF is to provide a sovereign loan to the Government of Nicaragua (Moody’s rating B1). The credit risk associated with this loan is considered low. The project has a medium-term loan from GCF with a long-term repayment profile is expected to support the Government in making repayment.

### Economic and financial viability:
The AE has provided an economic and financial analysis over the 20-year horizon, considering the potential for increased producers’ income and carbon credits revenues. The analysis shows that the project will be economically viable. The projected financial and economic internal rate of return would be 19.6 per cent and 30.7 per cent respectively. The sensitivity analysis shows that the project still results in positive net present value with a range between -25 to +48, at a 10 per cent decrease in benefits.

### Compliance risk (medium risk)
The AE has confirmed that the project and its activities will not engage with any entity or individual that may be subject to, or listed on, any United Nations Security Council sanctions list, and that no such entities or individuals are involved in this project are subject to or prohibited by these sanctions listed.

### CABEI:
CABEI is an international institutional development bank that has policies and procedures for implementing sanctions, anti-fraud and anti-money laundering/countermeasures and countering the financing of terrorism that are currently in force and in accordance with its policy and procedural manual. CABEI states that its policy and procedural manual is applied and observed in all active and passive operations, and in those involving procurement of goods and services in which it interacts directly or indirectly with entities or individuals subject to sanctions. CABEI is in the process of conducting a review of its anti-money-laundering and countering the financing of terrorism, integrity and sanctions due diligence in accordance with its policy and procedural manual.

### CABEI advice:
CABEI advises that money-laundering prevention policy, manual and procedures are currently being applied across all operations. The policies and procedures and manuals will be applied in order for the project and its activities to conform to any United Nations Security Council sanctions list. CABEI also states that it is not aware of whether entities or individuals involved in the project as counterparties or implementers are listed on any United Nations Security Council sanctions list.

### GCF:
The GCF has advised that it does not maintain a commercial relationship or operation if it suspects that funds of a client or potential client may originate from criminal activities.

# 4.4 Fiduciary

## 4.4.1 Project specific execution Medium financing this co-financing needs to be materialized in a timely manner.

## 4.4.2 GCF portfolio-concentration Low A majority of the project financing (about 82%) is going to be invested through trust funds. The success of the project will depend on the trustee managing these funds efficiently.

## Compliance Medium

### Fiduciary
For this project, CABEI will serve as the AE, while MARENA and MIHP will be the EEs. The project includes a lending component, with additional grant financing to fund sustainable landscape restoration and management, the creation of an enabling investment environment, and strong local capacities for territorial governments in Nangaritza.

In its role as the AE, CABEI will oversee procurement and financial management to ensure compliance with GCF fiduciary standards. The project will be implemented by MARENA and MIHP; as project EEs, they will receive the financial resources for the Bio-CLIMA program from different funding sources (GCF, GEF, CABEI, PCPF) using the specific disbursement mechanisms and reporting requirements of each.

For the receipt of GCF resources, CABEI will set up a special account out of its balance sheet to hold these funds. The account will be managed by the financial management unit attached to MARENA, and to the silvopastoral and cocoa-agroforestry restoration trust funds. These disbursements will have technical and financial support from the CABEI compliance team, which will monitor and evaluate implementation performance of each implementing entity during the lifetime of the project until guaranteeing its accounting and financial closure to GCF.

MARENA, as an implementing entity, will undertake monitoring and evaluation of implementation performance for each implementing entity. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation will be conducted in accordance with CABEI and GCF procedures and guidelines.

Technical project execution, coordination and monitoring will be carried out by MARENA, which will set up and run the technical coordination unit. For the delivery of project outputs and results, MARENA will enter into service agreements or contracts with implementing partners, selected according to their specific requirements.

The financial information will include at least the following documentation that CABEI will provide to GCF in the framework of the accreditation master agreement and the funded activity implementation agreement: disbursement reports, including disbursements made during the relevant period, progress reports, or memo, if so requested by GCF. The project will be subject to an annual audit conducted independently prepared by an independent auditing body. CABEI will guarantee the quality of project implementation and the effective use of assigned international and national resources.

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

The project is a mitigation project focused on land-use restoration and conservation in Nangaritza, Ecuador. It aims to achieve a net carbon benefit through mainly restoring interventions consisting of investments focused on sustainable landscape restoration and management, the creation of an enabling investment environment, and strong local capacities for territorial governments in Nangaritza. The project is expected to support 100 families. The project interventions are expected to sequester 47.3 MCO2eq over 20 years. As a result of the

### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Mater Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 6 September 2017 and became effective on 16 November 2017.

The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all necessary internal approvals for the implementation of the project. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the Funding Proposal to the Board (A) the Accredited Entity has obtained its internal approval and (B) the Fund has received a certificate from the National Designated Authority (NDA) in accordance with the letter sent to the Fund confirming that all final internal approvals by the Accredited Entity have been obtained and that the project is ready for implementation.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Nicaragua, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileged and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF cannot sue or be sued in Nicaraguan courts. The Fund has therefore requested that further legal advice be obtained on this matter.

The NDA has drafted a draft agreement (in English and Spanish) on privileges and immunities to the National Designated Authority (NDA) together with a background note on privileges and immunities in April 2018. The NDA conferred receipt of the draft agreement and background note.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have been expressed that it would not be equity reasonable to allow GCF activity in Nicaragua where the Fund is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the


---
**Page 120**
---

GCC are only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and empropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

### In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Delivery of a copy to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion within 120 days of the Board approval confirming that it has obtained all its internal approvals;

(b) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, which must be delivered to the Fund no later than the date on which the Accredited Entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all its internal approvals; and

(c) Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP146

## Proposal name: Bio-CLIMA: Integrated climate action to reduce deforestation and strengthen resilience in BOSAWAS and Rio San Juan Biosphere Reserves

### Accredited entity: Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

### Country/ies: Nicaragua
### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential - Safe: High

#### 1.1 The Republic of Nicaragua, the largest country in Central America, is located between the Caribbean Sea to the east and the Pacific Ocean to the west. It covers an area of 129,494 km² and has a population of 6.3 million. It is one of the least developed countries in Central America, with its economy heavily dependent on agriculture.

#### 1.2 The Caribbean Region (CR), the eastern region facing the Caribbean Sea, constitutes 54 per cent of the country's territory, while its population represents only 15 per cent of the total. Approximately 80% of the CR is covered by forests and woodlands, which are mainly composed of tropical dry forest.

#### 1.3 The main economic activities in the CR are subsistence agriculture, livestock, coffee, cocoa, palm oil, sugar cane, and bananas. These activities are carried out on small plots of land owned or rented by members of the country's indigenous peoples and those of African descent, who control most of the closed beneficial forests.

#### 1.4 Nicaragua is regarded as one of the countries most vulnerable to climate change. Its geographical position makes the country susceptible to climatic shocks caused by excessive precipitation in the Caribbean region and droughts in the Pacific region. The CR is also affected by the El Niño Southern Oscillation. Natural disasters, mainly hurricanes and tropical storms, cause damage to infrastructure in the CR, as well as losses for the agricultural sector, and impact people from their homes.

#### 1.5 The deforestation rate in the CR has exceeded the national average. In the period 2000-2013, the annual deforestation rate was estimated at approximately 4% of the total forest cover per year, which was equivalent to emission reductions of about 14 MCO2 eq per annum. Additional natural and anthropogenic factors have contributed to this deforestation, including fires associated with additional anthropogenic forest degradation.

#### 1.6 In the Bosawás Biosphere Reserve and Indo Maiz Biological Reserve, a part of Rio San Juan Biosphere Reserve, the annual deforestation rate was regarded as one of the highest in Nicaragua. This has had significant impacts on the conservation of biodiversity and the livelihoods and cultures of indigenous peoples and those of African descent.

#### 1.7 The CR is also affected by climate change pressures caused by drought in the Pacific and Central Regions, plus the expanding road system that enabled migration into the CR. Migrating settlers converted forests into extensive pastures with the establishment of cattle ranches. This led to a significant increase in deforestation rates down to 14.0 ha of extensive pastureland established in the period 1983-2015.


---
**Page 122**
---

6. The proposed mitigation project, Bio-CLIMA, aims to promote integrated climate action to reduce deforestation and strengthen resilience in the Rivas and Rio San Juan Biospheres in the CR. The project will be implemented over seven years.

7. The accredited entity (AE) is the Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI). The executing entity (EE) is the Ministry of Environment, Energy, and Natural Resources (MARENA) and the Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (MICP) of Nicaragua will implement the project as executing entities (EEs).

8. The project requires a total of USD 115.7 million, to be financed by GCF in the form of a grant and loan (USD 64.1 million, 55.4 per cent), CABEI in the form of a loan (USD 19.0 million, 16.4 per cent) and FCPF results-based payments (USD 23.7 million, 20.8 per cent). The Global Environment Facility (GEF) is providing USD 5.9 million as grant funding.

9. The loans from GCF and CABEI are extended against the sovereign guarantees of Nicaragua.

10. The project consists of three components, one primarily for investments, and two for technical assistance.

11. Component 1 will provide investments in sustainable land-use intensification, landscape restoration, agroforestry, silvopastoral systems, agroecology, agroforestry, sustainable agriculture, and close-to-nature planted forests (CNTPFs) through the creation of two trust funds. It will provide material and equipment for the establishment of the silvopastoral, cocoa agroforestry, and CNTPF plantations. Indigenous enterprises in indigenous territories are to be promoted to support the communities through forest management agreements with the CR government. The communities will receive a certification for their products of sustainable forest management of timber and non-timber products. Community-based forest restoration projects will also be supported by creating the Bonawá and Indio Maíz Biological Reserves. Community forest management and forest restoration implementation might also be facilitated in areas beyond the protected areas.

12. A budget of USD 58.3 million is allocated for this component, which represents 50 per cent of the total project cost. This will be financed with a GCF grant (USD 26.0 million), GCF loan (USD 36.1 million), CABEI loan (USD 4.7 million), FCPF results-based payments (USD 25.7 million), and GEF grant (USD 6.0 million).

13. Component 2 will help to create efficient local institutions and transparent governance schemes for the management of forest resources in indigenous territories, as well as maintenance. Public environmental institutions in the CR will be supported to enhance capacities for sustainable forest management and landscape restoration activities. The regional and territory governments of the CR will receive funds to improve the oversight and control of their territories (USD 17 million). In this component, two trust funds are to be created in coordination with project partners.

14. Component 2 requires funding of USD 13.9 million, representing 10.3 per cent of the total budget. This will be financed by a GCF grant (USD 6.8 million), CABEI loan (USD 10 million), FCPF results-based payments (USD 4.0 million), and GEF grant (USD 0.7 million).

15. Component 3 offers training and capacity-building programs to promote an integrated and sustainable farm, landscape and ecosystem restoration approach in the CR. Technical assistance will be provided for the development of a land-use plan. Both the regional and local levels will be trained in the use and implementation of the new land and territory planning instruments, legal and normative frameworks, and productive landscape restoration activities. The project will also provide training on forest fire management systems, such as deforestation and forest-fire early warning systems, and a timber


---
**Page 123**
---

# 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium/High

## 1.2.1 Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning

The project will establish and demonstrate landscape restoration models to counter accelerated deforestation in Nicaragua. They will be effective for enabling local farmers and communities to make informed decisions about their land use, and they will support farmers and communities to formulate a management and development plan of their lands and territories prior to undertaking investments in sustainable land-use intensification, landscape restoration, agroforestry systems, micro-catchments, and the establishment of CBFs and CNTPFs. The success of these project activities will confirm the feasibility of the approaches that the project promotes, and effectively facilitate scaling up and replication can be established.

However, the scalability and replicability of the project’s approach are dependent on the availability of resources for implementation. The LR environment requires available REDD+ plans, results-based payments from FCPF, or other REDD+ funding sources in the future.

## 1.2.2 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The project has technical assistance activities in components I and II to establish an enabling environment that will support the implementation of landscape restoration models on the CR. The components aim to build capacity at the regional and local level, and to help develop tools and instruments to support climate-resilient sustainable development. Training will be organized


---
**Page 124**
---

for community members and farmers to productive landscape restoration, agroforestry and silvopastoral models, restoration of degraded land, and management of their natural forests in an integrated way. Capacities will be built for participatory sustainable land-use management planning processes, including the development of action plans, project proposals, and marketing plans. A public communications strategy will be implemented under component 3, with a special focus on schools and universities.

### 1.2.5 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

The project will support enhancement of the regulatory framework and capacities at both the national and subnational levels for sustainable land management models in the Cofin component 2, the project includes technical support for local environment institutions to retain additional carbon stocks in degraded lands and forests. The institutional capacity of the forestry sector can be enhanced, especially for enforcement. Information systems, including a deforestation monitoring system, will be developed. A national forest inventory will be developed. Land use and land-use change, and a REDD plus measurement, reporting and verification framework operated by MARINA will be strengthened with training of regional and local authorities.

With its technical assistance for training and capacity-building, the project will contribute to the development of an enabling environment for sustainable land management policies and practices. However, its scalability and replicability are dependent on the availability of concessional financing. Methodologically, the independent TAP assesses the project parameters with potential as medium to high.

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential: Scale: High

#### 1.3.1 Environmental co-benefits

The project will contribute to the protection of the most biodiverse rich ecosystems at risks in Nicaragua and Costa Rica. The Carate Reserve, with an area of approximately 2 million ha., is the second-largest rainforest in the western hemisphere after the Amazon. The Indio Maíz Biological Reserve (IMBR) is the largest protected area in Central America and the largest rainforest reserve in Nicaragua. These areas are home to some 35 different ecosystems, 13 of the country's 20 biodiversity hotspots, and more than 40% of the species found in Costa Rica.

The project aims to promote better land use and sequestration in productive landscape restoration models. The agroforestry systems that will be promoted by the project are effective for restoring degraded soils and increasing resilience to climate change. Adapting to changing climatic conditions, CTWP's are effective for the productive rehabilitation and restoration of degraded landscapes.

#### 1.3.2 Economic co-benefits

The productive agroforestry that the project aims to promote will help sustain the livelihoods of communities and contribute to poverty reduction. As a result of the sustainable land management practices promoted by the project, soil fertility and organic matter content will improve, leading to higher productivity. As it enables a wider diversity of crops, agroforestry systems are more resilient than monocultures, not only the risk and flow of seasonal harvests but also risks associated with pests and diseases.

The project will extend support, especially to vulnerable households, in the remote areas that have been most affected by climate change. The project promotes climate-resilient, sustainable production schemes. These schemes will not only improve their livelihoods but


---
**Page 125**
---

also enhance their climate resilience. The project will further support business development of those vulnerable households through the trust funds.

### 1.3.3 Gender-sensitive development impact

The project will ensure women's participation and accounting for their specific needs in decision-making in all activities. The leadership role of women in land management and agricultural production, including agroforestry, will be strengthened. The promotion of agroforestry, silvopastoral, restoration and forest management activities will be enhanced. The project will prioritize support for women-headed households as beneficiaries.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: High

##### Absence of alternative sources of financing

Nicaragua is one of the least developed countries in Latin America. With high fiscal deficits and a large external debt, Nicaragua has experienced large capital outflows, which resulted in negative gross domestic product growth of 3.8 per cent in 2019. The sharp contraction in credit, limited access to private sector financing for investment, and lower private portfolio flows will continue to depress investment in Nicaragua.

The independent TAP considers that there are few alternate sources of financing for the project. The government has a large external debt burden and is unable to provide a grant and interest-free loan with a tenor of 40 years. Given the economic downturns when Nicaragua last received such loans, it is unlikely that the government will be able to meet its obligations.

The conditions are further tightened as its limited budget resources have to be directed to the health sector to suppress the COVID-19 pandemic. Accordingly, public sources of financing to subsidize the project are not available in the short term. The private sector has been hit hard and lending has contracted and external financing remains scarce for Nicaragua. Private financial institutions are also reluctant to lend.

##### The need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

The project includes various activities to respond to the country's needs to strengthen institutions and capacity. It will provide technical assistance to indigenous territories, government agencies, and civil society organizations in land management and forest governance. Additional technical personnel, equipment and operative capacities will be provided to support sustainable land-use planning and environmental/forest law enforcement. Technical personnel from public extension services will be trained on the project's activities related to landscape restoration and planning, implementation and maintenance of productive landscape restoration modules, investment promotion, market access, quality assurance, and standards and norms, strengthening of local organizations, quality management, and market access.


---
**Page 126**
---

15\. The independent TAP considers the needs of the project recipient to be high.

# Country ownership

## Existence of a national climate strategy

The project is in line with Nicaragua's nationally determined contributions (NDCs), under which Nicaragua will raise its carbon absorption capacity by 14 per cent in relation to the reference year 2010. The NDCs also include the following mitigation measures that Nicaragua proposes to implement: (i) promotion of agroecological production of permanent crops under tree shade; (ii) establishment and maintenance of agroforestry systems, including silvopastoral grazing and introduction of silvopastoral services; (iii) establishment of planted forests on idle or degraded forest land, prioritizing the use of natural regeneration of native tree species; (iv) application of soil conservation practices to reduce erosion and improve water retention and ecosystem services, taking advantage of adaptation and mitigation synergies.

The project is aligned with Nicaragua's National Development Plan 2018-2021 and the following Sustainable Development Goals: 1. No poverty; 2. Zero hunger; 3. The National Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation Policy: 1. Development of agriculture that is resilient to climate change, including adaptation measures for smallholder farmers to reduce GHG emissions; 5. Use and conservation of ecosystem services to achieve low-carbon sustainable land management practices; 6. Promotion of the sustainable development and rational use of forests, as the promotion of planted forests in forest land-use zones; and 7. Promote knowledge, research, finance and information about climate change mitigation and adaptation measures for smallholder farmers, including agroforestry and agroecosystems.

The project is being developed to complement the Emission Reductions Program (ERP) that Nicaragua agreed with COP21.

## Coherence with existing policies

### Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver

* CABEI, the AE, is a multilateral bank for the development of Central America. It promotes sustainable development in the region by providing financial and technical support from its founding member countries through public and private sector interventions. During the past 57 years, CABEI has provided more than US$10 billion to Nicaragua, including more than $2 billion through more than 100 financial intermediaries, 10 of which operate in Nicaragua.
* MARENA executes the climate change and environment portfolio at the national level. It has a critical role in the implementation of the NDCs, as well as in providing technical safeguards, and carbon accounting. It will be responsible for overall coordination of the project. Together with CABEI, it is also responsible for the provision of financial and technical support from various government agencies. It has experience in executing rural development projects funded by the Inter-American Development Bank, World Bank, European Union, Inter-American Development Bank, International Fund for Agricultural Development, and GEF. MARENA is the host body for the GEF and EU/GEF, and currently holds the pre-forestry project for the Central American Forest Fund for Emission Reductions.
* MHCIP is the governing body for public finances, and contracts public debt on behalf of the State. It manages financial contracts with international financial institutions including


---
**Page 127**
---

### World Bank, Inter-American Development Bank and CABEI. It directly manages and executes the portfolio of projects with values between USD 10 million and USD 250 million. MHCIP is the national designated agency for GCF.

#### The AE's assessment of the past performance and capacity of MAEENA and MHCIP has confirmed their technical capacity to operate as EEs for the implementation of the project.

##### Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

- The project has been developed in alignment and to complement the Caribbean Coast ERP, which implements the national strategy for avoided deforestation (END+REDD+).
- The national government is leading the process through a multi-stakeholder platform that includes indigenous consultation and participation processes involving relevant stakeholders. Consultations were undertaken with representatives of civil society organizations, including indigenous communities.
- Through the processes, a stakeholder participation plan was developed in the ERP, which also project also refers to, considering the alignment of the activities and territories.

##### A financial proposal has been prepared by CABEI for funding proposal.

##### The independent TAP concludes that the project's country ownership is high. The Government’s proposal to avail itself of GCF and CABEI sovereign loans demonstrates its strong country ownership of the project.


#### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

##### Scale: Medium/High

###### Cost-effectiveness and efficiency regarding financial and non-financial aspects

- The AE estimates that the proposed mitigation project will result in reductions of 12.8 MCO2eq per year, which is equivalent to avoided deforestation, carbon sequestration, CTNPFs, and sustainable community enterprises forest management and restoration. As mentioned, in seven years of implementation, the project is expected to achieve an estimated reduction of 95.6 MCO2eq (100% of the total project cost) or 83.4 MCO2eq (87%) per GCF contribution of USD 64.1 million and a total project cost of USD 115.7 million.
- The AE estimates that the project will result in reductions of 9.8 MCO2eq per year for GCF and USD 2.4 per USD0.5, respectively, if a longer term of 20 years is considered.

###### Amount of co-financing

- A co-financing ratio of 0:1 is estimated based on the GCF contribution of USD 64.1 million, which represents an investment of USD 78.3 million.
- The AE assumes funding for USD 94.1 million, or 21.0 per cent of the total project cost, will be available from REDD+ plus results-based payments from FCPF. The funding represents 18.2 per cent of the total project cost and is expected to be provided by CABEI, which is only half of the full estimated amount to be realized by FCPF over 20 years.

###### Programme/project financial viability and other financial indicators

- The AE expects the project to be financially and economically viable. The financial and economic returns of the project are estimated to be 196 per cent and 387 per cent, respectively, for GCF and CABEI funding, which is equivalent to USD 254.3 million and USD 1.0 billion, respectively. The estimates are the aggregation of the estimated results of various scenarios.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP 146)

## Proposal name: Bio-CLIMA: Integrated climate action to reduce deforestation and strengthen resilience in Bosawás and Rio San Juan Biosphere Reserves

### Accredited entity:
Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

### Country(ies):
Nicaragua

### Project/programme size:
Medium

## Impact potential
The project has an important focus on the conservation and restoration of areas degraded by deforestation, through the establishment of agroforestry and silvopastoral systems. We agree with the ITAP on this scale; since through the implementation of this project, 47.3 million tons of greenhouse gases will be avoided from entering the atmosphere. The project will also generate adaptation co-benefits as it will improve the livelihoods of people living in the intervention areas such as the Bosawás and Indio-Maíz reserves.

## Paradigm shift potential
We believe that the project will be innovative, since it will involve the indigenous communities and people in the protected areas, in activities to counter accelerated deforestation in Nicaragua, as well as biological restoration and conservation activities. This level of involvement is a paradigm shift for the country’s environmental policies and practices.

## Sustainable development potential
We consider that the project has environmental, economic, and gender equity benefits; but also benefits at the social level since it will allow the participation and involvement of indigenous peoples in conservation and biological restoration activities in protected areas. The Project will allow an increase in the capacity of the Bosawás and Indio-Maíz Biosphere Reserve’s Project Steering Committee. We agree with the scale that the ITAP has assessed for this criterion.

## Needs of the recipient
We agree with the assessment made by ITAP in this criterion. Nicaragua is one of the least developed countries in Latin America and Central America, and requires financial support under the terms of its National Development Plan (PNDN) for 2018-2023. However, due to the health and economic crisis caused by the COVID-19 pandemic, the country has limited resources to overcome the crisis.


---
**Page 130**
---

# Country ownership

Nicaragua has made efforts in recent years to formulate its main policies for mitigation and adaptation to climate change, and in the commitments embodied in the determined national contributions (INDCs) of Nicaragua. The project is part of the Public-Private Environmental Action Program (PRE) within which the Bioclima Project is found as one of the most relevant initiatives that are being implemented by the government at the national level. In this sense, we agree with this assessment of the ITAP on this criterion.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

We agree with the ITAP assessment of this criterion. However, we consider that the project is efficient in terms of cost benefits, because the cost per 002kg avoided is low considering the investment that the project funders will undertake (including the GEF), and in the total amount of the project. The project has a good financial performance with respect to its revenues and profitability. Additionally, we consider that the project has a reasonable co-financing ratio, which allows for an efficient usage of resources from various partners, such as CABEI, the World Bank's FCFP and the GEF.

# Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

CABEI as an Accredited Entity appreciates the assessment made by ITAP of the Funding Proposal for the Bio-CLIMA Project. We believe that the ITAP assessment has helped to improve the project proposal in terms of its alignment with the World Bank's environmental criteria and standards. The Project aims to achieve ambitious results in terms of mitigating climate change, through the implementation of activities for the conservation and restoration of biodiversity and the reduction of GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. This project will also have benefits in terms of improving the resilience of the population living in the intervention areas.

# I. INTRODUCTION

## 1.1 Project Introduction from an Intercultural Gender Perspective

The "Integrated climate action to reduce deforestation and strengthen resilience in BOSAWAS and Rio San Juan Biospheres" project, called Bio-CLIMA, seeks to transform existing practices of extensive cattle ranching, agriculture and timber exploitation into more intensive, reforestation-free production forms that integrate ecosystem services with production of goods and services for livelihoods. This will be carried out through three components with the following objectives: (i) increase the productivity and profitability of smallholder farmers by providing financial incentives for sustainable intensification of their agricultural practices and on-farm resource conservation; (ii) create an enabling environment in which clear and simple norms, efficient local institutions, and a strong rule of law can be established to reduce corruption and environmental impunity; and (iii) strengthen local producer organizations and facilitate access to markets that recognize the real-value of quality, sustainable production, climate action and biodiversity conservation.

It will be implemented in the Caribbean Coast region (CR) of Nicaragua, which occupies 54% of the national territory, contains 80% of the country's forests, and where 70% of indigenous peoples live. The North Autonomous Caribbean Coast Region (RACCN) and the South Autonomous Caribbean Coast Region (RACCSC) are also home to the BOSAWAS Natural Reserve and the Indo-Mazé Reserve respectively.

This project will contribute to and benefit from intercultural gender equality and women's empowerment by way of meeting two key project results. One is by improving and enhanced involvement of the most vulnerable people communities in the region, on the other it is by increasing their participation and decision-making power as young and adult indigenous, Afro-descendant and non-indigenous women. Together they will reduce the unequal power relations that underlie the gender gap between women and men of different ages within and across communities and settlements in the project area.

The Bio-CLIMA project in general and the gender components in particular will contribute to measurable changes in:

- Women's decision-making and participation at various levels: household, family farm (and other production units), community and territorial, municipal and regional governments.
- Women's economic empowerment.
- Capacity building of women farmers, producers and members of organizations/communities that addresses equitable participation (trainers and participants), women's empowerment, and breaks down obstacles to intercultural gender equality.
- Strengthening the voice of young indigenous, Afro-descendant and non-indigenous women within and across communities and settlements.
- Men's knowledge, attitudes and practices across age and ethnicity that support gender equality and women's empowerment.
- Sufficient financial and other resources allocated and spent.
- Implementation of the violence against women grievance and complaint mechanism (MillyG).

Overall, the project will address gendered climate threats to food and nutritional security through building increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions. It will use an intercultural gender approach to increase gender equality in all ethnicities.


# 1.2 Objective and Rationale of the Gender Assessment

## 1.1.3 Rationale
The Nicaraguan government has repeatedly manifested its commitment to gender equality at the highest levels. At international and regional levels it has signed and/or ratified:
- The Sustainable Development Goals (2015–2030);
- The Beijing Platform for Action (1995);
- The Cairo Declaration and Programme of Action (1994);
- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW, 1979);
- UN General Assembly Resolution on measure to prevent crime and criminal justice for the elimination of violence against women (A/RES/52/86; 1998);
- The Beijing Declaration;
- The General Assembly's Declaration of the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (2007);
- International Labour Organization (ILO) Convention 169 on indigenous and tribal peoples (2010); and
- Organization of American States (OAS), Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women (Belem-do-Pará, 1996).

## 1.1.2 Objective of the Gender Assessment
The objective of this Gender Assessment is to provide the foundation for the Bio-CIMA project's effective contribution to women's empowerment and intercultural gender equality. The Assessment is composed of:
a) an analysis of the current situation of gender relations and structures with respect to needs, interests, priorities and existing potentials of women and men of different age groups within and across the various ethnic communities in Nicaragua and the project region; and
b) an exploration of how the intercultural gender transformative approach will be applied in the Bio-CIMA project to contribute to measurable improvements in gender equality and women's empowerment.

## II. METHODOLOGY

The material used in this gender assessment was taken from two main sources: the consultations done to develop this funding proposal as well as an extensive literature review.

Three two-day consultative workshops were held for the development of the proposal for Bio-CIMA in 2019. The consultation for the RACCs on September 23-24, 2019 included 31% women (i.e., 28 of 90 participants). The consultation for the North Atlantic Autonomous Region (RAAN) took place on October 7 and 8 with 52% women (i.e., 39 of 71 participants). The consultation for the Alto Wangari and Bocay regions also took place September 19-20, 2019 in San Andrés de Bocay® with only 16% women (i.e., 11 of 70 participants). Note that youth were consulted separately from adults and the figures were not gender-disaggregated.

Participants consisted of members of territorial governments (GTI), central and regional governments, municipal governments, regional universities, and civil society organizations. The latter included a small number of women’s organizations and other groups representing women and girls as well as potential beneficiaries on the following topics, among others: the social and environmental management framework; social and environmental standards and impacts; distribution of benefits; and the grievance and complaints mechanism.

The documents cited rely on data from a range of official sources and, to a lesser extent, academic ones. There are major limitations to the data. One, there are almost no historical analyses of gender relations in the Caribbean region. Another is that updated and adequately disaggregated data by gender, age, ethnicity, and other characteristics are not available for most countries. Finally, gender-disaggregated data dates from between 15 and 20 years ago. These include official censuses and surveys from the last decade (e.g., the agricultural census (CENAGRO), 2011; the urban census of the RAANs and RAAS® (2018); the demography and health survey (EMDESA, 2006-13); the standard of living survey (2005)); and the Population and Housing Census (2005). They also compile data collected from various governments and state institutions. Due to limited time it was not possible for the author to analyze the original data sets for:

• MARENA, 2020: “Marco de Gestión Ambiental y Social - Evaluación y Gestión de Riesgos e Impactos Ambientales y Sociales.” Programa de Reducción de Emisiones para Combatir el Cambio Climático y la Polución en la Costa Caribe, Reserva Costera de Bocay® y Alto Wangari. Informe Final del Proyecto “Estrategia de Gestión Ambiental Integrada para reducir la deforestación y fortalecer la resiliencia en las Reservas de las Balsas Bosawás y Rio San Juan.” p. 41.

The attention to gender in the Caribbean region is limited and fragmented. From 1887 the official terms for the two autonomous Caribbean regions were “North Atlantic Autonomous Region” (RAAN) and “South Caribbean Coast Autonomous Region” (RACC®).

### III. BRIEF HISTORICAL OVERVIEW FROM A GENDER PERSPECTIVE OF THE NICARAGUAN CARIBBEAN REGION SINCE INDEPENDENCE

Prior to European colonization, there were several different Indigenous groups living on either side of present-day Nicaragua. Those in the north migrated south into Mesoamerica, while those on the Caribbean side migrated north from Colombia. Although Nicaragua can be drawn into different regions depending upon how it is defined, for the purposes of this study during the European colonial period, the Spanish came to the Caribbean coast of Nicaragua in 1523 but moved quickly to the centre and Pacific of the country and conquered the existing Indigenous population and established commercial relations with them. The British arrived on the Caribbean side of Nicaragua establishing commercial relations with Indigenous populations in 1633, and an alliance was formed between the Spanish and the British and the English. Only small British settlements existed in the area between 1724 and 1850.

In terms of religion, the Spanish started to convert the Indigenous populations to Catholicism from the time of their arrival. On the Caribbean, eventually the dominant religious presence was that of the Moravian church, which did not arrive to the Mosquito Coast until 1849. On the Caribbean coast, there were two major religions: Roman Catholic and Protestant. The British established a settlement and gathering, which gradually shifted to include trade with the British. The other consisted of profit-oriented religious groups such as the Moravians who arrived in 1750. In 1834, the British established sugar cane and cotton plantations with slave labour they forcibly brought from Africa. The British also began commercial logging in the eighteenth century. The first British Protectorate, established by the British East India Company, was the Mosquito Kingdom, which lasted until 1765. Nonetheless, Britain maintained its economic interests and "special relationship" with the Indigenous populations of the Mosquito Kingdom throughout the Mosquito Coast (a territory that covered parts of Nicaragua and Honduras).

The tumultuous nineteenth century saw the creation of the Republic of Nicaragua and its eventual political control over the entire territory as well as growing international interest in exploiting the natural resources of the Caribbean coast. The United Provinces of Central America, including Nicaragua, achieved independence from Spain on 1823 to 1838; each country was granted a degree of separate state sovereignty. Slavery was abolished in the Caribbean by 1841. From 1860 to 1865, the second British Protectorate was established with the goal to ensure British commercial interests. In 1860 Great Britain released more control, and the Mosquito Empire was created, which lasted until 1923.

There are various spellings, but generally Miskito is used in the region themselves; Mosquito by the British, and Miskito is commonly used in English and Spanish.


---

Nicaraguan state. In 1894, the Nicaraguan state incorporated all the Mosquito Reserve (i.e., the territory of the Caribbean side of the country) and its residents named Rama since before then-the president of Nicaragua declared the region "reconquered," a term that was repeated by the Caribbean community because it negates the region's separate history and identity[9].

Under the Somozas family dictatorship (1936-1979), the central Nicaraguan government had little involvement in economic, political and social life in the Caribbean region. Separate from the communal economy of the Rama people, the colonial-style plantation system was introduced to the area to build infrastructure (roads, schools, health centres) to and in the local communities. The Moravian church provided education for some children and some public services were provided by the state that might not have had access to public service[10]. During the twentieth century as during the previous two hundred years, the people of the Caribbean coast had little contact with the Pacific side of Nicaragua or with the rest of the country. The situation changed dramatically after the Sandinista victory in 1979; indigenous and Afro-descendent communities began to organize in different ways in the north and south of the country. Organizations of indigenous people in the north demanded collective land rights, while those on the Caribbean coast demanded recognition for their languages, Creole organization in the south sought improved economic development, education and political participation[11].

The start of the Sandinista Popular Revolution in 1979 until autonomy was granted in 1987 was a period of great upheaval for the Caribbean Coast and its inhabitants. The revolution came to power seeking to unify all Nicaraguans as part of its national liberation struggle to rid the country of not only the dictatorship but also the United States' influence[12]. The Sandinista government sought to improve people's lives especially the urban and rural poor, for example through land reform, free health care and education for all, an expanded social welfare net and even promoting popular culture. The nationwide literacy campaign was a major success with 90% of Nicaraguans learning how to read[13]. Sandinista leaders were able to convince the central government to offer literacy in indigenous languages and Creole. This process was later seen as significant for bolstering demand for autonomy at the community level.

Although very little history of the Caribbean region from a gender perspective has been told so far, since the 1980s women have become more active for their rights and in the revolution of the 1970s. That said, during the Sandinista Popular Revolution (1979-1990), women were able to access higher education and jobs. There were also more formal and informal opportunities for women to participate in politics[14]. However, there was still a long way to go before women had equal rights with men.

Since the 1960s and 1970s, some forms of women's activism have taken the form of artistic expression, such as poetry and song. This has been a means of identity and resistance. One very important feature of Caribbean Coast women's cultural production over time has been the representation of the multiple intersections of their collective action, race, ethnicity and geographical location.

It was a significant achievement to get the autonomy law (Law 28) passed in 1987. The bill was developed using a participatory process that involved many actors with various perspectives, some of which were contradictory. At the same time, this culmination point was also the start of the complex process of implementation. The autonomy law will be discussed in greater detail in the following section.

### IV. CONSTITUTIONAL, LEGAL AND INSTITUTIONAL FRAMEWORK OF GENDER EQUALITY AND AUTONOMY IN NICARAGUA AND THE CARIBBEAN COAST REGIONS

#### 4.1 Constitution

The 1987 constitution of Nicaragua[1] recognized both women's equality as well as the autonomy of the North Caribbean Coast Autonomous Region (RACCN) and South Caribbean Coast Autonomous Region (MACCS), commonly referred together as "the Coast", and the autonomy law recognizes women's equality.

To be more specific, article 5 of the constitution states:

- Liberty, justice, respect for the dignity of the human person, political, social and ethnic pluralism, the protection of the environment, private property, free international cooperation and respect for the free self-determination of peoples are principles of the Nicaraguan nation.

Article 48 establishes the state's duty to take affirmative action to ensure equality of conditions.

Article 90 states that:

- The communities of the Atlantic Coast have the right to preserve and develop their cultural identities within the national unity, to provide themselves with their own forms of social organization and to administer their local affairs according to their traditions.
- The Nicaraguan state recognizes the existence of indigenous peoples who enjoy the rights, duties and guarantees designated in the Constitution, and especially those to maintain and develop their identity and culture, to have their own forms of social

[1] República de Nicaragua, 2014. 1987 Constitution and its reforms.


---
**Page 141**
---

organization and administer their local affairs, as well as to preserve the communal forms of land tenure and their exploitation, use, and enjoyment, all in accordance with law.

For the inhabitants of the Communities of the Atlantic Coast, an autonomous regime is established in the present Constitution.


### 4.2 Autonomy Statute and Laws, Including Collective Land Rights and Legal Pluralism

The Autonomy Law, first implemented under the Sandinista National Liberation Front (FSLN) government in 1987, sought to redress the injustices created by centuries of foreign and internal colonization. It was designed to ensure that the inhabitants of the Atlantic Coast would be able to retain their historic right to the natural resources of the region as well as the right to defend, preserve, and promote their identity, history, culture and traditions. Woods Downs called it "the highest aspiration" of Costa Rican policy... – according to the law, they should be able to elect their own members.


According to article 28, Article 15, the inhabitants of the Communities of the Atlantic Coast (today Caribbean Coast) have the following rights:

- Absolute equality of rights and responsibilities, regardless of the size of their population and level of development.
- To preserve and develop their language, religions, and cultures.
- To use and benefit from the communal waters, forests, and lands, within the plans for national development.
- To freely develop their social and productive organizations in accordance with their own values.
- Education based on mother tongue and by means of programs which include their historical heritage, their value system, and the traditions and characteristics of their environment, all in accordance with the law.
- Communal, collective, or individual forms of property and the transfer of property.
- To elect their own authorities, or be elected as such in the Autonomous Regions.
- To use and benefit from traditional knowledge and natural medicine accumulated throughout their history, in coordination with the national health system.


In this context, autonomy constitutes an important component of a new strategy for regional development. It was established to address the union social, political, cultural and economic needs of the CR, or aims to promote "equity in diversity" by encouraging equitable and sustainable development while fostering the participation of all sectors of society.

The autonomy law (law 28, article 23) states that the autonomous governments are responsible for women's full participation in all sectors, political, economic, social and cultural. This responsibility is further defined in the regulatory legislation of the Nicaraguan autonomy statute (Decree 558A, article

28), which includes the establishment of a specialized body to ensure that women are fully engaged. The four forms of participation required and recommended in policy and action plans include engaging programmes and projects; ensuring participation in the decision-making of the regional autonomous governments and the regional councils; as well as informing the population about laws, programmes etc., from the central government and monitoring their implementation.

The normative framework for autonomy clearly establishes provisions to ensure the population's right to participate. However, this normative framework on paper cannot guarantee its implementation of all aspects of the law. To ensure that national laws and programmes uphold the collective property rights of indigenous peoples, in 2013, the Nicaraguan government granted communal land rights to the indigenous and ethnic communities of the Caribbean Coast and provided the parameters for making communal land claims under law no. 445, the law of the communal lands of the Atlantic Coast of Nicaragua. The law also provides for the creation of Autonomous Regions of the Atlantic Coast of Nicaragua and the Roca, Coco, Inza, and Mader River Laws". Law 445 represents "the ineluctable commitment of the State of Nicaragua to respond to the claim for the titling of the lands of the indigenous peoples of the Caribbean Coast and the territories of the Atlantic Coast of Nicaragua... This right to the land is recognized in the 1987 Political Constitution of Nicaragua and in the Statute of Autonomy of the Autonomous Regions of the Atlantic Coast.

Law 445 also refers to the more communal aspect of land communal property. This includes "the collective ownership concerning the lands, water resources, and other natural resources." The law also extends to include intellectual property regarding biodiversity and other assets that have traditionally belonged to these communities.

The autonomous Caribbean regions of Nicaragua are currently inhabited by six ethnic groups: Creole and Garifuna, Miskito, Rama, Sumo, Mulaiti, Mayagna (indigenous), and mestizos. The mestizos are the majority of the population over recent decades more peasant farmers from other regions of Nicaragua have moved to the Caribbean regions to settle. However, the indigenous and Afro-descendant communities continue to be the most numerous in these autonomous regions.

Among other criteria, the autonomy process: enriches national culture; recognizes and strengthens ethnic identity; respects the specificity of cultures, identities and religious freedoms; regulates values; respects history; and recognises property rights over communal lands. It also rejects any type of discrimination against indigenous peoples or communities.

The communities have the right to practice traditional forms of leadership and organization, as well as the formalization and regulation of diverse histories and traditions. The law also allows for the even distribution of taxes derived from exploitation of natural resources inside communal lands. It also affirms that the communities have a communal right to manage along with the Ministry of Natural Resources (MARENA) of the central government both nature reserves and protected areas located inside communal territories.

There are also other laws that make it possible for people of the Miskito, Rama, and Garifuna to regain their rights and redress the historical codification services in CR and the rest of the country. Some of these is the Law of Community Languages of the Communities of the Atlantic Coast of Nicaragua (law 182) of 1996. Another law is law 75, the Law of Traditional Ancstral Medicine (2002). These laws are widely used in the CR.

Article 62 of the organic law of the judiciary (law 206), establishes that the administration of justice in the autonomous regions will be carried out so as to reflect the cultural particularities of its communities, including a respect for legal pluralism. In other words, it recognizes the customary law of indigenous and Afro-descendant groups. The law also provides for the establishment of a regional judicial organization, although it has been adapted to the needs of the communities. Every Miskito, Rama, Garifuna, and Caribun community has an elected white (traditional leader and local judge) and Singe (person responsible for the administration of justice in the community). In the case of indigenous communities have a coordinator. Indigenous and Afro-descendant communities have their own justice traditions of negotiation and conflict resolution, carried out by whites. A recent reform to the criminal code granted whites the right to administer justice in these communities. The legal systems of indigenous and Afro- descendant legal traditions are complex, which may make it difficult to access justice12.

Among the main institutions that have taken on the responsibilities for building the autonomy process are the regional universities, particularly University of the Autonomous Caribbean Coast Regions of Nicaragua (UCAAN). These institutions have been involved in intercultural analysis and research. The intercultural analysis of violence against women. While all women may experience some of the same forms of violence, they do so within a context that is shaped by their culture, ethnicity, religion, class, gender, color, power relations, the patriarchal system of social organizations, the history, codes, norms, and myths related to violence. Among the issues that are relevant to this approach to studying violence against women in Nicaragua are: the role of space in the perpetration of violence; the critical analysis of the appropriation and significance of space, the implications of local judges and self-judges. The intercultural view opens up a range of perspectives based on women’s diverse experiences13.

### 4.3 Regional and Territorial Governments

The autonomy law, law 26, regulates the North and South Caribbean regions and creates a new level of government. Through this autonomy regime, the communities choose their own authorities from among the citizens of their jurisdiction. They have the authority to regulate situations of internal life and the administration of their regional interests14.

The purpose of law is to decentralize time and title indigenous and Afro-descendant territories to guarantee them the right to use, administration and management of their lands and natural resources. The law defines indigenous and ethnic territory: geographic space that covers the entire habitat of a group of indigenous or ethnic communities that make up a territorial unit when they develop their own political organization and self-government. This is the basis for creating the regional

and territorial governments, which is the entity in charge of developing and coordinating the Autonomous Plan of Development and Administration of each indigenous territory, as stipulated in the autonomy law.

The territorial government is formed by two representatives from each communal command. Usually, Twelve representatives usually form the territorial staff. However, the general assembly, which is constituted by the population of the territory, is the highest authority. The general assembly must give its prior and informed consent for any major decisions such as convening large projects on the territory.

The communities have the right to practice traditional forms of leadership and organization, as well as the formalization and regulation of diverse histories and traditions. The law allows for the even distribution of communal lands among women and men. Article 10 of Law No. 732/2015 also affirms indigenous and Afro-descendant communities’ right to co-manage along with the Ministry of Natural Resources (MARNENA) of the central government both nature reserves and protected areas located inside communal territories. Prior to this law, this responsibility was exclusively assigned to the central government.

### 4.4 Laws and Policies on Gender Equality

The most recent laws on gender equality are the following:

- **Law No. 790 amending Law No. 331 of the Electoral Act (2012)**: Quota law for candidate lists for municipal, parliamentary and Central American Parliament elections
- **Law 717 (2010)**: Creates a fund to buy land for rural women from a gender equality perspective
- **Criminal Code (2008)**: Bans abortion under any circumstances; Penalizes woman and medical professionals for abortion; Removes criminalization of homosexuality
- **Law of Equal Rights and Opportunities (2008)**: Gender perspectives in all public policies; Eliminates civil, political, economic, social and cultural rights between women and men; Law is obligatory and includes sanctions

### National and Regional Public Policies and Programs

* National Human Development Program (2018-2021)
