## V. CLIMATE CHANGE: FORESTRY AND ENVIRONMENT: LEGAL POLICY AND INSTITUTIONAL FRAMEWORK FROM A GENDER PERSPECTIVE

### 5.1 Climate Change and Environment: Laws and Policy

Between 2013 and 2013, forest loss decreased by 9.6% due to the implementation of a national plan for reforestation and a reduction in losses from wildfires. Data from the National Forestry Institute (Infor) showed that the average amount of deforestation, which had been 70,000 hectares, was reduced to 63,270.

Nicaragua is already exposed to a high recurrence of extreme events, natural disasters, very high temperatures on the Pacific Coast, and flooding throughout the country. It is expected that these events will occur more frequently with increased climate change. It is important to note that the estimates are not intended to be used as an absolute value for temperature, humidity, wind speed, and precipitation that are attributable to climate change, and therefore, the values of all other variables. The estimates should therefore be interpreted as possible outcomes if adaptation measures are not implemented. These include adaptive practices such as those that support soil conservation, reduce soil degradation and erosion, include components to reduce fuel for agricultural production, which in turn can lead to hunger.

The effects of climate change on Nicaragua's Caribbean region have been severe in the last decade. Many local residents have been forced to take protection and adaptation measures to address the phenomenon that have gradually undermined their safety and changed their way of life. For example, they have experienced a series of hurricanes, floods due to heavy rains or storm surges, environmental degradation, and loss of biodiversity, which has caused significant and sometimes economic damage to the local population. The region has historically endured heavy flooding, but something changed for the better: there were very few fatalities, wounded or missing people.

### 5.2 Forestry Law and Policy

The objective of the 2003 Forestry law, the Law of Conservation, Fomentation and Sustainable Development of the Forestry Sector is the establishment of a legal regime for the conservation, promotion and sustainable development of the forestry sector. The fundamental aspects are forest management of the natural wooded area, the promotion of plantations, and the protection, conservation and restoration of


---
**Page 147**
---

forest areas at the national level. Another important element is the list of trees that cannot be felled anymore. The Law created a National System of Forest Administration (SNFA), the National Forestry Commission, and forest utilization committees.

The forestry law provides for the organization of forest districts and development of local forest management plans. The Law Prohibiting Logging (Law No. 585) [2006] banned the export of timber, although a Presidential Decree (No. 48 of 2008) allowed the collection of trees fallen by Hurricane Felix in the Region of Matagalpa. The law also established that all forest products are subject to a tax and that wood is subject to a fee assessed on the value of the lumber. Requests for permission to cut timber must be accompanied by a forest management plan and permits are granted on the basis of an operating plan. Previous laws had allowed logging in protected areas.

In 2008, the Government adopted a National Policy for Sustainable Development of the Forest Sector (Executive Decree No. 69-2008), which emphasizes environmental protection and conservation, as well as sustainable development of the forests to support livelihoods and economic growth. It replaced the Forestry Law.

Although law 415 privileges the land claims of indigenous and ethnic communities, to date there has been no attempt to remove those families who are illegally occupying communal lands. In the meantime, these settlers remain in communal territories using the lands for economic activities that prove to be harmful to the environment: burning forests to clear land; cutting down trees to make charcoal for cows; deploying slash and burn agricultural practices; and attempting to farm in coastal soils that are unsuited for soil-intensive crops.

Policy regarding the environment and forestry is of vital interest to indigenous and Afro-descendant communities. Understanding their relationships and practices on these lands is essential for the ongoing survival of their communities. Both women and men understand that land as a critical aspect of Coast identity for indigenous and Afro-descendant peoples.


### 5.3 Natural Resources and Protected Areas: Legal, Policy and Institutional Framework

There are several laws that are relevant to the creation of reserves, including Madera and other protected areas in Nicaragua. These include Law No. 415 (2006) on the Protection of Indigenous and Afro-descendant Communities and Their Territorial Rights; Law No. 585 (2006), which prohibits logging and hunting; and its Incorporatory Reforms [647] the decree of protected areas of Nicaragua (D1-2007) and law 382. These areas became part of the National System of Protected Areas of Nicaragua. The declaration was developed in collaboration with the Ministry of Environment, Energy and Natural Resources, the Regional Government and the Autonomous Regional Council of the South Caribbean Coast of Nicaragua, SERENA, MARENA, as well as the Community Territorial Governments.

According to the regulation of protected areas (Decree D1-2007) the provisions for the Management of Protected Areas provides a model for the administration of protected areas, in which MARENA is the

### VI. INTERCULTURAL GENDER ANALYSIS IN NICARAGUA AND THE CARIBBEAN COAST: OVERVIEW

#### 6.1 Basic Demographic Statistics

The North Caribbean Coast Autonomous Region (RACCN) and the South Caribbean Coast Autonomous Region (RACC) are more multicultural and multiracial than the rest of Nicaragua. About 82% of the Nicaraguan population is mestizo, indigenous or Afro-descendant.

The proportions of different ethnicities varies greatly across the two regions, as indicated in tables 1 and 2.* The male/female ratio is close to 50% for all indigenous and Afro-descendant populations.*

A brief introduction to the different ethnicities present on the Caribbean Coast is essential. Article 12 of the autonomous statute states that the indigenous peoples living on the Atlantic Coast have the right to define and decide upon their own ethnic identity.* In the OR there are currently six main ethnicities:

* **RACCN:** Plan de Manejo baso el modelo de manejo conjunto del área protegida RACCN vigente para el período 2013–2024, del Refugio de Vida Silvestre del Sistema de los Caños Perlas (INVSI), Bluefields, BICU.

* **RACC:** Manual de Aplicación del Enfoque de Género en las Regiones Autónomas de la Costa Caribe,” Washington, DC: Inter-American Development Bank and World Bank.

* *Regional Statistical Yearbook (RSY), Table No. 18.* Autonomous Institute for the Regions of the Atlantic Coast (Indio Caribbean Coast) of Nicaragua


---
**Page 149**
---

two are Afro-descendant (Garífuna and Creole), three are Indigenous (Miskito, Mayagna and Rama) and mestizos10.

MAYANGNA: There are three socio-linguistic groups: the Tawahkas, Panamakas and Ulwas. The Ulwas are located in the community of Karawala, in the municipality of La Barra de los Rio Grande (RACCS). Tawahkas and Panamakas are generally located in the Bosawás reserve, RACCN. This ethnic group has little mobility within the regions.

RAMAS: This is the least numerous indigenous group today. Most live in Rama Cay Island, south of Bluefields Bay. There are also families scattered along the lower Caribbean coast in the basins and nearby areas of the rivers of Puerto Cabezas and Pearl Lagoon.

GARIFUNAS: This is the ethnicity that arrived most recently to the Caribbean Coast and traditionally live in the RACCS. They settled in villages at the Pearl Lagoon basin (RACCN) after migrating from Honduras. They are descended from African and indigenous Caribbean Arawak peoples.

MISKITOS: Miskito live in about 250 communities in various parts of both the RACCN and RACCS, including Puerto Cabezas, La Paz de la Araya, San Juan del Norte, and the municipalities of Puerto Cabezas and Pearl Lagoon basin. It is a population of great intra-regional mobility, but with a great sense of roots and belonging to their communities, to which they return after long periods of temporary employment in urban centers or other regions.

CREOLES: This population identifies as direct descendants of African slaves and the other Blacks from the Caribbean islands. They are mostly located in Bluefields, Corn Island, Laarga de Portis and the urban area of Puerto Cabezas. Creoles have traditionally been involved in commerce and public servants. Many women are nurses or teachers. They fish and farm in the Pearl Lagoon and Bluefields areas. Currently, young men and women seek temporary jobs on international cruise ships after completing their secondary education while maintaining family ties during their work stay. There are also some Creoles in the Meso-Isla-Mazie area.

MESTIZOS: The mestizo population has become a majority in the Caribbean region, and have lived in different urban and rural areas. Since the nineteenth century, Hispanic-speaking mestizos who have migrated from Central America to the Caribbean Coast have settled illegally on indigenous territories, including in forests and other natural reserves. Mestizos are present with a limited historical presence in both regions. Their main economic activities include cattle farming, timber extraction, and agriculture. Large numbers of Mestizos have settled in the natural reserves of Bosawas and Isla de Maiz.

The figures in the above tables provide a snapshot in time, as the situation has continued to change. The main reasons have been socioeconomic changes: the enclave economy; advance of the agriculture and cattle-raising frontier; indiscriminate exploitation and depletion of natural resources; and military conflict(1).

Tables 1 and 2 also show the low population density in the two autonomous regions. Whereas the two regions make up about half of the country's territory, it has only about 12% of the population(2). The regions are characterized by a young population: 48% is under 15 years old in the RACCS; populations are rural(3). This is one important factor that relates to income generation and/or subsistence activities as well as explaining the gender gap with respect to access to health and other social services.

Also, a higher proportion of the population is younger in the Caribbean region compared to the national rate. In the RACCS, 48% is under 15 years old and in the RACCS it is 45%. The national proportion is 37%(4).

### 6.2 Education and Literacy(5)

In general, there are not many significant gender gaps among population segments regarding literacy and primary and secondary education in the Caribbean Coast regions; however, there are with respect to technical education.

There is a higher literacy rate among rural populations in both Caribbean Coast regions (about 50%) as compared with urban populations (20%). The main gender gap found was that rural women in the RACCS are more literate(6).

Educational systems mostly show no significant gaps in terms of primary education. That said, data indicates about the greater enrolment of adolescent girls in secondary school – particularly the retention of adolescent girls in the RACCN – and about 10% higher enrolment of men in adult education. Both of these are important factors to consider when thinking about women's ability to work whereas adult women take much greater responsibility in the care economy and child-rearing.

There are significant gender gaps with respect to technical education in terms of both enrolment and specialization. For example, men are far more frequently enrolled in sectors related to forestry and agriculture(7). Women, however, are more highly enrolled in these areas than the national average. Also, although there is a small gender gap at the national level, the gap is significant in both Caribbean regions, and more so in the RACCN (see table 3).

## 6.3 Women, Decision-Making and Leadership

### 6.3.1 Political Representation and Decision-Making

Throughout the country there are two levels of government, national and municipal; meanwhile the RACCS and the RACCN have a third level of government to administer the autonomous regions. Indigenous peoples are represented in all three levels of government: national, regional, municipal, territorial and communal**. Law 704 and its 50% quota is gradually being implemented at each different level of government; in a few cases women's representation has already reached close to 50%.

In the national parliament there were 3 deputies for the RACCS and 2 represent the RACCN in the 2012-2016 period. All the RACCN representatives were women (40%) and all the RACCS deputies were men (80%). Their ethnic distribution was: 2 men/women from the RACCS (RNRN); 3 men/women on the RACCN (MNRN); 1 man/woman from the RACCN (C or Creole) and 1 man/woman of the indigenous region (20%). One study further pointed out that the number of deputies assigned to the RACCS and RACCN was disproportionate compared with their population sizes; this had the effect of leaving Coast peoples underrepresented**. This is why women in the National Assembly holds the position of First Secretary.

In the Regional Councils of the RACCS and the RACCN, 50% is slowly in the process of being reached, especially since 2010. In the data for the most recent period (2010-2013), women made up just 28.9% of representations in the RACCN and only 21.2% in the RACCS**.

More recent data for the 2014-2017 period shows that the percentages of women councillors increased once again. Table 5 provides both the total numbers of women as well as their ethnic distribution.

One analysis found that women's formal political leadership in Bilwi, Puerto Cabezas has been generated by a historical process of involvement in the Moravian church, the Sandinista revolution and post-1990 development[4].

Reflecting the growing emphasis on reaching equality in terms of women's formal decision-making power, participants in all three community consultations for Bio-CLIMA placed great emphasis on the need for women to have equal representation in the territorial governments (GT)[5]. They also insisted on increasing their participation and decision-making role, including by developing projects and providing resources[6].

### 6.3.2 Leadership

Contrasting to women's representation in formal politics, women of different populations in the Caribbean region have long been community leaders. They have exercised this role in different manners. At the same time there are a number of perspectives, positive and negative, about women's leadership at the community level[7].

One form of traditional leadership consists of transmitting indigenous and Afro-descendent identities and cultures to the community, for example through story-telling[8]. Another strategy consists of


---
**Page 154**
---

research and organizing of women, whether as indigenous or Afro-descendant women, or through intercultural coalitions'. Another pertinent way is through the strategic use of women's traditional cultural knowledge in land stewardship'.

That said, studies have found that women's informal leadership is not always recognized by others in society in general or by those with formal authority. An underlying factor relates to the 'invention of tradition'. For example, people commonly say "she knows more about children so send her home", or "her husband will decide". This reflects a tendency for men to be seen as leaders and women as followers. Different ethnicities have traditionally been active in various ways in agricultural production and land stewardship. This could mean that women are seen as either positive or negative actors in society, but also, culture can serve both as either a positive or negative factor in women's lives (for both), depending on how it constrains or enables women's autonomy102. This lack of recognition also points to the existence of a barrier that impedes women from becoming informal leaders because they are elected.

At the community level, where decisions about forests are made, one study found that women's leadership and decision-making is somewhat undermined by factors that impede women from participating effectively in forest decision making at the community level relate: to low levels of communication between women and men, lack of access to information for organizing women and informal sanctions. It concluded that improving women's participation in communal decisions is directly related to increasing women's decision-making equality at the household level103.

Another study found that women are more trusted as community leaders because the population thought they were less corruptible.


### 6.3 Civil Society and Inter-Sectoral Coordination

In the community consultations for Bio-CLIMA, the representation of non-indigenous women's organizations mentioned that her organization used to be part of an Alliance with the government and the Women's Directorate. However, this collaboration had almost stopped. She offered to coordinate with the government again given their presence in all indigenous territories104.

**6.5 Gender Division of Labour and Access and Control of Resources at the Household Level**

Studies have demonstrated a number of critical points regarding the household division of labour and decision-making. These are: (i) women and girls actively contribute to the household; (ii) their work

varies over time and across communities; (iii) their work is often not recognized; and (iv) their participation in income generation and decision-making at the household level varies greatly and it not necessarily linked to their participation in formal decision-making structures at the community or higher levels.

In her study of women's agricultural production in the Pearl Lagoon Basin, Barbeins maintains that "different traditions, socio-economic structures, cultural values, norms and gender ideologies are all embedded within these communities and serve as either allow and support women's farm practices or discourse" [15].

In general, men own far more land than women do, and the size of the land they own is greater. Furthermore, women have far less access to credit. This unequal relationship is evident at both the national levels and on the Caribbean Coast.

One study of Mayagna and Miskito communities in the RACCN found a complex relationship regarding extraction of forest resources, use of those products and control of income[16]. For example, it found that men do most of the extraction of forest resources (Table 8).

Qualitative methods, including focus groups and observation, found that despite beliefs of shared decision-making, in practice women faced many obstacles to playing an effective role in decision-making both at the household level and within the community. It was also found that women were not present in discussions related to decision-making; they do not necessarily influence the decisions made. Furthermore, women are more aware of this dynamic than men.

Other studies have gathered compelling evidence that women play a key role in accessing and/or controlling resources. One example relates to cocoa production. Women often cut the cocoa pods because they grow low. Then women and boys and girls cut open the cocoa, dry it and prepare it. Then men sell the pod and sell the cocoa. In this case, women are responsible for the entire process of harvesting and selling the product. If a family participates in planting, then men do the harvesting. If hulling the rice is industrialized then it is a man's activity; if not, he does that too. Women are also responsible for the marketing of cocoa beans as well as the sales. As for beans, mostly men do the planting and harvesting while women and older children dry them. Another example comes from artisanal gold mining. Adult women pan for gold by standing in water while young girls wash the sand. The women sell their gold to a middleman who sells it at prices of precious mine shafts. Then adult men take the small piece or pieces of gold they find (the size of a small fingernail or smaller) take them to the city to sell them. The women then go back home to continue panning for gold while the men go out and sell their gold. Women are also responsible for any direct income that she can decide how to spend or indirectly in the form of supplies purchased for the family. In some cases, women earn money by selling food at a market or by drinking at a bar. This dynamic is frequent in the region and can be found among various ethnicities.

Generally speaking, in the gender division of labour in the CR, rural women are the ones responsible for reproductive care economy, while their participation in production is usually invisible. There is some flexibility to this division as women may participate in agricultural activities or work outside the home as helpers of their husbands. For example, they prepare the land and women sow the seeds. Indeed, the organization of agricultural work at the family level relies on the participation of women and men, and

# 6.6 Women Headed Households

The rate of female headed households varies widely in the Caribbean macro-region by area and ethnicity, as well as over time. National census data show that women are heads of households in 21.0% of all households in the region. In the Caribbean, while the highest percentages are found in Rostro Rosita (predominantly a mestiza municipality) has 37%, and ethnically diverse municipalities in the RACCs are even higher: Com Island (46%) and Bluefields (50%). Studies from the Caribbean Coast show higher percentages of female headed households, with 48% being found among Bribri. The 2019 census also showed that the percentages had increased since 1993.

One participant in the community consultations for the Bio-CLIMA project, who represented the indigenous women's group AMIGA, said that in the communities where they work, about 70% of women are single mothers who are raising three or more children, and some have seven or eight.

# 6.7 Water and Sanitation

The rate of access to water and sanitation for the RACCs and the RACCN is far below the national average, as can be seen in table 10. While the percentage for households in the RACCN is less than half of the national average, those in the RACCS have a quarter of the national percentage. In terms of sanitation, the RACCN has only 24% access to improved sanitation facilities compared with 58% less than the national. This has a very negative effect on the health of all members of the family.

# 6.8 Health

Indicators of women’s sexual and reproductive health show a dramatic disparity between the health of Nicaraguan women overall and women of the RACCN and RACCSS in particular. This is largely the result of the lack of access to health care facilities. While there are health centres in every community, the services provided and resources available (medicines, etc.) are often limited. There is also a notable difference between the RACCN and RACCSS in terms of the quality of services offered by the public health care system. This can be explained by the greater proportion of rural population as well as the continued use of indigenous or Afro-descendant traditional practices. Nicaragua has one of the highest adolescent fertility rates in Latin America and the Caribbean, and the ratio of women aged between RACCN and RACCSS is significantly higher than the national one, where the figure in the RACCS is the highest. According to the criminal code, sex under the age of 14 years is statutory rape, so all those girls under 15 who are already mothers (1.2% RACCN and 1.0% RACCSS) – and many more who did not report their age – are victims of sexual abuse. The maternal mortality ratio per 100,000 inhabitants is high at the national level and somewhat higher in the RACCS; but it is notably higher in the RACCN. The prevalence of use of contraception methods and unintended pregnancies among users of contraception also showed much greater lack of use and unmet needs in the RACCN than the RACCSS.

The most common cause of cancer among women was cervical cancer, where the national prevalence rate is 13.9 cases per 100,000 women in 2002.

In the Caribbean region, adolescent and adult indigenous women may suffer at some point from “Gris Skinis” (or la wakita), which is an endemic cultural syndrome. Symptoms include headaches, loss of consciousness, vomiting, as well as a strong desire to run to a mountain or river and attack anyone who gets in their way. This has a disturbing impact on the victims, as well as the entire community who tries to help those affected.

# Migration Patterns

There are two main migration patterns to and from the CR, as well as several tendencies related to them: The main one is the advance of the agricultural frontier which began in the nineteenth century and became more intense first in the 1960s and even more so since the 1990s. In this flow, the Caribbean Coast is a destination point for mostly rural mestizos from the Pacific and central areas of the country who are seeking land to cultivate. The second migration pattern is the "slash-and-burn" - though it is titled to indigenous and Afro-descendant communities – they cut down all the trees and practice extensive slash and burn agriculture that deteriorates the soil. For example, between 1995 and 2000 the population of the Caribbean Coast increased by 34%, while the rest of the country only grew by 6%.

The other main dynamic is out-migration. The main tendency is Afro-descendant people who work on cruise ships out of Miami, referred to as "lapping out", while others may go to certain Caribbean islands. Between 1995 and 2005, the RACCS annual population change rate decreased by 1.2%.

One study of the lapping out dynamic found that Afro-descendant women face combined racial, gender and class discrimination and violence. Since they suffer discrimination and violence while on ships registered in other countries and working mostly in international waters, they have no recourse for justice[6].

## Religion and Spirituality

Life from a spiritual perspective has a significant positive impact on people's physical and mental health. For most Coast people, spirituality is an integral part of their life; it is understood to be the path to well-being. There are two main types:

*Spirituality is that internal force, that inexplicable energy that makes coastal people resilient, it is what we call Faith in God, complete resignation to the all-powerful. That provides us with energy that balances our life, to achieve the well-being that enhances and identifies the coast family," in the north and lower Caribbean Coast, spirituality is a way of life, it is not only a religious practice, but also as spirituality in itself. Very few differentiate between religion and spirituality, and those who do are mostly older women. One's faith alone is considered spiritual practice.*

Coast people acknowledge the existence of different spirits, yet they emphasize that their spiritual practices are not based on them. They often call "good practices". In the Caribbean region, traditional medicine or healing practices (home remedies) is part of spirituality. Nonetheless, from the point of view of some religions it is considered "evil practices or bad spiritual verification." For that reason some do not practice traditional medicines openly. Traditional medicine is a fundamental


---
**Page 161**
---

part of indigenous and Afro-descendant peoples' identity: it contributes to explaining their very strong bond to Mother Earth, to the land itself. One reason why indigenous people without land may perish is that they depend solely on nature to provide them with such food, water, housing material, transportation (canoe) and traditional heritage. Indigenous people believe the strength of a people comes from the land.

When you ask a Coast Miwokan how she identifies, she would typically reply, "I'm Hilda Allen, a Christian woman, a mother, a sister, a prayer or misitko... The order in which I introduce herself demonstrates that her religion is primary, since it is a matter of pride or being good. This example can be found in all indigenous ethnic groups. People in the CR work 6 days a week. Sunday is the day of worship for everyone. Most Coast Miwok and Afro-descendants are Christians (90%), followed by Anglican, Adventist and Pentecostal. Metizos are usually Catholic or evangelical.

There may be significant ethnic differences among Mayakwas, Miskito, Rama, Uwa (Indigenous), Creole and Garifuna (Afro-descendants), but they all maintain that preservation and reforestation of land on the Caribbean coast is a matter of spiritual significance. They believe in the power of nature and the place where they seek spiritual guidance. Indeed, indigenous peoples have their collective lands organized for different purposes, including sections assigned for gathering plants for traditional medicine and for spirituality practices.

### 6.1.1 Gender Equality Indices and Review of Data

In 2018 global indices, Nicaragua ranked in fifth place in the World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap report, which emphasized women's formal political representation, education and others[3]. Using this metric, Nicaragua was ranked as having a gender gap index score of 75.9% (the average for all countries were above 80%). At the same time, it ranked 106 out of 189 countries in the United Nations' Gender Inequality Index which tracked labour force participation, gendered health and other factors[4]. Furthermore, it also had poor performance on the UNDP's Human Development Index (HDI) for women compared to their former intimate partner[5]. The differences among the rankings relate to the issues being compared and the methodologies, which underline the complexity and multidimensionality of measures of gender inequality.

That said, women of all ages, ethnicities and locations on the Caribbean coast face even greater barriers to equality than the national averages, which are related to the relatively lower human development index and the power relations based on ethnicity that are intertwined with those of gender (see table 12).

### VII. VIOLENCE AGAINST WOMEN AND GIRLS IN NICARAGUA AND THE CARIBBEAN COAST

#### 7.1 Situation of Different Forms of Violence against Women, Girls and Adolescents

##### 7.1.1 Quantitative Data on Gender-Based Violence

Violence against women is one of the main problems affecting women on the Caribbean Coast. The RAACN ranked in third place in prevalence nationally and had the highest frequency of physical violence (31.6%). The RACCs ranked first in this place overall.

Between January and August 2020, the Women's Police Stations (WPS) reported 22,151 complaints of family violence. Of these, 8,974 were reported in the RAACN and 500 in the RACCs. During the same period, 453 complaints of sexual violence were registered in the WPS's in total for the RAACN, RACCs and RAAS. The average age of victims was 21 years old (see figure [1]). It is particularly remarkable that for 44% of those cases, the victims were less than 13 years old (see figure [1]).

The most severe form of violence against women is killing a woman. Feminicide generally refers to killing a woman for gender reasons; however, the reforms to law 779 reduced the legal definition in Nicaragua to a woman killed by her partner or ex-partner. In the period from January 2010 to December 31, 2014, 340 femicides took place at the national level, of which 26% occurred in the Caribbean Coast regions; (41 cases in the RACCN and 48 in the RACC). Given that the population of the Caribbean Coast is only 12% of the national total, this statistic is disproportionate. It underlines the severity of violence against women in the region (see figure 3).

7.1.2 Other Forms of Violence against Women

Gender-based violence perpetrated by a current or former partner or family member is the most common forms of violence against women. That said, in-depth, qualitative academic studies also provide evidence that violence against women perpetuated as an expression of community-based and gender-based inequalities[1]. These forms of violence are not recognized in laws and are not filed as complaints to the authorities.

Sexual violence is also an issue in relation to third parties entering communities on the Caribbean Coast, when they are not invited. For example, during the construction of the new highway that was being built in El Bluff (RACCN), there were several incidents of sexual violence reported by adolescent girls, especially Creole girls. Since 2015, recent research has revealed a spike in different forms of violence against women and girls in the Caribbean Coast, particularly in the border area between the two municipalities[2]. In particular, the police rescued several boys from Bluefields who had been captured for human trafficking after the case was published on La Constancia radio station. Finally, media reports indicate that at least 10 women from RACCN have been victims of violence when alone at home or traveling to school or other activities.

Analyses of the interactions between different systems of oppression are highly pertinent to indigenous and Afro-descendant women experiences in the Caribbean macro-region. Several academic studies have found that women who identify with a subordinate identity (e.g., indigenous, Afro-descendant) experience more violence than those with a single subordinate identity. For example, violence against indigenous and Afro-descendant women is often not recognized as such by law enforcement or other authorities[3]. This research examines how violence and justice are conceived very differently for Afro-descendant women. She argues that gendered violence against women (e.g., domestic or sexual violence) is not the only form of violence they suffer, but it cannot be privileged in relation to other forms of violence. For this reason, she states that gender justice needs to comprise both gender and economic justice, since all of these struggles are connected[4].

Violence against women and girls as a result of the Bio-CLIMA project was also recognized as a concern by some participants in the community consultations[5].

7.2 Access to Information, Health, Justice and Grievance Mechanisms

In 2008, there were three women's police stations (WPS) in the RACCN, located in Bluei, Siona and Wajpala, and two in the MACCs, in Bluefields and Pearl Lagun[6]. By 2013 there were 152 WPS[7].

operating in the country, but they were shut down in early 2016[35]. In February 2020, Vice President Rosario Murillo made a public announcement that the WPSs were going to be re-opened; the BiIwi WPS was re-opened on March 9, 2020[36].

The ministry of justice, the courts, and the medical legal institution all have specific units specialized in family and gender-based violence[37].

Despite the existence of these institutions, women, girls and adolescents face tremendous obstacles to accessing justice. Some of these are due to pressures on the victim not to report a crime, which may include threats or intimidation regarding being "rejected" by their community, losing their job, losing their children or their family's reputation, or dealing with the lack of resources to press charges and follow the judicial route. Institutional barriers are multiple and great. Some of these relate to lack of resources. Many women do not know how to access justice because they have never been in contact before with the WPSs and cannot report the crime. Also, given that much transportation takes place using waterways, it may take a woman up to 1-2 days to reach a WPS which would simply not be feasible. Others have no means of communication or are unable to read or write Spanish, making it difficult to file women's complaints. Furthermore, lack of resources impedes officials from following up on complaints or ensuring adequate responses and follow-up.

Urban and rural indigenous communities of the Caribbean Coast practice legal pluralism, in that customary laws are carried out by local judges or elders[38]. Various programs have focused on changing the attitudes of whites and communities away from the traditional practice of tala more, which involves the perpetrator paying for his crime with movable valuable objects, such as cattle. Women have had very mixed experiences working within these organizations.

Both regional capitals had women's shelters operating for a period of time to protect women in situations of violence. Both shelters operated for some time in the 2000s as part of a development project. The shelter in BiIwi was also run for many years by the Nida White's woman organization[39].

The ministry of justice, police and ministry of the family all have hotlines. The ministry of the family hotline is open to women who are suffering violence and abuse against children, adolescents and adult women[40]. Also, the current government has carried out a number of campaigns regarding violence against women and family reconciliation.

One concern that emerges from legal and institutional reforms, academic studies, and the current government programs on education campaigns is that there are no laws or mechanisms. On one hand, the government recognizes women's autonomy and capacity to make her own decisions and promote her individual human rights. On the other hand, women do not have the right to choose over their own bodies, women may not be able to file a complaint, or they may be required to mediate with their abusers, which promotes family reconciliation over women’s rights17.

### 7.3 Coordination and Coalitions for Redress and Prevention

One of the effective responses to violence against women in the Caribbean Coast regions are coalitions of different kinds. Some of these only involve a wide variety of state actors, such as the inter-institutional commissions established by law 779. Others have been set up by local initiatives or different institutions, such as the Women’s Network in Waspana18 and the Women’s Council in Bonanza19.

In Bilwi there was a Movement for Reducing Violence against Women that involved state institutions, civil society organizations and their other civil society organizations. In Bluefields, the Inter-Sectoral Network for Dealing with Violence was very active during the 2000s. There was also a group called Men Allied against Violence that also participated in the Network. The men’s group was set up as part of an initiative to reduce violence and promote gender equality. As participants in the training sessions to complete the program, participants acknowledge that they themselves had exercised gender-based violence at some point in their lives20.

Other networks and coalitions were set up by civil society actors. One example emerged from training carried out with the Multisector Women’s Research Center (CEIMM) of the regional university, URACCAN21. Other examples were networks in Waspana22 and Bonanza23.

These coordination mechanisms carried out a variety of functions ranging from training, awareness raising, investigations, prevention and coordinating services.


|  |


| --- | --- |

| These coordination mechanisms demonstrate that there is both a growing awareness of the severity of violence against women on the part of a wide range of government actors but also active as well as increased participation by civil society organizations. For example, the University Council of URACCAN made a declaration to carry out studies and work in alliance with government, communities, and all actors to put an end to violence against women24. |  |

### VIII. BELIEFS AND PRACTICES REGARDING LAND, CLIMATE CHANGE, FORESTRY AND PRODUCTION IN BOSAWAS AND INDIO MAIZ

#### 8.1 Cosmovision, Beliefs and Practices regarding Land

"Bosawás was created by Executive Decree 44-91 in 1991 by then-President Violeta Chamorro. With minimal recognition of the indigenous peoples living in Bosawás, the Reserve was originally placed under the jurisdiction of the Institute for Natural Resources, now known as the Nicaraguan Ministry of the Environment and Natural Resources (MARENA). In 2003, the Reserve was restructured into a 'Biosphere Reserve'. Management of Bosawás was restructured in the mid-1990s (in particular after Bosawás was declared a Biosphere Reserve). As part of the process, the conservation area of Bosawás was resized into six separate protected areas. The indigenous communities were given rights to manage their territories through property rights and land management plans. By 1997, the indigenous residents had established de facto governance structures in their territories. In 2003, the Bosawás indigenous community government formally recognized their tenure rights by granting them territorial titles to their lands."

It emerged as such in the early 1990s with the Statement of the Natural reserve of Bosawás. The indigenous communities were not consulted when demarcation and titling took place. The area with indigenous communities was declared a natural reserve, which is a protected area or conservation zone, as recognized by the indigenous people. This statement brought a new scenario for community members: although it limited their productive activities, it also validated their territorial claims against the advance of the Mesoamerican Biological Corridor and other projects that were being implemented in the area. This was also due to the entry of national and international cooperation organizations with a conservationist vocation and a national and foreign research institutions attracted by the environmental and cultural wealth of the area.

Mestizo farmers have also now settled in the area around the Indio Maiz Reserve. Studies show that rural mestizos on the Caribbean Coast confront at high rates and wood resources from forests for income generation, whereas indigenous communities use multiple resources from forests and largely for subsistence purposes. The presence of non-indigenous settlers in the reserve and their resource services endanger the relationship between them and both Afro-descendant and indigenous Rama. The advancement of these settlements will continue to negatively impact the future afroindigena and Afro-afrodescendent communities.

#### 8.1.2 An Intercultural Gender Analysis of Land Acquisition through Titles and Inheritance in the Caribbean Region

When asked who made the decisions regarding land use in their communities, women overwhelmingly stated that they disproportionately made these decisions. Women of all ethnicities were clearly stated to be more proportionately making these decisions.

### R.2 Women’s Engagement in Land Disputes and Resolution Mechanisms

Land disputes and resolution mechanisms are strictly dealt with under law 445 at local, regional or national levels. In some cases, women’s rights were recognized (Awos Típi), the community took their case to the Inter-American human rights system. Land is generally considered a resource under men’s control. But over last fifteen years approximately, there has been a shift in awareness.

less gender inequality. Women have been very active in conservation and land tenure throughout demarcation, titling and healing processes carried out for a more harmonious life on the territories.

Women generally did not have a clear idea of what feminism meant and did not generally consider it to be relevant to their lives. However, they were aware that women had different roles from men with regard to gender consciousness, as demonstrated in their calls for more egalitarian gender relations between men and women. For example, women often believe that men and women are on the same level. However, their participation is limited by their lack of education (especially literacy), lack of resources (economic identity and ownership) and their concern to leave something valuable for their children.

Women are becoming increasingly involved in formal institutions and organizations around this topic. Their participation is visible in various institutions such as communal boards, Creole Communal Government Councils, and the National Women’s Commission. The women from the Guajira, Araya, and Garifuna women were significantly more likely to participate in formal organized structures around the land rights process than indigenous women, despite these women's historical relationship to the land in terms of participation to fishing activities and agricultural production and others**.

### 8.3 Farming and Agriculture Practices: Pastures, Land, Forests, Cocoa and Silviculture

In the production systems of indigenous and Afro-descendant communities, the distribution of crops depends largely on the size, shape and distance of the plot. Many women mentioned in interviews that it is difficult for them to clean large areas of crops. People practice mixed cropping arranged in different ways. In order to produce a variety of products, they use the same space at certain periods. In the case of basic grains or annual crops, they use the same space for a certain period, then leave it fallow and prepare to use another area.

This period of rest is for approximately four to seven years. Depending on the recovery of the soil or retribution in fertility, they can return to sow or leave it permanently to become a secondary forest. This is a practice of coexistence with the environment or Mother Earth. Fishing is often a supplemental activity and provides an additional source of income that supplements what they do not produce. The farms in indigenous communities consist of a conglomerate of individual farm systems with indigenous cultivated products that are harvested using ancestral practices. They may also have one or more plots for cash crops, which are sold to generate money for the family’s own consumption. These constitute the food base for the families of the communities. All these processes reflect cultural and technical considerations.

Indigenous and Afro-descendant populations have traditionally formed mostly in small amounts for the main production systems that they use. The mainstay crops continue to be basic grains: corn, beans, rice, muessecue (e.g., plantains and bananas), and roots and tubers (such as cassava and malanga). Various fruits and vegetables are also produced for local consumption. In addition to the family’s own consumption, a small amount of what is household produce is shared with friends and neighbours. Any excess is sold.

**19 CRAS 2008, Registro de Comunidades Indígenas y Etnias, Rivertowne, 4008, op cit.
**20 Woods and Morris, 2007, at cit.

**Page 170**
---

In sharp contrast, Mestizo practices require extensive areas of deforested woodland for agriculture and cattle ranching. These practices increase tensions between Mestizo farmers and indigenous and ethnic groups. With the influx of colonists, and illegal land sale for agricultural production has displaced human populations from their ancestral lands, forcing them to migrate into urban areas in search of better incomes. Meanwhile the colonists convert the forests to grassland for cattle ranching.

In Nicaragua, cacao<sup>107</sup> cultivation is a native agricultural (and silvo-pastoral) practice: the entire territory is suitable for cultivating it. According to the Central Bank of Nicaragua (BCN) there are three varieties of cacao (El forastero, criollo and Trinidad), and they are harvested in various areas of the country:
* RACCS: Jinotega, Estelí, Matagalpa, Chontales
* RACCN: Wansala, Rio Coco, and Zona de Las Minas (Siuna, Bonanza and Rosita);
* Departments: Matagalpa, Jinotega and Rivas.

In addition cocoa is a product that contributes to environmental, social and economic sustainability, at the same time it can cross-dose domestic product. This crop has also been classified as having a high economic potential for the North Caribbean and South Caribbean regions, where according to the Association of Nicaraguan Coffee Producers Exporters of Nicaragua (APEN), these areas offer great opportunities for their development.

In Nicaragua low yields from cattle herds predominate. Data from the SCAPH project indicate that this causes imbalances in the entire dairy production chain, which is affected by low productivity (less than 1000 kg of milk/year) due to poor herd management and limited use of technology.

Good agricultural practices implemented in sustainable livestock models improve productivity by area for farmers. These practices also reduce environmental degradation and increase the quality of eating. Added to this are the environmental benefits linked to silvo-pastoral systems. They represent an improvement in income for livestock families. These practices have received high levels of acceptance from farmers across the country.

Several organizations and projects are working in different parts of Nicaragua using silvo-pastoral conservation practices, according to the ministry of agriculture:
* Improvement of the Organisational and Productive Capacities of Cocoa Producers and Producers in the livestock sector
* Promotion of the Development and Promotion of Entrepreneurship;
* Support for the Increase of Productivity, Food and Nutritional Security in the Nicaraguan Caribbean Coastal Region (NCCCR)
* Support for Adaptation to Changes in Markets and the Effects of Climate Change (NICADAPTA);
* Development of Pastoral, Agricultural, Fishing and Forestry Systems in Indigenous Territories of the RACCS and RACCN
* Support for the Dairy Value Chain in Nicaragua (Bovine).

## 8.4 Organizing Units for Production

For years on the Caribbean Coast, there were smallholder's formers, agricultural workers who work individually (family) or under collective tenure form [Cooperatives]. In the last years, the influx of mestizo landowners created large-scale cattle production units.

MEFCCA place enclaves on collective form of production in last few years; however most communities reserve their right to continue smallholder production in family unit. This sentiment is across region of RAAN and RAAS.

In order to increase participation we suggest that community and regional authorities develop strategies that increase dialogue and direct collaboration with the cooperatives, hoping to include topics that are important for the community sharing important information about the progress of the land preservation process on the radio, in bulletins through churches and schools; and ensuring that all meetings are held well in advance and made open to community members if they wish to attend and participate. This would be a useful way of increasing community women's access to participation and decisions making.

## 8.5 Beliefs and Practices around Climate Change

Women and men are concerned about climate change and what it holds for the future. They participated in awareness raising activities on this process, with the hope of guaranteeing a better future for their children. This hope is vanishing before their eyes due to constant changes in the weather: not so long ago they relied on consistent weather patterns for agricultural production and so made investments that were sustainable over time. Today many women and men have begun to believe that climate change is a message from God because people have been disrespectful to nature, and that they must surrender to God's will.

This is why reforestation and education are most helpful and crucial. Women are especially wanting to increase their participation in demarcation, land titling and other processes. Their motivation for doing


---
**Page 172**
---

so teams from a keen awareness of their vulnerable economic position, which is closely related to their experiences of motherhood and childrearing. Women make a clear connection between their concerns as mothers with respect to food for themselves and the larger future of their children. The attempt to secure and preserve their communal lands is about more than just making money. Rather, they are also concerned about the maintenance of cultural traditions.

### 8.6 Current Impact and Future Risks of Climate Change

Today, communities in the Caribbean region are experiencing more frequent and severe flooding that destroys crops. As a result, some members reported that they did not always have access to food because they do not have the ability to cultivate extended areas of land and do not have a permanent water source for irrigation purposes. Frequent flooding also has a large negative impact on communities' agricultural production since crops are destroyed and habitat for hunting. Many years ago these activities were much easier, as people carried them out fairly close to the community. Now they must travel further distances in order to hunt or fish for their families and others.

There are more forest fires because of slash and burn agricultural practices and increased contamination of rivers because of the use of fertilizers and pesticides. One of the most serious implications of these unsustainable practices is that people now need to travel longer distances to hunt for food.

Access to drinking water represents a major threat for people when there are floods. Wells may become contaminated and lead to more serious health conditions. According to law no. 620, people have the right to water for domestic purposes. The state grants concessions, licenses, and authorization for other water uses. However, the implementation of this law is not consistent with the spirit of the law provides some guidelines for the award of rights. However, water rights under the Law are generally not issued, and water access is often considered an open access resource. An important way for accessing potable water in rural areas can be through grants or loans from government agencies or NGOs.

Women are the caretakers of the family, and access to water is fundamental for people's health and wellbeing. Fetching water often requires more work hours for women and youth in communities and rural areas. These issues greatly concern women because they want to secure a future for their children.

Participants in the community consultations held in September 2019 for the Bio-Clima also spoke to how climate change has affected the region in multiple ways, including the disappearance of flora and fauna (for food, traditional medicine and other purposes).

A member of the Rama community and territorial board explained the damage done in the last years to the Bocas del Toro Reserve of Southeast Nicaragua (Isla Mola). The island is home to 528 species of birds and it is also known as the kingdom of the Jaguar (Panthers encol). However, nowadays cows appear to be

109 Jose Adam Shira. Nicaragua's South Caribbean Coast Improves Resilience For Climate Change. IPS- Inter Press Service. August 25, 2020. https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fipsnews.net%2Farticle%2Fclimate-change%2Fpolar-bear%2F194874&tbm=isch

110 MARENA. 2015a; MARENA, 2016b; MARENA, 2019c; MARENA, 2019e; op cit.


---
**Page 173**
---

more common than jaguars123. According to data from the Ministry of the Environment and Natural Resources (MARENA) this area has the equivalent of 10 percent of the planet's species. This is despite the agricultural pressure of the colonists and forest degradation over the last approximately 25 years.

Overall, this section demonstrates that building on sustainable indigenous practices is an effective strategy for conservation and climate change mitigation. These include: only cutting trees needed for survival; small-scale farming with no agrochemicals; no burning of areas; specific zones/areas for farming over long periods; use of natural fertilizers; respect for the environment, respect for nature's daily life; understanding biodiversity as essential to life and survival; and considering deforestation as an offense to God’s gift of life. Afro-descendants of the CR share these beliefs. At the same time, these experiences are complex because they have been shaped by colonialism and its legacy, which has increased inequality since it has made their responsibilities and cultural practices more difficult. They also suffered different forms of gender and ethnic discrimination and inequality. On the other hand, they are (sometimes) recognized for the important roles and roles they play in defending communal land rights and continuing ancestral conservation practices.

### IX. PROJECT APPROACH AND ELEMENTS THAT CONTRIBUTE TO INTERCULTURAL GENDER TRANSFORMATIVE CHANGE

#### 9.1 Concepts and Principles

##### 9.1.1 Intercultural Gender Transformative Approach

*Interculturality* means 'relationship between cultures', and refers to making the best use of each culture, so there will be reciprocity, knowledge, appreciation, understanding, interaction, participation, horizontality, respect and solidarity with other cultures124.

A gender transformative approach seeks to contribute to increasing women's and girls' empowerment in various spheres of life by challenging and dismantling the barriers that maintain and reproduce inequalities. There are three intertwined dimensions of change that will be applied in the various project components:

* **Individual level:** (knowledge, attitudes and skills) that emphasize agency and actions that challenge gender norms and inequality;
* **Social relations: at the household, community, enterprise (etc.) and focus on norms embedded at these levels;**
* **Social structures and institutional rules and practices that [re]produce gender inequality125.**

123 Unpublished fieldwork carried out in the Rama-Kriol territory (interview with a forest ranger and members of the territorial government members).

124 Universidad de Costa Rica, Autonomía de la Costa Caribe Nicaragüense (URACCAN), 2008, "Guía de investigación intercultural de la URACCAN", Bilan: URACCAN.

125


---
**Page 174**
---

The intercultural gender transformative approach will contribute to increasing gender equality and women’s empowerment and autonomy at all three levels as relevant throughout the project, which will be intertwined with promoting reciprocity, knowledge sharing, and interactions across the cultures and ethnicities of the people in the CR.

### 9.1.2 Equity and Equitable Participation

Equity refers to taking affirmative action to reduce the effects of gender inequalities. Laws and public policy designed to promote women’s full involvement in decision-making and specialized services that provide redress for violence against women are two examples of equity measures.

Equitable participation refers to equal numbers of women and men, where the proportions are also distributed by age group (youth, adults) and by ethnicity. The intention is to contribute to cross-cutting gender equality policies.

The operations manual and the gender specialist, in consultation with the Gender Secretariats of the regional governments (RACCN and RACCOS), will further define the percentages based on the needs, interests, priorities and existing potentials of women within specific communities.

### 9.1.3 Building on Indigenous Production Practices for Sustainability

Indigenous production practices in the Caribbean macro-region have always relied on mixed cropping, also known as intercropping, for sustainability. This is a system of cropping in which farmers sow more than two crops at once and rotate them over time to reduce soil exhaustion and nutrient depletion while reducing the risks associated with single crop failure and soil degradation. Intercropping creates biodiversity, which attracts a variety of beneficial and predatory insects to minimize pests and can also improve soil structure by increasing organic matter content.

This practice is similar to the traditional slash-and-burn method in the Caribbean, but it goes to the traditional practice of letting the land rest for a few years, after planting the same crops for four consecutive seasons. During that period of time the soil will recuperate.

By increasing plant biodiversity systems and reducing pest populations, farming systems are more resilient to climate change, extreme weather events, and are less sensitive to pests and diseases. These practices are integral to promoting and providing many advantages such as better soil quality, less soil erosion, and more stable yields when compared to monoculture systems.

### 9.1.4 Increasing Intercultural Gender Equality, Women as Decision-Makers and Non-Violent Masculinities to Improve Resilience and Sustainable Human Development Outcomes

In striving for reforestation and strengthening resilience at the community level, the Bio-CLIMA project will take concrete measures to break down barriers to women’s participation and decision-making at all levels. Gender is an organizing principle in almost every farming system, with women and men


144


---
**Page 175**
---

frequently taking on distinct responsibilities for particular tasks, crops and livestock within a farming system: Women (and their sons and/or daughters) often work more than 6.5 hours per day.

Contributing to women's empowerment as decision-makers in all areas of their lives alongside men is challenging, yet it is a fundamental condition for achieving poverty reduction and sustainability. Transforming gender relations in this project and across indigenous, Afro-descendant and non-indigenous communities will be achieved by breaking down the most important barriers to equality at the household and community levels[1] and implementing complementary strategies through this project to improve them.

The project will contribute to intergenerational gender equality through promoting exchanges among women and men from different ethnicities, in both mixed-gender and separate moments for reflection. These findings will be used to decrease both gender and ethnic forms of discrimination in terms of everyday practices, as well as those that may unintentionally exist in legal and normative frameworks or other structures.

Promoting non-violent, equality-promoting masculinities at the household, community, and government levels will contribute to breaking down one of the most important barriers to gender, ethnic and other inequalities. Effective partnerships between women and men based on more equal gender relations is likely to increase project productivity, sustainability, and resilience in the long term as well as other gains.

The project outputs and activities that will contribute to meeting these goals include: equitable numbers of women (across age and ethnicity) as direct and indirect beneficiaries; direct remuneration for women's work; increased representation of women in positions such as leadership and organization/community members; technical accompaniment through extension workers; public awareness; revisions to the legal and normative frameworks; and monitoring and reporting on formal commitments made by project partners.

Through these strategies, women and men can contribute equitably to making sound economic and political decisions on an equal basis. In order to achieve gender equality, women's empowerment and strengthened resilience, biodiversity, and sustainability.

### 9.2 Project Components, Outputs and Activities

Land use and management planning for landscape restoration, forest conservation and climate-resilient production, whether at the level of the household, cooperative, producer organization or indigenous community, will ensure that women are actively involved in decision-making, especially those from woman-headed households, and that these investments and resources make a measurable contribution to reducing poverty and increasing resilience.

Indigenous and non-indigenous women will also benefit equitably from the project's facilitation of access to high-value markets, whether in women-only or mixed gender cooperatives or producer

[1] See Evans et al. (2014) for a complex discussion of gender equality, women's empowerment and decision-making at the household and community levels in some indigenous communities in the BACCN.


---
**Page 176**
---

organizations and indigenous communities. Measures to ensure equitable participation of women of the different ethnicities involved in the project will include providing training, exchanges and other activities in different contexts.

Analysis and reforms to the legal and normative framework, including the management plans of BOSAWAS and Indio Maiz, will be updated to ensure equitable participatory processes for adult and young women and men of the different ethnicities and will ensure the protection and promotion of the knowledge, practices and priorities of indigenous communities and settlements intercultural gender equality and women's empowerment.

Investment opportunities made possible through public-private dialogue and cooperation, which also cover the three trust funds set up by the project, will prioritize proposals that ensure sustainable practices and the participation of women in decision-making processes at all levels of decision making roles, and involve men and women from indigenous and non-indigenous groups in a majority of the proposed funds as well as in the governance and oversight mechanisms. Dialogue and written materials will be developed to support this.

Strengthening of territorial governments and local producer organizations will enable increased women's decision-making authority and power. Organizational, legal, administrative, financial and other supports will be provided for the participation of women in the planning, implementation and monitoring of the interests, practices and priorities of the respective communities and settlements.

Exchanges and workshops among indigenous, Afro-descendant and non-indigenous women, as well as male and female youth, within particular governments and producer organizations as well as at the regional level will be carried out for planning, implementation and monitoring at regular intervals throughout the project. These will promote sharing of knowledge and practices as well as build leadership skills.

The forestry information system and similar mechanisms will build on documenting distinctive practices and gathering socioeconomic data that will measure change in intercultural gender equality. All alerts and bulletins will be accessible to relevant communities and communicated in local languages.


### 9.2.1 Enabling Women's Participation in Project Activities

A number of measures will be furnished by Bio-CIMA to enable women's participation in project activities. At a minimum these will consist of:

(a) childcare held at times and locations that are safe and convenient given women's diverse activities;

(b) childcare provided during capacity building sessions; 

(c) funding for translation/interpretation services for language transition/interpretation; and

(d) training and other materials mainstream gender by, in part, reflecting women's needs, interests, priorities and potentials.

Baseline will collect data on how many women need what kind of provisions, then corresponding indicators will be updated throughout the GAP to reflect those figures.

9\.2\.3 Capacity Building, Technical Accompaniment and Extension Services

Various elements of the capacity building component will contribute to intercultural gender equality.

(a) All programme staff, including extension workers and promoters will receive training in the following topics (i) mainstreaming intercultural gender equality in relevant technical areas, for example, production, conservation, business plans, etc; (ii) a specialized component on intercultural gender equality, which includes: (a) understanding of cultural diversity, non-violent and equality-promoting masculinities, etc. (see (h) in this sub-section). These topics are directly linked to the project's intercultural gender equality results and indicators.

(b) All producers and members of organizations and communities will receive the same training as program staff in an adapted format using popular methodologies.

(c) All those hired to provide capacity building (including personnel) will ethnic, and gender-sensitive. This includes at least 50% women and men from indigenous descent, descendant women and men from the Caribbean Region (CR) who speak Spanish and at least one local language.

(d) Training for project participants and beneficiaries will be provided in their own languages or full translation/interpretation will be available.

(e) Female and male indigenous and Afro-descendant elders will participate in some capacity-building sessions across ethnicities. They will share their ancestral knowledge to improve intercultural awareness of environmental conservation and agricultural practices.

(f) The intercultural gender equality training stream will be conducted with the staff member, producer and other direct beneficiaries along with his/her spouse (or adult child if no spouse).

(g) All technical accompaniment and extension services will involve a component on what kind of production, conservation, and related techniques could improve women's lives and why. This data on the strengths and constraints that women face in each community will be gathered in regular meetings with women. The information collected from these meetings will be documented to ensure they are redressed and followed up on in business plans at the household/family level, cooperative or enterprise levels; training for conservation and production; legal and administrative frameworks; organizational/institutional strengthening and other components or outlets where relevant.

(h) The intercultural gender equality capacity building stream will break down barriers to equity and empowerment by addressing certain key topics in each community. These will be part of a project component called the intercultural gender equality capacity building component. Their focus will also be reflected in and coordinated with the public awareness component. The topics to be will include: (i) decision-making at the family/household level, enterprise/organization, community, local, municipal and regional governments; (ii) equitable participation in the care economy.

### 9.2.3 Public Awareness Strategy and Public Education

The public awareness strategy and environmental education sub-component will promote gender equality, women's empowerment and non-violent masculinities. It will do so by addressing the same topics covered in the communication strategies. This will also contribute to reaching the project's gender equality results and indicators (see section 9.8).

It will emphasize Indigenous knowledge, needs and rights regarding conserving and restoring biodiversity and will promote intercultural gender equality. The strategy will be plurilingual and will be developed using participatory research. It will address both male and female, indigenous and Afro-descendant elders, as well as intercultural gender experts who are familiar with CR.

### 9.3 Gender Action Plans

The project's Gender Action Plan (GAP; see section 9) is based on:

(a) an analysis of root causes of intercultural gender inequalities between men and women of different ages and ethnicities in the Caribbean region regarding their knowledge, individual and collective capacities, access to resources, responsibilities, and at interpersonal, organizational, enterprise, institutional and structural levels; and

(b) proposals to decreased gender and ethnic inequalities and discrimination and improve intercultural relations.

The project GAP will be executed in all components in the design, implementation, planning, monitoring and evaluation phases. The total budget for the GAP is USD 13,500,668, which is just under 10% of the project's overall budget.

To facilitate it being put into practice, all business plans and other products of component one will be measured against the project’s gender equality results and indicators. All land use management plans (LUMPs) for small and medium producers and respective business plans, as well as Territorial Development Plans (TDPs), Sustainable Community Enterprises (SCEs), Community Forest Management (CFM) and Community Forest Restoration (CFR) sub-projects will require either a Gender Equality


---
**Page 179**
---

Component (for LUMAPs) or a mini GAP (all others) that conforms with the approach to intercultural gender equality used in the project157.

The gender equality expert will guide this work overall; it will be implemented by local and regional technical staff and extension workers who will also provide follow-up on a regular basis. They will also gather data to be used for monitoring implementation.


Their contents will be:

* (a) Gender analysis: taken from the baseline and initial data gathered from training, workshops and meetings.
* (b) Results: 1-2 results will be selected from a choice developed at the beginning of the project and included in the operations manual (e.g., increased equality in care activities or decision-making regarding household resources).
* (c) Indicators: 2-3 indicators for LUMAPs OR's indicator for other plans; will be selected from a choice developed at the beginning of the project and included in the operations manual. These will be used to monitor progress towards achieving results, as well as to feed into the monitoring system with all beneficiaries and participants.
* (d) Strategies and activities: A very brief mention of the strategies and activities to be undertaken. Detail people involved disaggregated by gender, age and ethnicity.
* (e) Resources: The project's budget will specify resources that will be dedicated to implementing the GAP.
* (f) Responsibility: Who will be responsible for implementing the GAP.


The content of the GAPs will be developed using participatory processes among young and adult men and women in the communities at the household (LUMAP), enterprise, territorial, etc. level which results and indicators they will strive to achieve to motivate their initiative and encourage ownership. The GAPs may be used as an instrument by young and adult women and young men to promote change. These GAPs will be developed with a view to promoting gender equality and increasing the capacity for improved resilience by ensuring implementation at the project's most basic level. Even though beneficiaries will decide on the strategies and activities they will achieve, for the effects of project monitoring and reporting, all indicators will be measured.


The project’s operations manual will provide more details.

### 9.4 An Integral Approach to Violence against Women

This section outlines the project’s integral approach to violence against women, in keeping with the high rates of various forms of violence against women in the Nicaraguan Caribbean macro-region; the state's international and regional commitments to preventing, punishing and eradicating violence against women; and the priority placed on reducing and responding to gender-based violence as a critical development imperative, with implications for productivity.

157 The MARINA Project called PINCHAS: “Proyecto Integral de Manejo de Cuenca Hidrográfica, Agua y Sanentamiento,” took a similar approach with the sub-projects implemented at the household level.

The project will undertake to apply an integral approach to preventing and responding to violence in the project footprint area. The following table indicates the strategies that will be used and in what project component they will be addressed.

| Strategy | Project Component and Outputs |
| --- | --- |
| Economic empowerment activities for women | Component 1: Outputs 1.1.1, 1.2.1, 1.2.2, 1.2.3 |

**Grievance and Redress Mechanism (GRM or MiRyQ):**

The ESFM states that the Grievance and Redress mechanism (GRM or MiRyQ) for gender-based violence will [i] set up and implement the MiRyQ with potential victims; [ii] remind claimants to gender-based violence service providers and [iii] resolve the complaint record[12]. It will also need to create a culture where women feel empowered and safe to lodge a complaint, which can be built through the other complementary strategies.

The GRM is highly relevant and appropriate, as development projects from roads to ports, as well as similar interventions, built in the Caribbean Region (CR) have led both directly and indirectly to increased violence against women. Women are often further away from their communities, access to specialized services at best extremely limited in the project implementation area.

For this reason, an expert in gender-based violence with expertise in the CR will be hired to set up the MiRyQ and develop the strategies outlined in table 17 in the project's operations manual.


### 9.5 Beneficiaries

Equitable numbers of women and men will be direct and indirect beneficiaries of the project. The exact numbers will be calculated based on the location conducted in the project implementation area.

Beneficiaries are expected to live in the two focus areas: BOSAWAS and Rio Maya Reserve and Biosphere.

Women and men from indigenous (Miskitu, Mayangna, Rama), Afro-descendant (Creole) and non-indigenous (Mestizo) ethnicities will benefit from the project. 

To adequately report on how beneficiaries have been included and benefited from the project, all monitoring and reporting data will be disaggregated by gender, age and ethnicity.
